Astrya

Engineering Handbook

Shiv Vyas · Revision A

Astrya is an astrology app built on one rule: nothing about the chart is guessed. The Swiss Ephemeris computes a person's real birth chart (planets, houses, nakshatras, dashas, doshas, transits and numerology) from their birth date, time and place, and Claude interprets only those computed facts. It ships as a Next.js web app on Vercel and a native SwiftUI app for iOS, both on one API and one Supabase project. This handbook is the single reference for how it is built, why it is built that way, what keeps it fast and cheap, and what it does when things go wrong. It is written to be studied: every chapter ends with a reading order through the code and questions to check yourself against.

Revision A 30 September 2026 Version 0.3.0 Next.js 15 · Supabase · iOS 17 Audience: contributors

Part one

Foundations

The shape of the system before any screen: how a birth chart is computed and never guessed, how a reading is written from it and what that costs, and how a person and a phone are let in.

  • 1. Architecture
  • 2. The chart engine
  • 3. Readings, prompts, and cost
  • 4. Identity and access

Chapter one

1Architecture

Astrya is built on one bet: the chart is computed, and only the reading is interpreted. The Swiss Ephemeris runs inside a Next.js route handler and produces every position, house, nakshatra, dasha and dosha as a number. Claude is handed those numbers and asked what they mean. Two clients, a web app and a native iOS app, call the same route handlers, share one Supabase project, and are told apart only by how they prove who they are.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It is the map for everything that follows: where the boundaries are, which side of each boundary a piece of code sits on, and why. The product was called Astra during development, and the repository, the bundle identifier com.shivvyas.astra and the production host still carry that name. Where the code and this handbook disagree, the code is right and this handbook is a bug.

1.1System context

There is one server, and it is the Next.js app deployed on Vercel. A web browser reaches it as pages and as /api/* route handlers; the iOS app reaches only the route handlers. The server talks to four outside parties: the Anthropic API for readings, Supabase for identity and every table, Apple Push Notification service for alerts, and Open-Meteo for birthplace geocoding. The Swiss Ephemeris is not an outside party. It is a WebAssembly module, loaded once per server process, that runs in the same function as the route handler that called it. Vercel Cron is the fifth caller, and it calls the same server twice a day.

Each party, how it is reached, and what proves the request is allowed:

PartyReached fromWithWhere in the code
Anthropic APIRoute handlers and cron onlyANTHROPIC_API_KEYlib/anthropic.ts
Swiss EphemerisIn-process, lazily initialised once per server processNothing; it is a WASM module in node_moduleslib/astrology/chart.ts
Supabase PostgresServer as the caller; iOS app directly over PostgRESTAnon key plus the user's JWT, or the service-role keylib/supabase/, mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Supabase.swift
Supabase AuthBrowser via server actions; iOS app directlyAnon keyapp/(auth)/actions.ts, Features/Auth/AuthStore.swift
Supabase StorageServer, admin client, avatars bucketService-role keyapp/api/profile/route.ts, app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts
APNsCron, over HTTP/2A provider token signed with the .p8 keylib/push/apns.ts
Open-MeteoGET /api/geocode, which is publicNothinglib/geo.ts
Vercel CronCalls the serverAuthorization: Bearer $CRON_SECRETvercel.json, app/api/cron/daily/route.ts

1.2The one bet

The README states it in a sentence: "Nothing about the chart is guessed." Every other decision in the system follows from taking that literally. A language model asked for a birth chart will produce one, and it will be plausible and wrong. So the model is never asked. The chart is computed by computeChart in lib/astrology/chart.ts, which converts the local birth time to Universal Time with luxon, asks the ephemeris for a Julian day, and reads eight bodies with calc_ut. Ketu is Rahu plus 180 degrees. For the Vedic chart the ephemeris is put into sidereal mode with the Lahiri ayanamsa and houses are whole-sign from the ascendant; for the Western chart it stays tropical and houses are Placidus. Vimshottari dasha periods come from the Moon's sidereal longitude. All of this is deterministic and unit tested. 

// lib/astrology/chart.ts
// Lazily initialize the WASM module once per server process.
let swePromise: Promise<InstanceType<typeof SwissEph>> | null = null;
async function getSwe(): Promise<InstanceType<typeof SwissEph>> {
  if (!swePromise) {
    swePromise = (async () => {
      const swe = new SwissEph();
      await swe.initSwissEph();
      return swe;
    })();
  }
  return swePromise;
}

The result is stored, not recomputed per request. saveBirthProfile in lib/data/birthProfile.ts computes both traditions and writes them as one jsonb value, { vedic, western }, into birth_profiles.chart. Every reader of that column goes through ensureCurrentChart, which checks schemaVersion against CHART_SCHEMA_VERSION (currently 2) and recomputes a stale chart on read. There is no migration job for charts: a chart written under an older house system corrects itself the first time its owner opens the app, and both traditions are rewritten together so a profile is never half upgraded.

Only then does the model appear. app/api/chat/route.ts loads the stored chart, derives facts from it (lords, aspects, dignity, combustion, from lib/astrology/derived.ts), computes today's transits, and builds a system prompt in lib/astrology/prompt.ts that is entirely made of computed values. Claude reads that prompt and writes prose. The models are named in one place: 

// lib/anthropic.ts
export const READING_MODEL = "claude-sonnet-5";
export const DEEP_READING_MODEL = "claude-opus-4-8";

export const LOW_EFFORT: { readonly output_config: { readonly effort: "low" } } = {
  output_config: { effort: "low" },
};

The comment above LOW_EFFORT records a measurement on one Vedic reading of the same chart and question: 1,452 output tokens at default effort, 719 at low, 328 with thinking off, and all three cited the same real placements. Routine readings therefore run at low effort; a deep reading keeps the default and upgrades to the larger model. The point for this chapter is the direction of dependency. The prompt depends on the chart. The chart never depends on the prompt.

What the bet rules out No route ever sends a birth date to the model and asks for positions. No client ever computes a chart; the iOS app decodes the stored jsonb into NatalChart and draws it. When the phone answers a question without the network, it answers by looking a value up in that decoded chart, never by reasoning about the sky. See 1.7.

1.3Module map

Three trees hold the code: lib/ for everything the server does, app/ for the Next.js routes that expose it, and mobile/ for the iOS app. tsconfig.json excludes mobile, so the TypeScript and the Swift never see each other; the phone reaches the server only over HTTPS. 

lib/
  astrology/      chart, dasha, derived, doshas, transits, numerology, prompt, constants, types
  timeline/       build, describe, explain, extract, load
  alerts/         run, predictions, compose, slots
  data/           birthProfile, chat, history
  supabase/       client, server, middleware, route, admin
  pdf/            kundli
  push/           apns
  admin/          guard, data
  anthropic.ts    model names, effort, the client
  env.ts          server-only environment access
  geo.ts          Open-Meteo geocoding and tz-lookup
app/
  page.tsx        landing page, redirects a signed-in user to /app
  (auth)/         login, signup, check-email, and the server actions
  (app)/app/      home, intake, chat, profile
  admin/          isolated admin area with its own login
  auth/callback/  Supabase code exchange
  api/            twelve route handlers, listed in 1.4
mobile/
  project.yml                 XcodeGen spec: three targets
  scripts/make-config.sh      writes the generated AppConfig.swift
  Sanchara/Sources/
    SancharaApp.swift, RootView.swift
    Design/                   Theme, Components, ReadingCard
    Features/                 Auth, Intake, Chat, Kundli, Timeline, Alerts, Profile
    Intents/                  App Intents, entities, shortcuts, deep links
    Services/                 SancharaAPI, Supabase, Push, ChartCache, WidgetRefresh
  SancharaWidgets/            ReadingWidget, PeriodWidget, ReadingControl

Nothing enforces the import rules between lib/ folders except the imports themselves, so the rules below were read off the code rather than off a manifest. They hold today, and the diagram is the shape they make.

ModuleOwnsImports from libRuns where
astrology/The engine: chart, dasha, derived facts, doshas, transits, numerology, the prompt builders, constants and typesOnly itself; externally luxon and swisseph-wasmServer. Pure functions apart from the WASM call in chart.ts
timeline/The dasha life map, the plain-language meaning per period, extraction of moments from chat historyastrology, anthropic, supabase/routeServer. explain, extract, load are server-only; build and describe are pure
alerts/The twice-daily reading, the dosha sweep, the copy the model writes, the local-time slot ruleastrology, anthropic, supabase/admin, pushServer, cron only. slots.ts is pure
data/Reads and writes of birth_profiles, conversations and messages; the history window a reading seesastrology, supabase/server, supabase/routeServer
supabase/Five clients: browser, server (cookies), middleware, route (bearer or cookies), admin (service role)envclient.ts is "use client"; route.ts and admin.ts are server-only
pdf/The kundli PDF, drawn with pdf-libastrology/constants, astrology/typesServer, server-only
push/The APNs client: provider token, HTTP/2 sessions, dead-token detectionenvServer, server-only
admin/requireAdmin and the admin queriessupabase/server, supabase/adminServer, server-only
anthropic.tsModel names, the effort setting, one cached clientenvServer, server-only
env.tsEvery environment variable, as a function that throws when a required one is missingnothingServer, server-only
geo.tsOpen-Meteo search and resolveTimezonenothingBoth. IntakeForm calls resolveTimezone in the browser

The server-only package is the one mechanical guard. Fourteen files import it, and a client component that pulls any of them in fails the build rather than shipping a secret. Client components are few and shallow: Chat.tsx, AppShell.tsx, IntakeForm.tsx, PlaceAutocomplete.tsx, AdminShell.tsx and the marketing pieces. Between them they import exactly two things from lib: the ChatMode and GeoResult types, and the resolveTimezone function. Everything else a page needs is fetched by a server component or a server action.

The iOS side

mobile/project.yml declares three targets and is the whole rule book for the phone. The Xcode project it generates is ignored by git. The app target depends on the supabase-swift package and on the widget target. The widget target is the interesting one, because its source list is not a folder but six named files: 

# mobile/project.yml
SancharaWidgets:
  type: app-extension
  sources:
    - path: SancharaWidgets
    # Compiled into the extension as well as the app.
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/ReadingCard.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/BirthProfileDetails.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift

The comment on that list says each file is dependency-free on purpose, because pulling in anything that imports Supabase would drag the whole client into a process that has no session to use it with. Those six files import only SwiftUI, Foundation and AppIntents. That is the iOS equivalent of the server-only marker: the widget extension is a process with no session, so the only code that may compile into it is code that needs none. Everything under Features/ and Services/ that talks to Supabase or to the API stays in the app target. The test target holds fourteen XCTest files and depends on the app.

1.4Two clients, one API

The web app and the iOS app do not use the server the same way, and the difference is worth stating plainly because it decides where a feature's code lives.

The web app mostly does not call /api. Its pages are React server components that import lib/data directly and render with the result; its writes are server actions (authenticate, sendMagicLink, saveIntake, signOut) that a browser form posts to. The browser itself touches three routes: POST /api/chat from Chat.tsx, because a streaming reading needs a response body that grows; GET /api/kundli as a plain link, because it returns a PDF; and GET /api/geocode from PlaceAutocomplete.tsx as the user types a birthplace.

The iOS app has no server components and no server actions, so everything it cannot do itself goes through SancharaAPI in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift. Its own header comment says why the API exists from the phone's point of view: it "holds everything that cannot ship in a client binary: the Anthropic key, the Swiss Ephemeris, and PDF generation." What the phone can do itself, it does directly against Supabase with the anon key and the user's session: it reads birth_profiles, conversations, messages, alerts and daily_readings over PostgREST and marks alerts and readings as read, which the RLS policies in migrations 0004 and 0005 allow.

RouteMethodCalled byWhat it does
/api/chatPOSTBrowser, iOSStreams a reading as plain UTF-8 text; the conversation id travels in an x-conversation-id header
/api/kundliGETBrowser, iOSThe kundli PDF
/api/profilePOSTiOSBirth-detail intake; the web uses the saveIntake server action for the same work
/api/geocodeGETBrowser, iOSBirthplace search with the timezone attached; public, no token
/api/timelineGETiOSThe dasha life map with pinned moments and per-period meanings
/api/timeline/explainPOSTiOSWrites the meaning of every period in one model call; maxDuration 90
/api/timeline/scanPOSTiOSProposes moments from the user's own messages; writes nothing; maxDuration 120
/api/life-eventsPOST, DELETEiOSPins and unpins moments, up to 50 in one call
/api/devicesPOST, DELETEiOSAPNs token registration, upserted on the token
/api/accountDELETEiOSDeletes the calling user; App Store guideline 5.1.1(v)
/api/cron/dailyGETVercel CronReadings then the dosha sweep; maxDuration 300
/api/cron/alertsGETNot in vercel.jsonThe dosha sweep alone, behind the same secret; .env.example still names it

Every route except geocode and the two cron routes opens the same way: resolve a Supabase client for the caller, ask it for the user, and return 401 when there is none. The user id then comes from that verified session and never from the request body. Every route that runs the ephemeris or the model declares runtime = "nodejs", and next.config.ts lists swisseph-wasm in serverExternalPackages, keeping it external so that Next bundles the .wasm binary for the server runtime.

1.5Two auth channels

Both clients sign in with Supabase Auth, but they carry the session differently, and lib/supabase/ exists mainly to make that difference invisible to route handlers.

Web: cookies

The browser gets a session through a server action. Password sign-in, sign-up, the magic link and the confirmation resend all live in app/(auth)/actions.ts; the email links land on app/auth/callback/route.ts, which calls exchangeCodeForSession and redirects to /app. From then on the session is a set of cookies that @supabase/ssr manages, and two pieces of code keep them fresh. middleware.ts runs updateSession from lib/supabase/middleware.ts on every request that is not a static asset, a Next internal or the .well-known folder. That function calls auth.getUser(), which refreshes the cookies as a side effect, and then redirects an anonymous request for /app to /login and for /admin (other than /admin/login) to /admin/login.

lib/supabase/server.ts is the client server components and actions use. Its setAll is wrapped in a try block whose comment explains the design: a server component cannot set cookies, so the write is ignored there, and the middleware is what actually refreshes them. Reading the two files together is the fastest way to understand the web session.

iOS: a bearer token

The phone never sees a cookie. supabase-swift signs in directly, with a password, with a six-digit emailed code (signInWithOTP then verifyOTP), or with Sign in with Apple, where Supabase verifies Apple's identity token through signInWithIdToken and the nonce the app generated. The session persists in the Keychain, which Supa in Services/Supabase.swift notes is the library's default on Apple platforms. When the app calls the API it reads the current access token and puts it in an Authorization: Bearer header. 

// lib/supabase/route.ts
export async function createRouteSupabase(request: Request): Promise<Db> {
  const token = bearerTokenFrom(request);
  if (!token) return createServerSupabase();

  return createClient(env.supabaseUrl(), env.supabaseAnonKey(), {
    global: { headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } },
    auth: { persistSession: false, autoRefreshToken: false },
  });
}

This is the seam. A route handler calls createRouteSupabase(request) and gets back a Db, which is just SupabaseClient; the comment above the type notes that both the cookie client and the bearer client are instances of it, so every function in lib/data and lib/timeline accepts either. With a bearer header the client forwards the JWT on every PostgREST call and Row Level Security evaluates as that user. Without one it falls through to the cookie client and the web is unchanged. bearerTokenFrom is small enough to be fully specified by five tests in lib/supabase/route.test.ts: it accepts a lowercase scheme, and returns null for a missing header, a non-bearer scheme, or an empty token.

Why the anon key ships in the binary

mobile/scripts/make-config.sh reads exactly two values from .env.local, NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL and NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY, and writes them into a generated, git-ignored AppConfig.swift along with the API base URL. Its header states the rule: these are "designed to ship in clients (RLS is the security boundary, not secrecy)", and the service-role and Anthropic keys "are never read here and must never reach the app binary." The anon key is safe to publish because it grants nothing by itself. Every one of the eleven tables in supabase/migrations/ has enable row level security, and every policy is keyed on auth.uid(), so an anon-key request without a valid user JWT can read no rows, and one with a JWT can read only that user's rows. The same key is in every web page's JavaScript through lib/supabase/client.ts. Nothing about the phone is special. 

-- supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql
create policy "birth self" on public.birth_profiles
  for all using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

Admin: a third door, same key

The admin area at /admin has its own login page but not its own auth system. A user is an admin if a row for them exists in admins, a table with no insert policy and therefore no self-service path; requireAdmin in lib/admin/guard.ts checks it with the service-role client and redirects otherwise. Admin pages then read other users' transcripts with that same client, which bypasses RLS, and this is the only place a page does so.

1.6The privacy boundary

The boundary in the system-context diagram is a red dashed line between the clients and Vercel. In code it is lib/env.ts. The file begins with import "server-only", and every variable is a function, not a constant, so a missing required value throws at the moment it is used rather than at import.

VariableLeaves the server?Read by
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL, NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEYYes, by design: browser bundle and iOS binarylib/supabase/client.ts, make-config.sh, and the server clients
SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEYNeverlib/supabase/admin.ts only
ANTHROPIC_API_KEYNeverlib/anthropic.ts only
CRON_SECRETNever; Vercel sends it, the cron routes compare itapp/api/cron/*/route.ts
APNS_KEY_ID, APNS_TEAM_ID, APNS_BUNDLE_ID, APNS_PRIVATE_KEYNever; optional, and pushes are skipped when absentlib/push/apns.ts

The service-role client deserves its own rule, and lib/supabase/admin.ts states it in one line: "Bypasses RLS. Only ever import from server code behind an admin auth check." In practice it is imported from exactly the places that need to act as nobody in particular: the two cron modules, the admin queries, and three writes that RLS would correctly refuse. Avatar upload writes to the avatars bucket with it. Account deletion calls auth.admin.deleteUser with it, for the user id taken from the caller's own session. And device registration upserts device_tokens with it because, as the route's comment explains, a phone handed from one account to another has to change owner, and the owner-only policy would refuse that update. In each case the decision of who was made by the caller's own client first.

What crosses the boundary in the other direction is also bounded. The model receives a first name, the computed chart and derived facts, the numerology numbers, today's transits, the timeline description, and the conversation. The extraction route sends only the user's own messages, never the assistant's, and writes nothing until the user confirms; its comment calls filing an inferred death or divorce onto someone's timeline "a bad thing to be wrong about." life_events is the one table holding what a person said about their own life rather than what the ephemeris computed, and migration 0006 gives it no admin read policy for that reason.

What is not enforced The server-only marker is on fourteen files, but not on lib/supabase/server.ts or lib/data/*. They are safe today because they import next/headers or a server-only module and would fail in a client bundle anyway. A new file in lib/data that imported neither would not be caught. Add the marker when you add the file.

1.7What lives on the phone

Astrya is not a local-first app; the server holds the truth and the phone reads it. But one piece is deliberately local, and it exists because three processes on the phone need the chart and only one of them can sign in. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift opens with that argument: the app, the App Intents extension Siri runs, and the widget timeline provider all need the data, and neither of the last two can hold a Supabase session or wait on a network round trip. So the app writes and the others only read. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift
static let appGroup = "group.com.shivvyas.astra"

private func write<T: Encodable>(_ value: T, to name: String) {
    queue.async {
        guard let data = try? JSONEncoder().encode(value) else { return }
        try? data.write(to: self.url(name), options: [.atomic, .completeFileProtection])
    }
}

The cache is four JSON files in the App Group container, chart, details, reading and period, plus one short string in the App Group's UserDefaults suite, pendingDestination, which an intent or a Control Center button writes milliseconds before the app launches and the app drains once. Writes are atomic and use complete file protection; reads and writes go through one serial queue. If the container is missing, which happens in a simulator build without provisioning, the cache falls back to the caches directory and simply stops sharing with the widget.

FileWritten byRead by
chartProfileStore.loadChart after every successful fetch of birth_profiles.chartProfileStore.load on launch, the kundli, OnDeviceReasoner, the intents' entities, PeriodWidget as a fallback for dates
detailsProfileStore.loadIntentData.details(), for the intents
readingDailyStore and IntentData when a reading is fetchedReadingWidget, TodaysReadingIntent, while isFresh (today, or under 24 hours old)
periodTimelineStore after each timeline loadPeriodWidget
pendingDestinationOpenKundliIntent, DeepLinkDeepLink.drain on launch and every return to the foreground

Two consequences follow. First, the app launches offline. ProfileStore.load reads the cached chart and details before it fetches, and if the fetch fails but both are present it sets the state to ready anyway; only a device that has never loaded a chart sees the failure screen. The chart is about 4 KB and changes only when birth details are re-saved, so a stale cache is almost never wrong. Second, the widgets never fetch. ReadingWidget asks to be rebuilt at the next six o'clock, morning or evening, because that is when a new reading can exist; PeriodWidget asks for midnight, because a mahadasha changes about once a year but the progress bar should move by a day. WidgetRefresh nudges WidgetKit when the app itself has just received a new reading or a new period meaning, and only then, because reloads are rate-limited.

The cache holds nothing secret, no token and no session, but a birth chart is personal, so AuthStore.signOut unregisters the push token, signs out, and then calls ChartCache.shared.clear(). The comment there says why: the files would otherwise outlive the session, and the widget would go on showing the previous person's chart to whoever signs in next.

On-device answers

The cached chart is also what makes OnDeviceReasoner possible. Questions that are lookups, where a planet sits, what is in the seventh house, which dasha is running, are routed to Apple's on-device model with the chart exposed as a tool, and only interpretive questions escalate to /api/chat. That is the bet from 1.2 applied on the phone: the model looks a value up in a computed table; it is never asked to produce the table.

1.8Time and the twice-daily job

vercel.json schedules /api/cron/daily twice, at 02:30 and 15:30 UTC. Vercel sends Authorization: Bearer $CRON_SECRET with each call, and the route refuses everything when the secret is unset rather than expose a job that spends model tokens to anyone who finds the path. It then runs two functions in order, runDuePredictions and runDailyAlerts, each over up to 500 profiles that have a chart, and returns both summaries as JSON. maxDuration is 300 seconds.

The route's own comment carries the design: "Both halves are decided per user rather than per run." The schedule is in UTC, but nothing about a user's reading is. Each user's timezone column, captured at intake from their birthplace, sets a local clock, and from that clock lib/alerts/slots.ts picks the slot: 

// lib/alerts/slots.ts
export const NIGHT_STARTS_AT_HOUR = 14;

export function slotForHour(localHour: number): Slot {
  return localHour < NIGHT_STARTS_AT_HOUR ? "morning" : "night";
}

/** Whether this user still needs the reading for the slot they are currently in. */
export function isSlotDue(localHour: number, alreadyWritten: Slot[]): Slot | null {
  const slot = slotForHour(localHour);
  return alreadyWritten.includes(slot) ? null : slot;
}

The stored for_date is the user's local date, so "my reading for the 26th" means their 26th. And daily_readings carries unique (user_id, for_date, slot), which migration 0005 describes as "what makes the job safe to run more often than expected, or twice by accident." writeDueReading reads the slots already written for today, returns early when the current one exists, and if two runs race past that check the insert fails with Postgres error 23505, which the code treats as "already done" rather than as an error. That is the whole idempotency argument for the readings: extra runs find nothing to do, and a race costs one wasted model call, never a duplicate row or a duplicate push.

The dosha sweep is idempotent for a different reason. runAlertsForUser recomputes today's sky at the user's birthplace with the same computeChart the chat uses, detects the conditions it forms against the natal chart, and diffs them against the open rows in transit_conditions, where a row is open while ended_on is null. What is in the new set and not the old has started; what is in the old and not the new has ended; what is in both is ignored, which is what stops a standing Sade Sati from becoming a daily nag. A partial unique index, transit_conditions_open_signature on (user_id, signature) where ended_on is null, guarantees one open row per condition however often the sweep runs. Only caution and warning severities reach a lock screen, at most six per alert, and a natal condition is never announced after a user's first sweep, because a natal condition cannot start or end and a change in one is an artifact of the computation changing. deriveHadNatalRows fails closed on a count error for exactly that reason, and its comment names the bug it prevents: pushing "Mangal Dosha has begun" to someone whose chart has not changed since birth.

Pushes go out through ApnsClient, which keeps one HTTP/2 session per environment for the life of a run and reuses a provider token for 45 minutes. A token Apple reports as unregistered is deleted from device_tokens on the spot, since Apple only says a token is dead when you use it. When APNs is not configured the readings and alerts are still written; the phone reads them from the tables and the widget from the cache, and only the notification is missing.

The cost shape Each reading's system prompt has two parts. The chart block is identical for a user on every run and is sent with a one-hour cache marker, so a morning and night reading that land within the hour share it; the today block changes daily and is not cached. The readings run on READING_MODEL at low effort. The alert copy in lib/alerts/compose.ts runs on DEEP_READING_MODEL, also at low effort.

1.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/env.ts, for the boundary. Twenty lines, and every secret in the system is one of its functions.
  2. lib/supabase/route.ts, then server.ts and middleware.ts, for the two auth channels meeting in one Db type.
  3. app/api/chat/route.ts, for the bet in practice: watch what is computed before the model is called.
  4. lib/astrology/chart.ts, for the engine, and lib/data/birthProfile.ts, for where its output lives and how it upgrades itself.
  5. mobile/project.yml, for the three targets and the six files the widget may compile.
  6. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift, then SancharaAPI.swift and Supabase.swift, for what the phone keeps, what it asks the server for, and what it reads directly.
  7. lib/alerts/slots.ts, predictions.ts and run.ts, with migrations 0004 and 0005 open beside them, for the two idempotency arguments.

Check yourself

Why is the anon key safe to ship in the iOS binary?

Because every table has Row Level Security with an owner-only policy, so the key grants nothing without a user's JWT and only that user's rows with one. The same key is in every web page.

How does a route handler tell a web caller from an iOS caller?

It does not need to. createRouteSupabase builds a bearer client when an Authorization: Bearer header is present and falls through to the cookie client otherwise; both are a SupabaseClient.

Name the three writes that use the service-role client on behalf of a signed-in user, and what they have in common.

Avatar upload to Storage, account deletion, and device-token registration. In each the user id comes from the caller's verified session first; the admin client only performs a write RLS would refuse.

Which files may the widget extension compile, and what do they have in common?

The six named in project.yml: Theme, ReadingCard, ChartCache, ChartData, BirthProfileDetails and OpenKundliIntent. None imports Supabase or the API client, because the extension has no session.

The cron runs at 02:30 and 15:30 UTC. Why does a user in any timezone still get a morning and a night reading?

The slot is chosen from the user's local hour (before 14:00 is morning), the date stored is their local date, and the unique index on (user_id, for_date, slot) means a run that finds the slot written does nothing.

What makes the dosha sweep safe to run twice in a day?

It diffs today's conditions against the open rows in transit_conditions; a second run finds the same set open, so nothing has started or ended, and no alert is written. The partial unique index enforces one open row per signature.

Where does a stale chart get fixed, and why is there no migration for it?

In ensureCurrentChart, on read. Both traditions are recomputed and rewritten the first time the owner uses the app after a schema change, so no backfill job has to touch every row.

Chapter two

2The chart engine

Nothing about a chart is guessed. lib/astrology/ turns a date, a time and a place into planets, houses, nakshatras, dashas, doshas and transits with the Swiss Ephemeris, and every later chapter reads from that output. Claude interprets these numbers; it never produces them.

This chapter describes the engine as it is on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It is a pure computation layer: no database, no network beyond one geocoding call on the input side, no interpretation. The same nine files serve the web app, the iOS app, the kundli PDF, the timeline, the twice-daily alerts job and the chat. If a number in a reading is wrong, this is where it went wrong, and the tests in section 2.10 are how you find out.

2.1What a chart is here

A birth chart is the sky at one moment, seen from one place on Earth, projected onto the ecliptic and cut into twelve signs of 30 degrees. The engine computes it in two traditions, and almost everything that follows depends on which one is asked for.

  • Tropical and sidereal. Western astrology measures longitude from the vernal equinox (tropical). Vedic astrology measures it from the fixed stars (sidereal). The two zodiacs drift apart by about 50 arcseconds a year and the gap today is roughly 24 degrees. That gap is the ayanamsa; the engine uses Lahiri's value, the one Indian almanacs use. A Sun at 10 degrees Capricorn tropical is at about 16 degrees Sagittarius sidereal, which is why one birth has a different Sun sign in each tradition.
  • The ascendant (lagna in Vedic usage) is the point of the ecliptic rising in the east at the birth moment. It passes through all twelve signs every day, which is why the birth time matters.
  • Houses divide the sky into twelve regions of life, counted from the ascendant. In the whole-sign system the ascendant's sign is house 1, the next sign house 2, and so on. In Placidus the houses are unequal arcs computed from the latitude and the sidereal time, and one can span parts of two signs. Vedic charts here are whole-sign, Western charts Placidus; section 2.4 explains why the engine once got that wrong and how a stored chart is corrected.
  • Nakshatras are the 27 lunar mansions of the sidereal zodiac, each 13 degrees 20 minutes wide. The Moon's nakshatra at birth decides the dasha.
  • Dashas are planetary periods. The Vimshottari system assigns 120 years of life to nine lords in a fixed order; the mahadasha is the major period and the antardasha the sub-period inside it. Where you are in that sequence is what "which dasha am I in" asks.
  • Doshas (and the yogas the code files alongside them) are named afflictions read from positions: Mars in certain houses, every planet on one side of the nodes, and so on.
  • Gochara is transit: today's sky read against the birth chart, by the house each moving planet occupies counted from the ascendant and from the natal Moon. Sade Sati, Saturn's seven and a half years around the natal Moon, is the best known.

The whole output is one JSON value, typed in lib/astrology/types.ts:

// lib/astrology/types.ts
export type Chart = {
  tradition: Tradition;                 // "vedic" | "western"
  ascendant: { sign: string; degree: number };
  houses: number[];                     // 12 cusp longitudes
  planets: Planet[];                    // name, sign, degree, house, retrograde, nakshatra?
  moonSign: string;
  sunSign: string;
  ayanamsa?: number;
  dasha?: DashaInfo;                    // Vedic only: current mahadasha and antardasha
  /** Bumped when the computation changes in a way that moves stored values. */
  schemaVersion?: number;
  /** Classical readings of the positions above. See lib/astrology/derived.ts. */
  derived?: Derived;
};

Three things about this shape decide the rest of the chapter. A planet is stored as a sign and a degree within that sign (0 to 30), not as an absolute longitude; every module that needs the longitude rebuilds it as signIndex(sign) * 30 + degree. The houses array is always the twelve Placidus cusps, even for a Vedic chart, and nothing renders from it. And dasha and derived exist only for Vedic, because every technique in them is Vedic doctrine.

2.2Files and the input side

lib/astrology/
├── types.ts          BirthInput, Planet, Chart, DashaInfo, Tradition, ChatMode
├── constants.ts      SIGNS, NAKSHATRAS, signOf, degreeInSign, nakshatraOf,
│                     houseFrom, ORDINAL, CHART_SCHEMA_VERSION
├── chart.ts          computeChart: the ephemeris, houses, Ketu, dasha, derived
├── dasha.ts          Vimshottari: startingDashaLord, computeVimshottari, vimshottariTimeline
├── derived.ts        lords, dignity, combustion, aspects, deriveFacts, factsFor, isChartStale
├── doshas.ts         detectNatalDoshas, detectTransitAfflictions, Condition, highestSeverity
├── transits.ts       transitChart (cached), describeGochara, describeToday
├── numerology.ts     mulank, bhagyank, name number, personal cycle, Lo Shu, relationships
└── prompt.ts         renders all of the above as text for the model (chapter 3)

lib/geo.ts            geocodePlace (Open-Meteo), resolveTimezone (tz-lookup)
app/api/geocode/      GET ?q= wraps geocodePlace for the intake forms
lib/data/birthProfile.ts   saveBirthProfile computes both charts; ensureCurrentChart upgrades

The engine's input is a BirthInput: a date as YYYY-MM-DD, a time as HH:mm, a latitude, a longitude and an IANA time zone name. The place name a person types never reaches the engine. lib/geo.ts turns it into those numbers. 

// lib/geo.ts
export function resolveTimezone(lat: number, lng: number): string {
  return tzlookup(lat, lng);
}

export async function geocodePlace(query: string): Promise<GeoResult[]> {
  const q = query.trim();
  if (q.length < 2) return [];
  const url = `https://geocoding-api.open-meteo.com/v1/search?name=${encodeURIComponent(q)}&count=5&language=en&format=json`;
  const res = await fetch(url, { headers: { accept: "application/json" } });
  if (!res.ok) return [];
  ...
  return (data.results ?? []).map((r) => ({
    name: [r.name, r.admin1, r.country].filter(Boolean).join(", "),
    lat: r.latitude,
    lng: r.longitude,
    timezone: resolveTimezone(r.latitude, r.longitude),
    country: r.country ?? "",
  }));
}

Two choices are worth naming. The geocoder is Open-Meteo's free endpoint, called without a key and capped at five results, and any failure (a short query, a non-2xx response) returns an empty list rather than an error. And the time zone is not taken from the geocoder: tz-lookup resolves it offline from the coordinates, so the zone is consistent with the point actually chosen. That is why the only test in lib/geo.test.ts is two coordinate pairs, New Delhi to Asia/Kolkata and New York to America/New_York. The offset in force on the birth date, daylight saving included, is then luxon's job inside computeChart.

2.3Positions: the ephemeris

The Swiss Ephemeris is the standard high-precision planetary library, here as the swisseph-wasm package, which compiles it to WebAssembly so that it runs inside a Next.js route handler with no native binary. chart.ts initialises it once per server process and keeps the promise, so concurrent first requests share one initialisation rather than racing to build two: 

// lib/astrology/chart.ts
let swePromise: Promise<InstanceType<typeof SwissEph>> | null = null;
async function getSwe() {
  if (!swePromise) {
    swePromise = (async () => {
      const swe = new SwissEph();
      await swe.initSwissEph();
      return swe;
    })();
  }
  return swePromise;
}

computeChart(input, tradition) then runs four numbered steps, which the file itself labels.

1. Local time to Universal Time

luxon parses "${birthDate}T${birthTime}" in the input's IANA zone, converts it to UTC, and the engine asks for the Julian day of that instant with swe.julday(year, month, day, decimalHour). A birth at 06:30 in Kolkata and one at 06:30 in New York are different skies, and luxon knows what offset was in force on that date.

2. Flags

Every call passes SEFLG_SWIEPH | SEFLG_SPEED: use the Swiss Ephemeris data files, and return velocities, because retrograde is read from speed. For a Vedic chart the engine also calls set_sid_mode(SE_SIDM_LAHIRI, 0, 0), adds SEFLG_SIDEREAL, and records get_ayanamsa_ut(jd) to three decimals as the chart's ayanamsa. A Western chart is tropical and has none.

3. Houses and the ascendant

houses_ex(jd, iflag, lat, lng, "P") returns thirteen cusps (index 1 to 12 used) and an ascmc array whose first element is the ascendant longitude. The letter is Placidus, in both traditions. The file's comment records the subtlety that makes this usable for Vedic: "In sidereal mode swisseph applies ayanamsa to cusps too", so the ascendant comes back sidereal and signOf(ascLon) is the lagna sign. The cusps become chart.houses, rounded to two decimals; for Vedic they are carried but never used to place a planet.

4. Planets

Eight bodies are asked for, in the order Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn and Rahu, where Rahu is SE_MEAN_NODE, the mean lunar north node. Each calc_ut returns [lon, lat, dist, lonSpeed, ...]; the engine keeps the longitude and the longitude speed, and a planet is retrograde exactly when that speed is negative. The Moon's normalised absolute longitude is kept aside for the dasha calculation. Each planet is stored as: 

planets.push({
  name: body.name,
  sign: signOf(lon),
  degree: Number(degreeInSign(lon).toFixed(2)),
  house: tradition === "vedic" ? houseFrom(ascSign, signOf(lon)) : houseOf(lon, cusps),
  retrograde: speed < 0,
  nakshatra: tradition === "vedic" ? nakshatraOf(lon) : undefined,
});

Ketu is not an ephemeris body. It is the south node, always exactly opposite Rahu, so the engine rebuilds Rahu's longitude from its stored sign and rounded degree, adds 180, and pushes a ninth planet with retrograde: true (the mean nodes always move backwards). Rounding first can move Ketu by up to a hundredth of a degree, well below anything rendered.

The helpers in constants.ts are one-line modular arithmetic on a longitude normalised into 0 to 360: signOf divides by 30, degreeInSign takes the remainder, nakshatraOf divides by 360/27. The chart then records sunSign and moonSign, the dasha for Vedic, the schema version, and, for Vedic only, the derived block (section 2.6).

Cost A chart is eight calc_ut calls and one houses_ex on an in-process WASM module: milliseconds after the first request, more on the cold start that initialises the module. chart.test.ts computes six full charts in about 50 ms. There is no per-chart cost beyond CPU.

2.4Houses: whole-sign and Placidus

Two functions place a planet in a house, and which one runs is decided by the tradition.

// lib/astrology/constants.ts
/** The 1-indexed house of `sign` counted from `from`, the classical "Nth from". */
export function houseFrom(from: string, sign: string): number {
  const a = signIndex(from);
  const b = signIndex(sign);
  if (a < 0 || b < 0) return 0;
  return ((b - a + 12) % 12) + 1;
}

// lib/astrology/chart.ts
function houseOf(longitude: number, cusps: number[]): number {
  const lon = ((longitude % 360) + 360) % 360;
  for (let i = 0; i < 12; i++) {
    const start = cusps[i];
    const end = cusps[(i + 1) % 12];
    if (start <= end) {
      if (lon >= start && lon < end) return i + 1;
    } else {
      // wraps past 360°
      if (lon >= start || lon < end) return i + 1;
    }
  }
  return 1;
}

houseFrom is whole-sign counting and it is the most-called function in the engine: the chart, the derived facts, every dosha detector and every transit line use it, with the ascendant sign or the natal Moon's sign as from. It returns 0 for an unknown name so a caller can tell the failure apart from house 1. houseOf walks the Placidus cusps, handling the one pair that wraps through 0 degrees Aries, and is used only for Western charts.

VedicWestern
ZodiacSidereal, Lahiri ayanamsaTropical
Ascendantascmc[0], siderealascmc[0], tropical
Planet househouseFrom(ascSign, sign): whole-signhouseOf(lon, cusps): Placidus
chart.housesPlacidus cusps, carried, unusedPlacidus cusps, the truth
NakshatraPer planetAbsent
dasha, derivedPresentAbsent

The upgrade of stored charts

Until the change recorded in docs/design/2026-09-17-specific-readings.md, section 0, Vedic planets were placed by houseOf too. The kundli renderers and the PDF had always drawn the houses whole-sign (house 1 labelled with the lagna's sign) and then filled them by the stored Placidus number, so a planet could be drawn inside a house labelled with a sign it was not in. Placidus also lets two houses carry the same sign and one sign appear in none, which leaves "the lord of the 7th" undefined, and every technique in derived.ts rests on lordship. So Vedic became whole-sign: 

// lib/astrology/constants.ts
/**
 * Version 2 moved Vedic charts from Placidus to whole-sign houses. A stored
 * chart below this version has house numbers that disagree with the rashi
 * labels every renderer draws, so readers upgrade it before use.
 */
export const CHART_SCHEMA_VERSION = 2;

A chart written before then carries no schemaVersion at all, which isChartStale reads as 1. The rule in derived.ts is that a chart is stale when its version is below 2 or it has no derived block. Two mechanisms then cooperate so the app ships without a migration or a backfill job:

  • Readers recompute houses from the sign, never from planet.house. deriveFacts builds its own house map with houseFrom(asc, p.sign), and the dosha detectors do the same, so a stale chart still derives correct facts in memory. Both test files pin it with fixtures whose stored house is deliberately 99.
  • The stored row is rewritten on read. ensureCurrentChart in lib/data/birthProfile.ts runs on every getBirthProfile. If the stored Vedic chart is stale it recomputes both traditions together, so a profile is never half upgraded, writes the pair back and returns the fresh row. A compute or write failure is logged and swallowed: an upgrade that cannot be written is not a reason to fail the request that triggered it. The rewrite is needed because the kundli, the PDF and the iOS app draw from the stored numbers.
A visible change Whole-sign moves planets. One in the lagna's sign at a degree below the lagna's degree moves from house 12 to house 1, and similar shifts happen throughout. The design note records that users' charts change when next computed and that whether to say so in the app is a product decision. It also records the consequence for alerts: a house change alters which natal doshas are detected, and the alerts job suppresses natal conditions for anyone who already has a natal row so the migration cannot push "Mangal Dosha has just begun". See chapter 9.

2.5Nakshatras and dashas

The 27 nakshatras are listed in constants.ts in zodiacal order from Ashwini at 0 degrees Aries. Each is 360/27 degrees, and nakshatraOf is just the floor of the longitude over that span. The names, nine per row, are:

IndexNakshatras
0 to 8Ashwini, Bharani, Krittika, Rohini, Mrigashira, Ardra, Punarvasu, Pushya, Ashlesha
9 to 17Magha, Purva Phalguni, Uttara Phalguni, Hasta, Chitra, Swati, Vishakha, Anuradha, Jyeshtha
18 to 26Mula, Purva Ashadha, Uttara Ashadha, Shravana, Dhanishta, Shatabhisha, Purva Bhadrapada, Uttara Bhadrapada, Revati

The rows are not decorative. Vimshottari assigns each nakshatra a lord, and the lords repeat every nine nakshatras, so the lord of nakshatra n is ORDER[n % 9]. That is the entire content of startingDashaLord. The order and the years are:

PositionLordYearsStarts under it
0Ketu7Ashwini, Magha, Mula
1Venus20Bharani, Purva Phalguni, Purva Ashadha
2Sun6Krittika, Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha
3Moon10Rohini, Hasta, Shravana
4Mars7Mrigashira, Chitra, Dhanishta
5Rahu18Ardra, Swati, Shatabhisha
6Jupiter16Punarvasu, Vishakha, Purva Bhadrapada
7Saturn19Pushya, Anuradha, Uttara Bhadrapada
8Mercury17Ashlesha, Jyeshtha, Revati

The years sum to 120, the TOTAL constant. A year is 365.25 days (addYears adds y * 365.25 days), which the file labels the Vedic year and which the tests pin: seven Ketu years from 1 January 1990 end on 31 December 1996.

The virtual start

Nobody is born at the start of a mahadasha. The Moon at birth is some fraction of the way through its nakshatra, and that fraction of the first lord's period has already elapsed. The first span therefore begins at birth and runs for the balance, years * (1 - fractionElapsed). But the antardashas inside it must be laid out from where the mahadasha would have begun, not from birth, or every sub-period for the rest of that mahadasha lands in the wrong place. dasha.ts carries that point as virtualStart: 

// lib/astrology/dasha.ts
const first = ORDER[startLordIndex];
const spans: Span[] = [{
  lord: first.lord,
  start: birthUt,
  // The first period is the *balance* of the one already running at birth.
  end: addYears(birthUt, first.years * (1 - fractionElapsed)),
  virtualStart: addYears(birthUt, -(first.years * fractionElapsed)),
  fullYears: first.years,
}];
for (let i = 1; i < 12; i++) {
  const o = ORDER[(startLordIndex + i) % 9];
  const start = spans[i - 1].end;
  spans.push({ lord: o.lord, start, end: addYears(start, o.years), virtualStart: start, fullYears: o.years });
}

Twelve spans, not nine: the cycle is 120 years and the engine lays out a third of a second cycle so a long life still has a current period. Each span's nine antardashas run in the same order starting from the span's own lord, each lasting fullYears * subYears / 120 of the parent, laid out from virtualStart. A sub-period that ended before birth is dropped; one straddling birth is clipped to start at birth. As the comment puts it, a chart has nothing to say about time the native was not alive for.

Two exported functions share this layout. computeVimshottari(moonLon, birthUt, now) answers "where am I now": it finds the span containing now (the current UTC instant by default, injectable so tests are deterministic), then the antardasha containing it, and returns both as ISO dates in the DashaInfo the chart stores; outside every span it falls back to the first, which keeps the function total. vimshottariTimeline(moonLon, birthUt) returns all twelve spans with their antardashas nested, and is what lib/timeline/build.ts draws the life timeline from, rebuilding the Moon's longitude from the stored sign and degree. The chart itself carries only the current pair, because that is the only part that ages.

2.6Derived facts

derived.ts exists because a model handed nine planet lines and a lagna writes sign-level prose. What makes an answer specific is which house a planet rules, whether it is strong or weak where it sits, whom it aspects, and where the current dasha lord actually is. The module's header states the scope: "a fixed classical table applied to positions the ephemeris already produced. There is no I/O and no interpretation: the output is the grounding a reading is written from, not the reading."

The tables

SignLordSignLordSignLord
AriesMarsLeoSunSagittariusJupiter
TaurusVenusVirgoMercuryCapricornSaturn
GeminiMercuryLibraVenusAquariusSaturn
CancerMoonScorpioMarsPiscesJupiter

SIGN_LORD is declared in zodiacal order and a comment forbids reordering it: both oppositeSign and the house loop index into Object.keys(SIGN_LORD). Rahu and Ketu rule nothing, which is the first of three rules the module deliberately leaves out.

PlanetExalted (deep degree)DebilitatedMoolatrikonaCombustion orb, direct / retrogradeSpecial aspects
SunAries 10LibraLeo 0 to 20never7th only
MoonTaurus 3ScorpioTaurus 4 to 3012 / 127th only
MarsCapricorn 28CancerAries 0 to 1217 / 174th, 8th
MercuryVirgo 15PiscesVirgo 16 to 2014 / 127th only
JupiterCancer 5CapricornSagittarius 0 to 1011 / 115th, 9th
VenusPisces 27VirgoLibra 0 to 1510 / 87th only
SaturnLibra 20AriesAquarius 0 to 2015 / 153rd, 10th
Rahu, Ketunonenonenonenever7th only

The debilitation sign is not a table; it is oppositeSign of the exaltation sign. The moolatrikona ranges use a half-open interval, from <= degree < to.

Dignity, and the one three-way case

dignityOf(name, sign, degree) returns one of exalted, debilitated, moolatrikona, own or neutral, plus fromDeepPoint, the distance in degrees from the exact exaltation degree, reported only when the planet is in its exaltation or debilitation sign. Exaltation and debilitation are read by sign, not by degree, so the Sun at 29 Aries is still exalted; the deep point is extra information, not a threshold. The precedence is exalted, then moolatrikona, then own, then debilitated, then neutral.

Two planets break the simple chain: Mercury's exaltation and moolatrikona are both Virgo, and the Moon's are both Taurus. For them a dedicated block owns the whole sign: below the moolatrikona range's from the planet is exalted, inside it moolatrikona, above it own sign if the planet rules the sign (Mercury rules Virgo; the Moon's range runs to 30 so the question never arises). The comment above that block warns that it "must return on every path", because falling through would hit the unconditional exaltation check, and asks the reader not to simplify it into a plain if/else chain. So Mercury at 14 Virgo is exalted, at 18 moolatrikona, at 25 own, and at 15.5 exalted, because 15.5 is below from: 16 even though it is past the deep degree of 15.

Combustion and aspects

A planet is combust when its shorter arc from the Sun is at or within its orb, and the orb depends on whether it is retrograde: Mercury tightens from 14 to 12 and Venus from 10 to 8, the others do not change. The Sun and the nodes are never combust. arcFromSun rebuilds both longitudes from sign and degree and takes the shorter way round, so Venus at 4 Leo is 6 degrees from a Sun at 10 Leo, and Venus at 5 Cancer is 35 degrees away, not 325.

Aspects (graha drishti) are by house, not by degree. Every planet aspects the 7th house from its own; Mars adds the 4th and 8th, Jupiter the 5th and 9th, Saturn the 3rd and 10th. The nodes get the 7th only: the second deliberately absent rule, because the tradition does not agree on a nodal 5th and 9th. aspectedHouses(name, house) wraps past 12, so Saturn in the 12th aspects the 2nd, 6th and 9th.

deriveFacts

deriveFacts(chart) assembles four lists. For each planet a PlanetFact: its whole-sign house (recomputed from the sign, as section 2.4 explained), the houses it rules (its signs, each counted from the ascendant, sorted), its dignity and deep-point distance, whether it is combust and how far it is from the Sun, the houses it aspects, the planets in those houses, and the planets it is conjunct with (same house, itself excluded). For each of the twelve houses a HouseFact: its sign, its lord, where that lord sits and in what dignity, its occupants, and which planets aspect it. For the dasha, two DashaFact entries (mahadasha and antardasha) each carrying its lord's own PlanetFact as placement, which is what lets a reading say the Jupiter period is running from the 8th house rather than just "Jupiter period". And conditions, the natal doshas from section 2.7.

The third absent rule is friendship. Dignity never consults planetary friendship, because, in the header's words, friendship is a compound judgement, natural plus temporal, and half of it is misleading rather than partial. (Numerology does use a natural friendship table, for a different purpose; see section 2.9.)

Two small functions decide who gets these facts:

// lib/astrology/derived.ts
export function isChartStale(chart: Chart): boolean {
  return (chart.schemaVersion ?? 1) < CHART_SCHEMA_VERSION || !chart.derived;
}

export function factsFor(chart: Chart): Derived | undefined {
  if (chart.tradition !== "vedic") return undefined;
  return chart.derived ?? deriveFacts(chart);
}

factsFor is what the chat route and the alerts job call. A Western chart gets undefined, and the callers fall back to plain chart data rather than resurrecting Vedic technique for a tropical chart. The comment at the end of computeChart says the same thing from the other side: "Every technique in derived.ts is Vedic. Western continues to use Placidus houses and gets no derived block at all."

2.7Doshas

doshas.ts reads afflictions from a chart, and from today's chart against a birth chart, into one shape: 

// lib/astrology/doshas.ts
export type Condition = {
  kind: string;
  signature: string;
  label: string;
  severity: "info" | "caution" | "warning";
  scope: "natal" | "transit";
  /** A plain factual sentence, the grounding the model is allowed to interpret. */
  detail: string;
};

The signature is what makes the alerts job possible. It folds in the phase and the sign, so Saturn moving to the next sign, or Sade Sati stepping from rising to peak, reads as a new condition rather than the same one continuing. The daily job diffs signatures against the rows it stored last time; one that appears has started, one that disappears has ended, and only those two events send a push. The detail is a sentence of fact with the ordinal already in it, and it is the only text the model is given about the condition.

Natal doshas

detectNatalDoshas(chart) runs six checks. They never change for a person, so the job reports them once, when a chart is first read.

KindRule as codedSeveritySignature
mangal_doshaMars in house 1, 2, 4, 7, 8 or 12 from the ascendant, house derived with houseFrom from the signcautionmangal_dosha:<house>
kaal_sarpAll seven classical planets present, and their arcs measured forward from Rahu are all strictly between 0 and 180, or all above 180warningkaal_sarp:<Rahu sign>
grahan_sun, grahan_moonThe Sun or the Moon sharing a sign with Rahu or Ketu; one condition per paircautiongrahan_sun:<node>:<sign>
kemadrumaNo planet in the 2nd or 12th sign from the Moon, ignoring the Sun and the nodescautionkemadruma:<Moon sign>
pitru_doshaRahu or Ketu in the 9th from the ascendant, from the signinfopitru_dosha:<node>:<sign>
shrapit_doshaSaturn and Rahu in the same signcautionshrapit_dosha:<sign>

Kaal Sarp is the only detector that needs degrees; a planet exactly on the axis breaks the yoga, which the strict inequalities encode. The Mangal and Pitru checks carry a comment on why they compute the house rather than read mars.house: a chart stored before the whole-sign change carries a stale Placidus number, and the natal conditions must agree with the whole-sign facts in the same Derived object, or, as derived.test.ts puts it, one object would carry "two contradictory claims about the same fixed birth fact".

Transit afflictions

detectTransitAfflictions(natal, transit) is the set that comes and goes, and the set worth a notification when it does. Every rule is a whole-sign count from the natal Moon or the ascendant, or a shared sign.

KindRule as codedSeverity
sade_satiTransit Saturn in the 12th (rising), 1st (peak) or 2nd (setting) from the natal Moonwarning at peak, else caution
shani_dhaiyaTransit Saturn in the 4th or 8th from the natal Moon, only when Sade Sati did not matchcaution
kantaka_shaniTransit Saturn in the 4th, 7th or 10th from the ascendantinfo
node_over_moon_rahu, node_over_moon_ketuA transiting node in the natal Moon's signcaution
node_over_lagna_rahu, node_over_lagna_ketuA transiting node in the ascendant signcaution
angarak_transitTransit Mars in the natal Moon's signcaution
ashtama_suryaTransit Sun in the 8th from the natal Mooninfo
guru_6_8_12Transit Jupiter in the 6th, 8th or 12th from the natal Mooninfo
guru_chandal_transitTransit Jupiter and transit Rahu in the same signinfo
retrograde_saturn, retrograde_mars, retrograde_mercuryThat planet retrograde and in the ascendant sign or the natal Moon's sign; a retrograde elsewhere is ignoredinfo

Every transit signature includes the transiting sign, and Sade Sati's includes the phase, so a Saturn ingress or a phase change is a fresh event. detectConditions concatenates the natal and transit lists, and highestSeverity reduces a list to its worst entry with info as the floor, which the alerts job uses to decide whether a push is warranted.

2.8Transits (gochara)

A transit chart is a birth chart for now: the same computeChart, with today's date, and the user's birthplace as the location. transits.ts adds a cache and a renderer around it. 

// lib/astrology/transits.ts
const cache = new Map<string, Promise<Chart>>();
const MAX_ENTRIES = 200;

export async function transitChart(args) {
  const day = args.day ?? utcDayKey();
  // One degree is ~111km; users in the same city share an entry.
  const key = `${day}|${args.tradition}|${args.lat.toFixed(0)}|${args.lng.toFixed(0)}`;
  const hit = cache.get(key);
  if (hit) return hit;
  const pending = computeChart(
    { birthDate: day, birthTime: "12:00", lat: args.lat, lng: args.lng, timezone: "UTC" },
    args.tradition,
  );
  if (cache.size >= MAX_ENTRIES) cache.clear();
  cache.set(key, pending);
  try { return await pending; }
  catch (err) { cache.delete(key); throw err; }
}

The sample point is midday UTC, for two reasons the file gives. The obvious one is no ephemeris run per message. The one that pays is that the text built from this chart is identical for the whole UTC day, so it can sit in the cached half of the system prompt (chapter 3) instead of being billed on every turn. Only the Moon moves enough within a day to show at sign resolution, and sampling the middle halves its worst-case drift. The key rounds the coordinates to whole degrees so a city shares one entry; the cache holds promises so concurrent misses share one computation; it clears itself at 200 entries rather than hold stale days; and a failed computation is evicted, never cached.

One caller does not use this cache. The alerts job's condition diff in lib/alerts/run.ts calls computeChart directly with the actual UTC time of the run, since it is deciding whether an affliction has started at that moment. The reading text the same job writes goes through transitChart, for the prompt-cache reason.

describeGochara

The old transit text was "Saturn in Pisces, Jupiter in Gemini", which the file notes "was true of everyone alive on that date and related to the reader not at all". What makes a transit mean anything is the house it falls in from the lagna and from the natal Moon. describeGochara(natal, transit, { includeAfflictions }) writes one line per transiting planet: its sign and retrograde flag; for a Vedic chart its house from the ascendant and its ordinal from the Moon; and any natal planets it sits on by sign, joined so three names read "a, b, and c". For a Vedic chart it then appends a line per transit affliction. The flag exists because the alerts job renders the afflictions itself, filtered by severity, and false avoids saying the same thing twice in one prompt; the chat route has no other source and takes the default.

A Western chart gets no house counting and no afflictions, and the comment says why: the house numbers would be whole-sign where Western is Placidus, and Sade Sati has no meaning in that tradition. It keeps the plain sky, where each body is and which natal body it sits on. describeToday is the last export: a weekday, a date and one of morning, afternoon, evening or night (thresholds 12, 17 and 21) in the user's zone, coarse enough to stay identical for hours.

2.9Numerology

numerology.ts is the one module with no ephemeris in it. Everything is digit arithmetic on the birth date string, and the core operation is reduceToDigit, which sums the digits of a number repeatedly until one digit remains. It never preserves the "master numbers" 11 and 22 some systems keep; every result here is 1 to 9.

FunctionInputRuleExample from the tests
computeNumerology: mulankDay of birthReduce the day23rd gives 5
computeNumerology: bhagyankFull dateReduce the sum of all eight digits1990-01-23 gives 25, then 7
computeNameNumberA namePythagorean: A=1 to I=9, J=1 again, letters only, reduced; 0 when there are no letters
personalYearBirth date, todayReduce birth month + birth day + current year15 July 1990 in 2026: 7+15+2026 = 2048, then 5
personalMonthBirth date, todayReduce personal year + current month5 + 9 = 14, then 5
personalCycleBirth date, todayBoth of the above as { year, month }22 March 1985 on 10 January 2026: year 8, month 9
loShuBirth dateCount each digit 1 to 9 in the eight digits; zeros are not placed on the grid1990-07-15: two 1s, two 9s, missing 2, 3, 4, 6, 8

Two comments explain design choices. The personal year changes once a calendar year, which is why the prompt carries it in the half that varies rather than the cached half. personalCycle exists because personalYear and personalMonth take identical arguments, so a caller building the pair from two positional calls could transpose them with every test still passing; one function a test can assert against by name moves that risk out of every call site. The Lo Shu grid is, in its own comment, "the most concrete thing numerology has": three 1s and no 4 is specific in a way a root number never is.

Number relationships

Numbers 1 to 9 are ruled by planets, and the relationship of two numbers is the natural friendship of their rulers. The rulers are Sun 1, Moon 2, Jupiter 3, Saturn 4, Mercury 5, Venus 6, Mercury 7, Saturn 8, Mars 9. The comment is candid that 4 and 7 belong to Rahu and Ketu, who have no friendships the tradition agrees on, and that giving them Saturn's and Mercury's rows is a convention kept in one place so it can be corrected in one place.

PlanetFriendsEnemies
SunMoon, Mars, JupiterVenus, Saturn
MoonSun, Mercurynone
MarsSun, Moon, JupiterMercury
MercurySun, VenusMoon
JupiterSun, Moon, MarsMercury, Venus
VenusMercury, SaturnSun, Moon
SaturnMercury, VenusSun, Moon, Mars

The table is genuinely asymmetric: the Moon lists no enemies, but Venus lists the Moon. Read only the first number's row and numberRelationship(2, 6) is neutral while numberRelationship(6, 2) is enemy, and the prompt renders the result as a reciprocal sentence, so two users would get opposite claims about the same pair. The function resolves it compoundly: the same ruler is a friend; enemy if either direction says enemy; friend only if both directions say friend; neutral otherwise. Sun and Moon are friends, Sun and Venus enemies, Sun and Mercury neutral (the Sun does not list Mercury, so one direction fails).

2.10Tests

Six test files sit beside the modules, 78 tests, all run by vitest run with no network and no database. Only chart.test.ts touches the ephemeris; everything else is fixtures. The reference birth for the ephemeris tests is 1 January 1990, 12:00 in New Delhi. prompt.test.ts also lives here but tests the rendering for the model, which is chapter 3's subject.

  • chart.test.ts (9). A Western chart has twelve cusps, all nine bodies, a tropical Sun in Capricorn and an ascendant degree in 0 to 30. The Vedic chart of the same birth has its Sun in Sagittarius, an ayanamsa between 22 and 26, a nakshatra on the Sun and a mahadasha and antardasha present. Then the house rules: every Vedic planet's house equals houseFrom(ascendant, sign); a planet in the lagna's sign is in house 1 whatever its degree; the schema version is stamped; and Western is still Placidus, proven by asserting that at least one planet does not match whole-sign counting. Finally the derived block is stored with a lord for every house, a chart with it stripped is stale and factsFor recomputes, and a Western chart has no derived block at all.
  • dasha.test.ts (9). A Moon at 0 degrees (Ashwini) starts under Ketu; at 120 degrees (Magha) Ketu again, proving the nine-fold repeat. A fixed now of 2020 gives Sun for a 1990 birth at 0 degrees (Ketu 7, Venus 20, Sun 6). The timeline has twelve mahadashas with the first ending on 1996-12-31 (7 times 365.25 days), no gap between consecutive periods, nine antardashas per period starting with the period's own lord, the first antardasha starting where its mahadasha starts and the last ending where it ends, a Moon halfway through Ashwini giving a 3.5-year balance ending 1993-07-02 with fewer than nine antardashas, and agreement with computeVimshottari about the current pair.
  • derived.test.ts (23). Dignity by sign not degree; the deep-point distance; Mercury in Virgo at 14, 18, 25 and 15.5; the Moon in Taurus; moolatrikona beating own sign; the nodes neutral. Combustion across a sign boundary, the tighter retrograde orbs for Venus and Mercury, never for the Sun or the nodes. Aspects for everyone, for Mars, Jupiter and Saturn, wrapping past the 12th, and the nodes' 7th only. Then a Scorpio-lagna fixture whose every stored house is 99, proving deriveFacts recomputes houses, assigns lordships (none to the nodes), marks Mars exalted at its deep point, finds conjunctions and aspects by house, marks Mercury combust two degrees from the Sun, gives every house a lord with its placement, carries both dasha lords with their placements, folds in a Mangal dosha from the sign with the stored house still 99, and returns no dasha facts for Western.
  • doshas.test.ts (13). houseFrom counts inclusively and wraps. A spread-out chart raises nothing. Mangal dosha follows the sign and ignores a wrong stored house in both directions, and its ordinal and signature read 7th from a stored 99. Kaal Sarp with everything hemmed between Rahu at 0 Aries and Ketu at 0 Libra, broken by moving Saturn past Ketu. Kemadruma for a Moon with no neighbours, cancelled by a planet in the 12th from it. Grahan for Rahu with the Sun. Pitru from the sign. The three Sade Sati phases with peak at warning; Dhaiya without Sade Sati; a node over the Moon and over the lagna; a retrograde flagged only on a sensitive sign; and highestSeverity.
  • numerology.test.ts (12). Mulank for the 23rd, 9th and 10th; bhagyank for 1990-01-23; the personal year worked example and its stability across the calendar year; the personal month; personalCycle against a pair chosen so year and month differ (8 and 9), because the other fixture reduces both to 5 and would hide a transposition; the Lo Shu counts, missing and repeated lists; and relationships, including the asymmetric Moon and Venus pair resolving to enemy in both directions.
  • transits.test.ts (12). For a Scorpio lagna with a Cancer Moon, transiting Saturn in Pisces gets "house 5 from the ascendant" and "9th from the Moon", Jupiter in Cancer "1st from the Moon", each names the natal planet it sits on, and the text never contains "undefined". Saturn on the natal Moon's sign yields a Sade Sati line by default and none with includeAfflictions: false. The same fixture as a Western chart still says where Saturn is and that it is over the natal Moon, but counts no house and names no Sade Sati, while the Vedic version of the same sky gets all three.

2.11Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/astrology/types.ts, for the shape everything else produces or consumes. Forty-seven lines.
  2. lib/astrology/constants.ts, for houseFrom, which you will then recognise everywhere.
  3. lib/astrology/chart.ts, top to bottom, with the four numbered steps.
  4. lib/astrology/chart.test.ts, which states the house rules as assertions.
  5. lib/astrology/dasha.ts, for the virtual start; then dasha.test.ts for the dates it produces.
  6. lib/astrology/derived.ts, the header comment first, then dignityOf, then deriveFacts.
  7. lib/astrology/doshas.ts, reading each detector against the tables in section 2.7.
  8. lib/astrology/transits.ts, for the cache and the Western guard.
  9. lib/data/birthProfile.ts, ensureCurrentChart, for how a stale chart is corrected.
  10. docs/design/2026-09-17-specific-readings.md, section 0, for why.

Check yourself

Why are Vedic houses whole-sign but Western houses Placidus?

Every Vedic technique in derived.ts rests on lordship, and Placidus can put one sign in two houses and another in none, leaving "the lord of the 7th" undefined. Placidus is correct for the Western tradition, which uses none of those techniques.

A stored chart has schemaVersion missing and Mars with house: 12. What does detectNatalDoshas do with that 12?

Nothing. It computes houseFrom(ascendant.sign, mars.sign) and ignores the stored number, so the answer agrees with the whole-sign facts. The row is then rewritten by ensureCurrentChart the next time its owner is read.

Why does the first mahadasha have a virtualStart before birth?

Because the antardashas must be laid out from where the period would have begun, not from birth; otherwise every sub-period for the rest of that mahadasha is misplaced. Sub-periods that ended before birth are dropped and one straddling birth is clipped.

Mercury at 15.5 degrees Virgo: exalted or moolatrikona?

Exalted. The shared-sign block gives exaltation to everything below the moolatrikona range's from of 16, even past the deep degree of 15.

Why is the transit chart sampled at midday UTC rather than at request time?

So the gochara text is identical for the whole day and can sit in the cached half of the system prompt. Only the Moon moves enough within a day to matter at sign resolution, and midday halves its worst-case drift.

Which three rules does derived.ts refuse to encode, and why?

Nodes ruling signs, nodes aspecting the 5th and 9th, and friendship in dignity. The tradition does not agree on them, and a contested claim repeated in every reading is worse than a missing one.

Chapter three

3Readings, prompts, and cost

Claude never computes a chart and never sees one it was not handed. A reading is an interpretation of numbers the Swiss Ephemeris produced, delivered through a system prompt built in two halves so that the expensive half is paid for once and read from cache after that.

This chapter describes the reading pipeline as it stands on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It covers the prompt builders in lib/astrology/prompt.ts, the model and effort settings in lib/anthropic.ts, the caching and history rules that the chat route applies, the one test file whose name promises more than it delivers, and the measured cost figures in docs/MODEL_COSTS.md. The chat feature that sits on top of all this, with its route handler, tables and clients, is chapter 6. The chart engine that produces the facts is chapter 2.

3.1What a reading is

A reading is one streamed answer from Claude to one question, grounded in facts the server computed. The facts are the user's stored chart (birth_profiles.chart, holding a Vedic and a Western chart side by side), the derived facts attached to the Vedic one (lords, aspects, dignity, combustion, natal doshas), the numerology of the birth date and name, today's sky read against the natal chart, and the pinned moments of the user's life timeline. Claude is told, in so many words, that these are the only facts it has.

Three modes exist, and the type system keeps two of them apart from the third. Tradition in lib/astrology/types.ts is "vedic" | "western": the two ways of computing a chart. ChatMode adds "numerology", which has no chart at all and reads from the birth date and the name. The chat route accepts a ChatMode; only the two chart modes ever reach the ephemeris.

A reading is also either standard or deep. Standard runs on Sonnet with brief thinking and a 160 word length rule; deep runs on Opus with the default thinking budget and 320 words. The client decides with one boolean, deep, in the request body.

Chat is not the only caller. Every model call in the repository goes through the one client in lib/anthropic.ts, and three other places make readings of their own: the daily morning and night readings, the dosha alerts, and the timeline's period meanings and moment extraction. They share the prompt builders and the effort setting, which is why this chapter is a foundations chapter and not a chat chapter.

CallerModelEffortPrompt builders used
app/api/chat/route.ts, standardREADING_MODELlowbuildChartSystem or buildNumerologySystem, then buildTodaySystem
app/api/chat/route.ts, deepDEEP_READING_MODELdefaultthe same
lib/alerts/predictions.ts, the twice-daily readingsREADING_MODELlowbuildChartSystem then buildTodaySystem with maxWords: 150
lib/alerts/compose.ts, the dosha alert copyDEEP_READING_MODELlowits own system string
lib/timeline/explain.ts and lib/timeline/extract.tsREADING_MODELlowtheir own system strings

3.2Files

lib/astrology/prompt.ts          buildChartSystem, buildTodaySystem, buildNumerologySystem,
                                 buildSystemPrompt and buildNumerologyPrompt (the joined forms)
lib/astrology/prompt.test.ts     what the prompt must and must not contain
lib/astrology/derived.ts         factsFor: the derived facts, Vedic only
lib/astrology/transits.ts        transitChart (memoised per day), describeGochara, describeToday
lib/astrology/numerology.ts      computeNumerology, computeNameNumber, loShu, personalCycle
lib/timeline/describe.ts         describeTimelineForPrompt, appended to the stable half
lib/anthropic.ts                 READING_MODEL, DEEP_READING_MODEL, LOW_EFFORT, the client
lib/data/history.ts              selectHistory and HISTORY_BUDGET_CHARS
lib/data/history.test.ts         the budget rules
lib/data/injection.test.ts       the data helpers accept an injected Supabase client
lib/data/chat.ts                 getMessages, appendMessage, getOrCreateConversation
app/api/chat/route.ts            where the halves meet the cache breakpoints
docs/MODEL_COSTS.md              the measured numbers, and why each change was made

lib/anthropic.ts begins with import "server-only". Importing it from a client component fails the build, which is the mechanism that keeps ANTHROPIC_API_KEY on the server: the key is read through env.anthropicApiKey() in lib/env.ts, and that module is server-only too. The client is a lazy singleton; anthropic() constructs it on first use so that a module import never throws for want of a key.

3.3The system prompt in two halves

The system prompt is two strings, built by two functions, sent as two blocks in the system array of the request. The first block is everything that never changes for a user. The second is everything that does. The split exists for one reason, stated in the comment above buildChartSystem: the first half carries a cache breakpoint, so it must be byte-identical on every turn and across conversations, and anything that varies has to live in the second half and come after it.

The stable half: buildChartSystem

buildChartSystem takes the first name, the tradition, the chart, optionally the derived facts, and optionally the two numerology numbers. It returns one string in a fixed order: an identity line, the facts block, the numerology line, then three rule lists headed Accuracy, How to answer and Format, and a closing line. The identity line names the system the way the astrologer would: "Vedic (sidereal, Lahiri)" or "Western (tropical)".
