The facts block is introduced as the user's real chart, computed with the Swiss Ephemeris, and the sentence that follows is the whole grounding contract: these are the only facts you have.

The Accuracy rules forbid stating any placement, lordship, aspect, dasha, condition or number not listed above; require every claim to name the placement it reads from, its house and the technique; ask for tendencies and timing rather than guaranteed outcomes; and tell the model to say what the chart does show when it does not show what was asked, or to ask one short question when one missing detail would change the answer. The How to answer rules ban sign-personality writing outright: a sentence that would be true of a twelfth of the population is not an answer, and the model is told to delete it and read a house lord, an aspect, or the dasha lord's placement instead. The Format rules ask for two or three short sections under bold markdown headings, plain text with no emoji and no bold inside sentences, and a final section titled In simple words. The last line is the one the tests check for the spirit of the thing: never invent, and never flatter, and if the honest reading is unremarkable, say so plainly.

The facts block has two renderers, and which one runs is the Vedic versus Western branch of the whole system.

The Vedic branch: renderChartFacts

When derived is present the block is rendered by renderChartFacts, which writes five labelled sections from the Derived structure that lib/astrology/derived.ts computes:

  • The ascendant, marked as setting house 1, and the Sun and Moon signs.
  • PLACEMENTS: one line per planet from planetLine. Each line gives sign, degree, house and nakshatra, then the houses the planet rules, its dignity word (exalted, debilitated, in moolatrikona, in its own sign, neutral), "within a degree of exact" when it sits under a degree from its deep point, combustion with the distance from the Sun, retrograde, the houses it aspects, and the planets it is conjunct with.
  • HOUSES: one line per house giving its sign, its lord, where that lord sits and with what dignity, which planets occupy the house and which aspect it.
  • CURRENT PERIOD: the mahadasha and antardasha lords with their dates and, for each, the lord's own natal placement, dignity and rulerships, because that placement is what gives the period its character.
  • STANDING CONDITIONS IN THE BIRTH CHART: the natal doshas, one label and one detail line each.

Two grammar helpers exist because the model copies the prompt's register. houseSuffix returns "s" for a plural list of houses and nothing for one, so "aspects house 9" and "aspects houses 4, 7, 8" both read correctly; the test suite pins both forms. RELATIONSHIP_PHRASE phrases a number relationship with the pair as the subject ("are friends", "are enemies") to avoid an article agreement error. One sentinel matters more than the grammar: a house whose lord is not a body in the chart comes out of deriveFacts with lordHouse of 0, and the renderer says "lord Sun (not a body in this chart)" rather than printing house 0 as though it were a placement. Every real chart has all nine bodies, so this is only reachable from a hand-built fixture, but a prompt that stated a placement that does not exist would be teaching the model to do the same.

The Western branch: renderChart

When derived is absent the block is rendered by renderChart: the ascendant, the Sun and Moon signs, the dasha line if the chart has one, and one plain line per planet with sign, degree, house, nakshatra and a retrograde flag. No lordship, no aspects, no dignity, no doshas. The comment above the function explains why, and the fix it records is worth knowing because it was a real bug in this release. Every technique renderChartFacts adds is Vedic. Handing it to a Western reading would cite Indian technique and Vedic doshas as though they were tropical, and the whole-sign house numbers it derives would disagree with chart.planets[].house, which for a Western chart is Placidus, and which is what the PDF and the iOS chart view actually render.

The gate is not in the prompt module at all. factsFor in lib/astrology/derived.ts returns undefined for any chart whose tradition is not "vedic", and returns the stored derived block, or a freshly computed one for a chart written before derived facts shipped, otherwise. The chat route calls factsFor(chart) and passes whatever comes back, so the Western branch is chosen by the chart's own tradition field and no caller can opt a Western chart into Vedic technique by mistake. The transit text follows the same rule in describeGochara: houses from the ascendant and from the Moon, and the transit afflictions, are added only when the natal chart is Vedic; a Western reading gets the plain sky, where each body is and which natal body it sits over.

The numerology branch: buildNumerologySystem

Numerology has no chart, so it has its own stable builder. buildNumerologySystem introduces Astrya as a Vedic numerologist and lists the real numbers computed from the birth date and the name: the mulank (root, from the birth day), the bhagyank (destiny, from the full date), the namank (from the full name, which is quoted in the prompt), the digits that repeat in the birth date and the digits that are missing from it (the Lo Shu grid), and whether the namank and the mulank are friends, neutral or enemies. Its Accuracy rule is stricter in one way: every number cited must match the values above exactly. Its Format rules are the same as the chart prompt's, down to the closing In simple words section.

The volatile half: buildTodaySystem

buildTodaySystem takes the day, optionally the transit text, optionally a length, and optionally the personal year and month. It writes "Today is …" with an instruction to use it for anything about today, now, or the current period; the personal cycle line when given (used by numerology); a SKY TODAY block when transits are given, followed on its own line by the instruction to name the transiting planet and the natal house or planet it touches; and the length rule, "160 words or fewer unless they ask for more" by default. The chat route sets 160 for a standard reading and 320 for a deep one; the daily readings job sets 150.

The word "today" is deliberately coarse. describeToday in lib/astrology/transits.ts renders the weekday and long date plus a part of day (morning before noon, afternoon before five, evening before nine, night after) and the zone name, in the user's own timezone. The route computes it with Luxon in profile.timezone, never the server's clock. A clock time here would change the prompt every minute, which is the first of the two things that used to break the cache.

What the route appends

The route appends one more thing to the stable string before sending it: the life timeline. describeTimelineForPrompt in lib/timeline/describe.ts renders the current dasha period (Vedic only, since Western has no dashas) and up to MAX_MOMENTS, which is 30, of the user's pinned moments newest first, each with its date at the precision it was pinned and, for Vedic, the period it fell in. It returns an empty string when there is nothing to say, so the route can append it unconditionally. It goes in the stable half on purpose, and the route's comment says why: it only changes when the user pins or removes a moment, so it belongs with the chart and not with the day.

Why byte-identical, and how it is kept so

A cached prefix is matched by exact bytes. If the stable half differed by one character between two turns, every turn would write a new cache entry and read none. So the rule is that nothing in buildChartSystem may depend on the clock, the sky, the length setting, or the order in which a JSON store returns fields. Three tests in lib/astrology/prompt.test.ts hold the line: the stable half must not contain "Today is", "SKY TODAY" or "words or fewer"; two calls with the same inputs must return the same string; and a Derived that has been through JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(…)), which is how a stored chart returns it, must render identically to a freshly computed one.

buildSystemPrompt and buildNumerologyPrompt join the two halves into one string for callers that do not cache. No route calls them today; the tests do, and they are the simplest way to see the whole prompt at once.

The blank line before the numerology sentence renderChartFacts ends whichever labelled block comes last with no trailing blank line. The numerology line therefore begins with two newlines of its own, or it would land inside HOUSES, CURRENT PERIOD or STANDING CONDITIONS and read as one more item of that block. The test named for it checks that the line before "Numerology:" is empty.

3.4Models and effort

// lib/anthropic.ts
export const READING_MODEL = "claude-sonnet-5";
export const DEEP_READING_MODEL = "claude-opus-4-8";

export function supportsAdaptiveThinking(model: string): boolean {
  return model === "claude-sonnet-5" || model === "claude-opus-4-8";
}

export const LOW_EFFORT: { readonly output_config: { readonly effort: "low" } } = {
  output_config: { effort: "low" },
};

Two models, one switch. Standard readings run on Sonnet 5, which the file's comment describes as intelligent and stable with adaptive thinking for well-reasoned interpretation. A deep reading upgrades to Opus 4.8. Both support adaptive thinking; the comment notes that Haiku 4.5 would not, which is why supportsAdaptiveThinking exists as a function rather than a constant. Every caller spreads thinking: { type: "adaptive" } into the request only when that function says yes.

LOW_EFFORT is the more consequential export. Thinking tokens are billed as output, and output is the expensive half of a reading. The comment above the constant records a measurement made on a standard Vedic reading of the same chart and question:

SettingOutput tokens
adaptive thinking, default effort1,452
adaptive thinking, effort "low"719
thinking disabled328

All three cited the same real placements. That is the finding that set the policy: routine readings run at low effort, reasoning intact and spend halved, and deep readings keep the default, because the default is what a person is asking for when they turn Deep on. The chat route applies it with one spread, ...(body.deep ? {} : LOW_EFFORT). The daily readings, the alert copy and both timeline jobs apply it unconditionally.

The type of LOW_EFFORT is written out longhand because output_config is generally available on the API but postdates the types in the installed SDK (@anthropic-ai/sdk at ^0.68.0 in package.json). The value is cast so the wire format stays exact. The same is true of "adaptive", which each caller casts through unknown to Anthropic.ThinkingConfigParam.

max_tokens is set per caller and is a ceiling, not a target. The route's comment is explicit that the length rule in the prompt is what governs how much is generated. The values are 1,600 for a standard chat reading, 3,000 for a deep one, and 1,600 for both the daily readings and the alert copy.

A stale comment in the route Above the thinking spread in app/api/chat/route.ts a comment still says that only the deep (Opus) model supports adaptive thinking and that Haiku rejects it. That was true of an earlier model pair. supportsAdaptiveThinking is the source of record, and it returns true for both current models, so standard readings think too, at low effort.

3.5Caching and the cost of a turn

The API is stateless. Every turn re-sends the system prompt and the whole retained history and is billed for all of it as input. Prompt caching lets a request declare breakpoints; the prefix up to a breakpoint that matches a recent request byte for byte is billed as a cache read at a tenth of the input rate. The chat route sets two.

The two breakpoints

The first breakpoint is on the stable system block: cache_control: { type: "ephemeral", ttl: "1h" }. The hour, per the route's comment, is so that a reply half an hour later still reads the chart from cache rather than paying full price for it again. The second is on the last stored turn of the history: the route maps the retained turns into MessageParams and gives the final one a content array with cache_control: { type: "ephemeral" }. From the second message of a conversation onward the whole prefix, chart, today and history, is a cache read.

The route's comment and docs/MODEL_COSTS.md both record the subtlety. A prefix below the API's minimum cacheable length, 1,024 tokens on Sonnet, is silently not cached. The measured stable half is 942 tokens, so on its own it may fall under the line, and the first breakpoint is then simply ignored. That is why the conversation breakpoint, not the system one, does the real work: by the second turn the prefix up to the last stored turn is well over the minimum, and it includes the system blocks.

Making the prompt hold still

Two things used to change on every request and had to stop before caching could work. The date line carried a clock time, so the prompt differed every minute; it is now the date plus a part of day, as describeToday renders it. And transits were computed for the current instant. transitChart in lib/astrology/transits.ts now computes them for midday UTC and memoises the promise in a module-level map keyed by day, tradition and the latitude and longitude rounded to whole degrees, so users in the same city share an entry. The map is cleared when it reaches MAX_ENTRIES, 200, and a failed computation is deleted from it so an error is never cached. Midday is the sample point because the Moon is the only body that moves enough within a day to matter, and sampling the middle halves the worst-case drift. The memo also stops the ephemeris running on every message, which was the lesser of its two purposes.

The history budget

// lib/data/history.ts
export const HISTORY_BUDGET_CHARS = 6000;

export function selectHistory(turns: Turn[], budget: number = HISTORY_BUDGET_CHARS): Turn[] {
  const kept: Turn[] = [];
  let used = 0;
  for (let i = turns.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    const turn = turns[i];
    const cost = turn.content.length;
    if (used + cost > budget && kept.length > 0) break;
    kept.unshift(turn);
    used += cost;
  }
  while (kept.length > 0 && kept[0].role === "assistant") kept.shift();
  return kept;
}

The route loads every stored message of the conversation with getMessages in lib/data/chat.ts, ordered by created_at ascending, and hands them to selectHistory. The budget is in characters, roughly four to a token, so 6,000 characters is about 1,500 tokens. It walks newest first, because the most recent exchange matters most for coherence, and stops when the next turn would overflow, except that the newest turn is always kept even if it alone exceeds the budget. It then drops any leading assistant turns, because the API requires the first message to be from the user, and a reply with no question above it reads as a non sequitur anyway. The old rule was a flat last ten turns; the budget replaced it so that one long reading cannot crowd out the recent exchange, and so a conversation that runs all evening does not keep getting more expensive.

The measured numbers

Every figure below is reproduced from docs/MODEL_COSTS.md, which states that each one was measured against the real API on a real chart asking a real question, not estimated. "Before" is the pipeline before the four changes that document describes: caching, effort, the history budget and the explicit length rule. "After" is the pipeline this chapter describes.

MeasureBeforeAfter
System prompt1,608 tokens942
System plus a 10-turn history4,017 tokens2,868 (8 turns kept)
Input billed from turn 2 on4,017 at full rate2,867 as cache read (0.1 times)
Output on a standard reading1,452 tokens719
Length of the reading itself167 words169

At the Sonnet 5 prices the document quotes, $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output, a mid-conversation turn went from roughly $0.023 to $0.008, and the reading did not get shorter. The document also records two figures that did not become defaults: disabling thinking entirely on the standard path would take output to about 328 tokens, a quarter of the original, and was not chosen because chart work benefits from some reasoning; and the alerts job, which runs once per user per day on Opus, had its max_tokens cut from 4,096 to 1,600 alongside the move to low effort.

Watching it in production

After the stream ends the route awaits finalMessage() and logs one line per turn: 

chat usage conv=<id> model=claude-sonnet-5 in=13 cache_write=0 cache_read=2867 out=538

The fields are the API's input_tokens, cache_creation_input_tokens, cache_read_input_tokens and output_tokens. The diagnostic rule from the cost document: cache_read staying at 0 across a conversation means something reintroduced a per-request change into the prefix, and the date line and the transit text are the first two places to look.

The cheapest turn never reaches the API

docs/MODEL_COSTS.md ends with the one saving this chapter does not cover. On iOS 26 with Apple Intelligence, questions that are lookups rather than interpretation ("where is my Saturn", "which dasha am I in") are answered by the on-device model from the cached chart, and never become a paid turn at all. The saving is in the turn count, not the per-turn price. The router, the tool it reads through, and the vocabulary rules it must obey are in chapter 10.

3.6Guarding the prompt

There is no filter in this repository that inspects a user's message for instructions aimed at the model. The guard is structural, and it is worth being precise about what it consists of.

User text goes in messages, never in system. The route reads body.message, appends it to the messages array and stores it; it is never interpolated into either system string. Two kinds of user-authored text do reach the stable half, and both are the user's own data reaching only the user's own prompt: the first name (and, for numerology, the full name, which is quoted as the source of the namank), and the titles of the moments the user has pinned on their timeline, which describeTimelineForPrompt renders verbatim.

The system prompt contains only computed facts and fixed rules. The chart block is rendered from numbers the ephemeris produced. The Accuracy rules tell the model that those numbers are the only facts it has and that a sentence with no placement behind it does not go in the answer. This is what makes an invented placement unlikely: the model has a complete, labelled list, and an instruction to cite from it.

Identity is checked twice. The route calls supabase.auth.getUser() and returns 401 without a user. getOrCreateConversation in lib/data/chat.ts, when handed an existing conversation id, verifies that the row belongs to this user_id before reusing it and throws "Conversation not found" otherwise; its comment calls this defence in depth on top of RLS. The tables themselves carry owner-only policies, described in chapter 6. So a user cannot append to, or read history from, a conversation that is not theirs, and the history that becomes part of the prompt is always their own.

What lib/data/injection.test.ts actually tests

Despite its name, this file has nothing to do with prompt injection. It tests dependency injection: that the data helpers accept an injected Supabase client and use it, and fall back to the cookie client only when none is given. Its three cases are that saveBirthProfile writes through the injected client, that appendMessage writes through the injected client with exactly the row shape the messages table expects, and that appendMessage with no client calls createServerSupabase once.

Why that matters to readings is the bearer path. A web request carries Supabase cookies, and createServerSupabase in lib/supabase/server.ts builds a client from them. An iOS request carries Authorization: Bearer <jwt> and no cookies. createRouteSupabase in lib/supabase/route.ts reads that header and returns a plain supabase-js client that forwards the token, so Row Level Security evaluates as that user. Every helper in lib/data/chat.ts and lib/data/birthProfile.ts takes an optional db?: Db for this reason. If a helper ignored it and built its own cookie client, an iOS turn would run as nobody: the conversation insert would fail RLS and the reading would never be stored. The test pins that createServerSupabase is not called when a client is injected, which is the whole contract.

Name versus contents If you came to injection.test.ts looking for prompt injection guards, the honest answer is that the guards are the four structural facts above, and the tests that hold them are in prompt.test.ts (the rules text is present) and history.test.ts (the history is well formed). The chat route itself has no test file.

3.7Tests

All are Vitest, run with npm test, and none touches the network. The prompt tests build from a fixture chart (Leo ascendant, Sun in Sagittarius, Moon in Taurus, Venus in Libra, Venus mahadasha, Sun antardasha) and compute its derived facts once so every assertion reads the same numbers the prompt was rendered from.

  • lib/astrology/prompt.test.ts, buildSystemPrompt: the joined prompt carries the name, the tradition, the ascendant, a nakshatra and the dasha lord; it contains "never state a placement", "only this data", "guidance and reflection" and "in simple words"; the stable half carries no "Today is", no "SKY TODAY" and no length rule, while the volatile half carries all three; and two calls with the same inputs return the same string.
  • buildTodaySystem transit framing: with a multi-line transit block the instruction lands on its own line, not glued to a bullet, and there is no double period.
  • derived facts in the prompt: pinned to the fixture's computed values rather than the shape of a sentence. The Sun's line says what it rules; the Moon at 3.1° Taurus is exalted; Venus in house 3 "aspects house 9", singular; a Mars in Aries lagna "aspects houses 4, 7, 8"; house 10's line names Venus in house 3 in Libra as its lord; the mahadasha line says where Venus sits; the generic register ban ("true of a twelfth of the population") is present; and a lord that is not a body in the chart is said to be so rather than placed in house 0.
  • a Western chart gets no derived facts: the C1 regression. With a Leo ascendant and Mars stored in Placidus house 5, whole-sign counting would put it in house 6; the test asserts the prompt reads "Mars: Capricorn 2°, h5", contains no moolatrikona, no "aspects house" and no STANDING CONDITIONS, through both buildChartSystem with no derived and buildSystemPrompt's default.
  • numerology stays its own block: the blank line before "Numerology: Mulank 3, Bhagyank 7."
  • derived facts survive a JSON round trip: fresh and round-tripped Derived render identically.
  • numerology prompt: for the birth date 1990-07-15 the repeated digits are 1 and 9, the missing digits 2, 3, 4, 6 and 8, and "The namank 1 and the mulank 6 are enemies." appears as one literal sentence, so a swapped argument order or a wrong relationship word fails.
  • buildTodaySystem with personal cycles: distinct year and month values both render, and neither appears when absent.
  • lib/data/history.test.ts: everything is kept when it fits; the oldest turns drop first; a window never starts on an assistant turn (and can be empty as a result); the newest turn is kept even when it alone exceeds the budget; an empty conversation yields nothing.
  • lib/data/injection.test.ts: the three dependency injection cases in section 3.6, with computeChart stubbed so the ephemeris binary is not loaded.

3.8Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/astrology/prompt.ts, top to bottom. Read renderChart's comment before renderChartFacts, so the Western branch makes sense as the older, plainer one.
  2. lib/astrology/derived.ts, just factsFor and the comment above it, for the gate that chooses the branch.
  3. lib/astrology/transits.ts, for the memo key and describeToday, which are the two things that made the prompt hold still.
  4. lib/anthropic.ts, for the three measured effort numbers.
  5. app/api/chat/route.ts, from the profile read to the messages.stream call, watching for the two cache_control objects.
  6. lib/data/history.ts, for the budget.
  7. docs/MODEL_COSTS.md, for what each change bought.
  8. lib/astrology/prompt.test.ts, which reads as the list of rules in plain English.

Check yourself

Why is the timeline appended to the stable half rather than the today half, when it is user data that can change?

Because it changes only when the user pins or removes a moment, which is rare, while the today half changes several times a day. Putting it in the stable half means a pin costs one cache write; putting it in the today half would gain nothing, since the today half is not behind its own breakpoint.

The stable half measures 942 tokens and Sonnet's minimum cacheable prefix is 1,024. Why does caching work at all?

Because of the second breakpoint on the last stored turn. From turn 2 the prefix up to that turn includes both system blocks plus history, is well over the minimum, and is what is read from cache. The first breakpoint is a bonus when the chart is large enough.

Why does a Western reading get no lordships, aspects or doshas?

factsFor returns undefined for any non-Vedic chart, so buildChartSystem falls back to renderChart. The techniques are Vedic, and whole-sign house numbers would contradict the Placidus houses the PDF and the iOS view draw from chart.planets[].house.

Why is the length rule in the prompt rather than in max_tokens?

Because max_tokens truncates; it does not shorten. A model told to write 160 words writes about 160 and stops cleanly. The ceiling of 1,600 exists so a runaway answer is bounded, not to shape a normal one.

What does selectHistory do with a stored assistant turn that has no user turn before it in the window?

Drops it. The window is trimmed from the front until the first turn is a user turn, because the API requires that and because an answer with no question reads as a non sequitur. The test named "never starts on an assistant turn" pins it, including the case where the result is empty.

What does injection.test.ts guard against?

A data helper that ignores the injected bearer client and builds its own cookie client. On an iOS request that client has no session, RLS denies the write, and the reading is never stored.

Chapter four

4Identity and access

One Supabase project, two ways in, and a single rule under both: the database decides who may read a row, not the code that asked for it. The web app carries its session in cookies, the iOS app carries a bearer token, and every table refuses anyone but its owner.

This chapter describes identity and access as they are on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. Nothing here is a framework of its own. Supabase Auth issues the sessions, Postgres Row Level Security enforces ownership, and the application code is a thin set of clients that make sure a request reaches Postgres as the person who sent it. The interesting decisions are about where each key is allowed to be, which paths bypass the rule and why, and how the phone gets a session without ever leaving the app.

4.1Two channels

A browser and a phone reach the same Postgres through different doors. On the web, the session lives in cookies that @supabase/ssr reads and refreshes; a server component, a server action, a route handler and the middleware all build a client from those cookies and Postgres sees the request as that user. On iOS, supabase-swift holds the session in the Keychain and the app does two things with it: it reads a few tables directly over PostgREST, and it calls the same /api/* route handlers the web uses, sending the access token as Authorization: Bearer. A route handler builds a client that forwards that header, and again Postgres sees the request as that user.

The design follows from one sentence in the README: every table is protected by Row Level Security, so the anon key is safe to ship in the iOS binary. The anon key and the project URL are public by design. What must never ship is SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY, which bypasses RLS, and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. Both are read only through lib/env.ts, which begins with import "server-only", and the script that generates the iOS configuration (mobile/scripts/make-config.sh) reads only the two public values and says so in its header.

4.2Files

lib/supabase/
├── client.ts          createBrowserClient. Present, imported by nothing today
├── server.ts          createServerSupabase: the cookie client for server code
├── middleware.ts      updateSession: refresh cookies, redirect the signed-out

4.3Session on the web

createServerSupabase in lib/supabase/server.ts wraps createServerClient from @supabase/ssr with the Next.js cookie store. Its setAll is wrapped in a try block with a comment that explains the whole arrangement: when the client is created from a Server Component, cookies cannot be written, so the write is ignored and the middleware is what refreshes them. That is why the root middleware.ts exists at all. 

// lib/supabase/middleware.ts
const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();

const path = request.nextUrl.pathname;
const isProtectedApp = path.startsWith("/app");
const isProtectedAdmin = path.startsWith("/admin") && path !== "/admin/login";
if ((isProtectedApp || isProtectedAdmin) && !user) {
  const url = request.nextUrl.clone();
  url.pathname = isProtectedAdmin ? "/admin/login" : "/login";
  return NextResponse.redirect(url);
}
return response;

updateSession does two jobs on every request. It calls getUser, which verifies the token with Supabase Auth rather than trusting the cookie's contents, and in doing so refreshes an expiring session and writes the new cookies onto the response. Then it redirects anyone signed out away from /app and /admin, sending the two areas to their own login pages. The matcher in the root file excludes _next/static, _next/image, favicon.ico, images/ and .well-known/, so static assets and the Apple association file are served without a session lookup.

One form, two outcomes

The login and signup pages render the same AuthForm with the same authenticate action; only the title differs. The action is the web's "smart" sign-in, and its comment states the intent: an existing account with the right password logs in, a new email creates an account, and an existing email with the wrong password is told so.

  1. Try signInWithPassword. A session means a returning user; redirect to /app.
  2. If the error message contains "not confirmed", the account exists but the address was never confirmed; redirect to /check-email with the address in the query string.
  3. Otherwise call signUp with emailRedirectTo set to <origin>/auth/callback. If Supabase answers that the address is already registered, the first step failed because of the password, and the form shows "Incorrect password for this account. Try again, or use a magic link."
  4. A new account with a session (email confirmation switched off in the project) goes to /app; without one it goes to /check-email.

The order matters. Trying sign-in first makes the common case one round trip, and using the sign-up error to distinguish "wrong password" from "new person" means the form never has to ask which the visitor is. The cost is that the message strings from Supabase are matched by substring ("already registered", "already exists", "already been registered"), which is fragile by nature and is the reason the iOS app chose a different shape (4.4).

sendMagicLink calls signInWithOtp with the same redirect and bounces back to /login?sent=1. resendConfirmation calls auth.resend with type signup. signOut signs out and redirects to /login; the form that calls it lives in components/AppShell.tsx at the bottom of the sidebar.

The callback

// app/auth/callback/route.ts
const code = searchParams.get("code");
if (code) {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  await supabase.auth.exchangeCodeForSession(code);
}
return NextResponse.redirect(`${origin}/app`);

Every link Supabase sends (confirmation, magic link, the admin's one-time link) resolves to this route with a code. The route exchanges it for a session, which writes the cookies, and redirects to /app unconditionally. If the code was missing or the exchange failed, the redirect still happens and the middleware bounces the visitor to /login. There is no error page for a bad link on the web; the login page is the error page.

lib/supabase/client.ts exports a createBrowserClient wrapper for client components. No file imports it today. Every read on the web goes through server components and server actions, so the browser never holds a Supabase client. Keep that in mind before reaching for it: adding a browser client would mean the anon key and the session start being used from JavaScript, which is safe under RLS but is a second channel to reason about.

4.4Session on the phone

Supa.client in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Supabase.swift is the app's single SupabaseClient, built from the generated AppConfig. The file's comment records the requirement that made supabase-swift the right choice: it persists the session in the Keychain by default, never in UserDefaults. Supa.accessToken() returns the current access token or nil, and that is the whole bridge to the API. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift
private static func request(_ path: String, method: String) async throws -> URLRequest {
    var req = URLRequest(url: AppConfig.apiBaseURL.appendingPathComponent(path))
    req.httpMethod = method
    guard let token = await Supa.accessToken() else { throw APIError.unauthorized }
    req.setValue("Bearer \(token)", forHTTPHeaderField: "Authorization")
    return req
}

Every call in SancharaAPI goes through this function except geocode, whose comment says why: the route is public, so it carries no token. A 401 from any route maps to APIError.unauthorized, whose description is "Your session expired. Please sign in again."

The bearer channel on the server

// lib/supabase/route.ts
export async function createRouteSupabase(request: Request): Promise<Db> {
  const token = bearerTokenFrom(request);
  if (!token) return createServerSupabase();

  return createClient(env.supabaseUrl(), env.supabaseAnonKey(), {
    global: { headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } },
    auth: { persistSession: false, autoRefreshToken: false },
  });
}

This is the file that lets one set of route handlers serve both clients. bearerTokenFrom reads the Authorization header, accepts the scheme case-insensitively, and returns null for a missing header, a different scheme, or an empty token. With a token, the handler gets a plain supabase-js client that forwards the header on every request, so RLS evaluates as that user; the client neither persists nor refreshes anything, because the phone owns the session. Without a token, the handler falls through to the cookie client and the web behaves as it always did. The exported Db type is SupabaseClient, and the comment notes that both clients are instances of it, which is what lets lib/data/birthProfile.ts accept either.

Nine route handlers use createRouteSupabase: account, chat, devices, kundli, life-events, profile, timeline, timeline/explain and timeline/scan. Each begins the same way, getUser then a 401 when there is no user, and the user id used afterwards always comes from that verified session, never from the body.

Direct reads

Not everything on the phone goes through Vercel. ProfileStore reads birth_profiles straight from Supabase with Supa.client.from(...), and the alerts and daily-reading stores do the same for their tables. There is no user id in those queries. The birth self policy already restricts the result to the caller's own row, so the query is simply "select from birth_profiles where chart is not null, limit 1". That is the practical meaning of RLS as the boundary: the client does not filter by owner because it cannot see anyone else's rows to begin with.

The state machine

AuthStore is an @Observable class on the main actor with four states: loading, signedOut, signedIn, and awaitingCode(email:purpose:). start() sets the initial state from whether a session exists, then listens to authStateChanges: signedIn and tokenRefreshed with a session mean signed in, signedOut means signed out. RootView switches on the state and shows LoadingScreen, AuthView, VerifyCodeView or SignedInView.

The store's header comment explains the one deliberate divergence from the web. Sign-in and sign-up are separate paths: "a person who mistypes a password should be told that, not silently pushed into creating a second account." AuthView has a two-segment picker, and a device that has never signed in opens on "Create account" (AuthStore.hasSignedInBefore, a UserDefaults flag under a key that keeps the pre-rename prefix so that returning users are not shown the first-run screen). signIn maps "not confirmed" to awaitingCode with purpose confirmSignUp; signUp maps "already registered" to an error and sets shouldSwitchToSignIn, which the view watches to flip the picker with the email still filled in. Both validate the address and the password length (minimumPasswordLength = 6, Supabase's default) before spending a round trip.

signOut does three things in a fixed order: unregister the push token while the bearer token is still valid, sign out, then ChartCache.shared.clear(). The comment says why the third step exists: the chart, the birth details and the last reading live on disk in the App Group, and without clearing them the widget would keep showing them to whoever signs in next.

4.5Email code

On the phone, the passwordless path sends a six-digit code rather than a link. docs/EMAIL_OTP.md gives the reason and the one setting it depends on. The reason: a code is typed into the app that is already open, with no hop out to a mail client and no Universal Link that a browser might swallow. The setting: Supabase decides link-versus-code by what the email template contains. A template with only {{ .ConfirmationURL }} sends a link and no code, and the app's code screen would have nothing to accept. Both the "Magic Link" and "Confirm signup" templates therefore include {{ .Token }} and keep the link as well, so the same email serves the phone (type the code) and the web (tap the link).

Three details in AuthStore decide how this feels in practice.

  • Which code was sent decides how it is verified. CodePurpose has two cases. signIn verifies with EmailOTPType.email; confirmSignUp, reached when a password sign-up needs its address confirmed, verifies with .signup. resendCode re-sends through the matching path: signInWithOTP for the first, auth.resend(type: .signup) for the second.
  • Digits only. normalizedCode keeps the numeric characters and takes the first six, because people paste codes with spaces and mail clients wrap them in punctuation. VerifyCodeView applies it on every change, and once six digits are present it submits on its own; the comment calls a separate button "ceremony". The field is marked .textContentType(.oneTimeCode), so iOS offers the code from the email above the keyboard.
  • The link still works. sendEmailCode passes redirectTo pointing at auth/callback on the API host, so anyone who taps the link instead of typing the code lands back in the app through a Universal Link (4.6).

A failed verification whose message contains "expired" or "invalid" is reported as "That code didn't work. It may have expired, send a new one." Codes expire after an hour by default. The screen also offers "Use a different email", which simply calls signOut to return to signedOut.

The web has no code screen. Its magic-link button calls the same signInWithOtp and so receives the same email, but the only thing it can do with it is follow the link to /auth/callback. The code in that email is for the phone.

4.6Sign in with Apple and Universal Links

Apple

Sign in with Apple is fully native. Apple returns an identity token and Supabase verifies it directly through signInWithIdToken; there is no web sheet, no redirect, no password and no confirmation email. The header of AppleSignIn.swift calls it the fastest way onto Astrya, and docs/APPLE_SIGN_IN.md records the two one-time steps it needs: the Apple provider enabled in the Supabase project with the bundle id com.shivvyas.astra as a client id, and the Sign In with Apple capability on the App ID. The entitlement com.apple.developer.applesignin is already in mobile/project.yml. For the native flow no Services ID, secret key or key id is needed; those exist only for Apple's web OAuth, which the site does not offer. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Auth/AppleSignIn.swift
SignInWithAppleButton(.continue) { request in
    let nonce = AppleSignIn.randomNonce()
    rawNonce = nonce
    request.requestedScopes = [.fullName, .email]
    request.nonce = AppleSignIn.sha256(nonce)
} onCompletion: { result in
    let nonce = rawNonce
    Task { await auth.completeAppleSignIn(result: result, rawNonce: nonce) }
}

The nonce is the part worth understanding. randomNonce draws 32 characters from a 64-character alphabet using SecRandomCopyBytes, rejecting any byte of 252 or above so the modulo does not bias the distribution. Its SHA-256 goes to Apple, which signs it into the identity token; the raw value goes to Supabase, which hashes it and compares. The comment states what this buys: a captured token cannot be replayed against the project, because the replayer does not hold the raw nonce that produced its hash.

completeAppleSignIn treats a cancelled sheet (ASAuthorizationError.canceled) as a normal action and reports nothing. On success it extracts the identity token, calls signInWithIdToken(credentials: .init(provider: .apple, idToken:, nonce:)), and then keeps credential.fullName if Apple sent one. Apple sends the name only on the very first authorization for this App ID, ever, so AppleSignIn.remember stores it in UserDefaults and the intake screen (chapter 5) pre-fills the first and last name from it. The keys keep their pre-rename prefix on purpose: the bundle id did not change, existing installs upgrade in place, and a renamed key would lose a name Apple will never send again.

Known gap Apple's guideline 5.1.1(v) asks apps that offer Sign in with Apple to revoke the Apple token when a person deletes their account. DELETE /api/account deletes the Supabase user and everything that cascades from it, but it does not call Apple's revoke endpoint. docs/APPLE_SIGN_IN.md lists the three steps to close this (a Services ID and key to mint a client secret, capturing the authorization code at sign-in, and posting the refresh token to the revoke endpoint before the Supabase user is removed) and says it is worth doing before the first App Store submission that includes Apple sign-in. As of 0.3.0 it is open.

Universal Links

Every email Supabase sends carries a link that resolves to /auth/callback on the production host. On a phone with the app installed, that URL should open the app, not Safari. Two files make that happen. The entitlements in mobile/project.yml declare applinks:astra.shivvyas.com and webcredentials:astra.shivvyas.com. And the site serves public/.well-known/apple-app-site-association, which names the app as the team id joined to com.shivvyas.astra, and claims exactly two paths: /auth/callback and /auth/callback?*. The webcredentials entry is what lets the iOS password autofill offer credentials saved for the site to the app.

When iOS hands the URL to the app, RootView.onOpenURL receives it. Two kinds of URL arrive there, and the comment is explicit: Supabase's auth callback and the app's own sanchara:// scheme from a widget tap. DeepLink.isDeepLink checks the scheme; a deep link goes to DeepLink.shared.handle, anything else goes to AuthStore.handle(url:), which calls Supa.client.auth.session(from: url) and moves to signedIn. Handing a widget URL to the auth store would produce "That link has expired", which is the failure the check prevents. Without the app installed, the same URL reaches the web route in 4.3 and the person is signed in on the web instead.

4.7Row Level Security

RLS is the boundary. The clients above exist only to make sure a request reaches Postgres with the right identity; what that identity may then see is decided entirely by the policies in supabase/migrations/. Every table has enable row level security, and every policy is written in terms of auth.uid(). Here is all of them.

TablePolicyCommandsRuleMigration
profilesprofiles selfallid = auth.uid()0001
birth_profilesbirth selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0001
conversationsconv selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0001
messagesmsg selfalla conversations row exists with this conversation_id and user_id = auth.uid()0001
adminsadmins read self-or-adminselect onlyuser_id = auth.uid() or public.is_admin()0001
device_tokensdevice tokens selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0004
transit_conditionsconditions self readselect onlyuser_id = auth.uid()0004
alertsalerts self read; alerts self updateselect; updateuser_id = auth.uid()0004
daily_readingsdaily readings self read; daily readings self updateselect; updateuser_id = auth.uid()0005
life_eventslife events selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0006
life_event_scanslife event scans selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0006
period_readingsperiod readings selfalluser_id = auth.uid()0007

Every for all policy carries both using and with check with the same predicate, so a user can neither read another person's row nor insert a row that claims another owner. The three tables with select-only or select-and-update policies are the ones the scheduled jobs write: transit_conditions, alerts and daily_readings. The comments in 0004 and 0005 say it directly: the daily job writes them with the service-role client, and the policies exist so the app can read its own rows and mark them read. A user cannot insert an alert. admins has no insert, update or delete policy at all; the comment on the table reads "presence in this table = admin. No self-service path". The only way in is the SQL statement in docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md, run by hand.

Two functions complete the picture. public.is_admin() is security definer and checks for the caller's row in admins; it is used by the admins policy so an admin can see the whole list. public.handle_new_user() runs on after insert on auth.users and creates the profiles row, taking a display name from the sign-up metadata or the part of the email before the @. Every table with a user_id references auth.users(id) on delete cascade, which is what makes account deletion one call (chapter 11).

Where the service-role key is allowed

// lib/supabase/admin.ts
import "server-only";
// Bypasses RLS. Only ever import from server code behind an admin auth check.
export function createAdminSupabase() {
  return createClient(env.supabaseUrl(), env.supabaseServiceRoleKey(), {
    auth: { persistSession: false, autoRefreshToken: false },
  });
}

import "server-only" makes the bundler fail if a client component ever imports this file, so the key cannot leak into a browser bundle by accident. Beyond that the rule is convention, and the convention is narrow. These are the only production files that call createAdminSupabase, and each has a reason RLS cannot serve.

FileUses the service role toWhy RLS cannot do it
lib/admin/guard.tslook up the caller in adminsThe guard runs before any admin session is proven; reading through the caller's own client would work for their own row but the guard is also the place that must never be wrong
lib/admin/data.tslist users, read birth profiles and transcriptsAdmin reads cross owners by definition; the 0001 comment says admin reads happen via the service-role client
app/admin/actions.tsauth.admin.generateLink for a one-time login linkMinting a link for another user is an Auth admin API
app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts, app/api/profile/route.tsupload the avatar to the avatars bucketNo storage policy is defined for users; the upload path is <user id>/avatar.<ext> and the user id comes from the verified session
app/api/account/route.tsauth.admin.deleteUserDeleting an auth user is an Auth admin API; the id is the caller's own
app/api/devices/route.tsupsert and delete device_tokensThe comment explains: a device handed from one account to another must change owner, and the owner-only policy would correctly refuse that update
lib/alerts/run.ts, lib/alerts/predictions.tswrite conditions, alerts and daily readings for every userThe cron job has no user session; it is authenticated by CRON_SECRET instead

Note what every entry has in common: the row being written is still scoped to a user id that came from getUser on the caller's own client, or the code is a scheduled job with no caller at all. The admin client is never used to act on a user id taken from a request body.

4.8The admin boundary

The admin area at /admin has its own login page, its own sign-in action, and a guard on every page. Being an admin is not a role or a claim; it is a row in admins. Three layers check it.

  1. The middleware redirects any signed-out request under /admin, except /admin/login itself, to /admin/login. This proves a session exists, not that it is an admin's.
  2. adminSignIn in app/admin/actions.ts calls signInWithPassword with the cookie client, then isAdminUser. A valid account that is not in admins is signed out again immediately and sent back with "Not authorized". There is no sign-up on this form, no magic link, and no way to become an admin from it.
  3. requireAdmin in lib/admin/guard.ts is the first line of every admin page and every admin action. It reads the user from the cookie client, redirects to /admin/login if there is none, and redirects with "Not authorized" if isAdminUser is false. The layering means a person removed from admins while signed in loses access on their next request, not at their next login.
// lib/admin/guard.ts
export async function requireAdmin() {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) redirect("/admin/login");
  if (!(await isAdminUser(user.id))) redirect("/admin/login?error=Not+authorized");
  return user;
}

The admin session is an ordinary Supabase session in the same cookies as the app's. What separates the two areas is the guard, the separate login page, and the layout: app/admin/layout.tsx is a passthrough with force-dynamic, and each authenticated page wraps itself in AdminShell so the sidebar never appears on the login screen. What an admin can read and do once inside is chapter 11's subject; the short version is that the admin reads users, birth details and transcripts, can send a password reset and mint a one-time login link, and writes nothing to a user's data.

Two things to keep straight The web's user login accepts a magic link; the admin login does not. And is_admin() in SQL and isAdminUser in TypeScript answer the same question by different routes: the SQL function runs inside a policy as security definer, the TypeScript function runs on the server with the service-role client. Change one without the other and the admins list becomes visible to the wrong people or the guard starts lying.

4.9Tests

Identity is the part of a system where a test is cheapest to write and most expensive to lack, and the coverage here is narrow but exact. Nothing in it needs a network or a Supabase project.

  • lib/supabase/route.test.ts pins bearerTokenFrom with five cases: a token is extracted from a Bearer header; a lowercase scheme is accepted; a missing header returns null; a Basic scheme returns null; a Bearer scheme with an empty token returns null. The last two are what keep a malformed header from falling through to the cookie client with a garbage token attached.
  • mobile/Sanchara/Tests/AuthValidationTests.swift covers the pure functions on AuthStore and AppleSignIn. testAcceptsOrdinaryAddresses and testRejectsMalformedAddresses pin emailLooksValid (no leading @, a dot in the domain, no leading or trailing dot, no spaces). testCodeNormalisationKeepsOnlyDigits pins normalizedCode against "123 456", "Code: 123-456.", a ten-digit paste that must be cut to six, and letters. testAppleNonceIsRandomAndHashed pins the 32-character nonce, that two nonces differ, and that the SHA-256 hex is 64 characters and stable.
  • The route handler tests for account, devices and profile each begin with a case that a request with no user gets a 401 and touches nothing, which is the bearer channel's contract exercised from the other side. Chapter 11 lists them.

What is not tested: the middleware redirect, the smart sign-in's error-string matching, the callback route, and requireAdmin. All four depend on a live Auth server or on Next.js request plumbing, and none has a test today. The error-string matching in app/(auth)/actions.ts is the one most likely to break silently if Supabase rewords a message, and would be the first to cover.

4.10Study notes

Read in this order

  1. supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql, for the tables, the trigger, is_admin() and the first five policies. Everything else in this chapter exists to satisfy these.
  2. lib/supabase/route.ts, forty lines, and its test. This is the whole bearer channel.
  3. lib/supabase/middleware.ts and the root middleware.ts, for cookie refresh and the two redirects.
  4. app/(auth)/actions.ts, for the smart form, then app/auth/callback/route.ts.
  5. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Auth/AuthStore.swift, top to bottom. The comments carry the reasoning.
  6. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Auth/AppleSignIn.swift, for the nonce and the remembered name.
  7. docs/EMAIL_OTP.md, for the template setting that decides link or code.
  8. lib/supabase/admin.ts and lib/admin/guard.ts, then grep for createAdminSupabase and check each caller against the table in 4.7.

Check yourself

Why can the anon key ship inside the iOS binary?

Because it grants nothing by itself. Every table has RLS enabled and every policy is written against auth.uid(), so a request with the anon key and no session sees no rows, and a request with a session sees only its owner's rows.

What does createRouteSupabase do when the header is Authorization: Bearer with nothing after it?

bearerTokenFrom returns null for an empty token, so the handler falls through to the cookie client. On a phone that means getUser finds no user and the route answers 401.

Why does the iOS app separate sign-in from sign-up when the web merges them?

The web's merged form tells a wrong password from a new address by matching Supabase's error strings after a sign-up attempt. On the phone the store's comment says a person who mistypes a password should be told that, not pushed toward a second account, so the screen picks the path and the store never guesses.

Which tables can a user read but never insert into, and who inserts?

transit_conditions, alerts and daily_readings. The scheduled jobs in lib/alerts/ write them with the service-role client; the user can only select, and update read_at on the last two.

Why does randomNonce reject bytes of 252 and above?

The alphabet has 64 characters and 256 is a multiple of 64, but the loop discards bytes at or above 252 so that a future change to the alphabet size cannot bias the modulo; the comment says every character must be equally likely.

Why does RootView.onOpenURL check the URL scheme before handing it to the auth store?

A widget tap opens the app with a sanchara:// URL. Passing that to session(from:) would fail and show "That link has expired" for something that was never a link.

How does someone become an admin?

Only by a row inserted into public.admins by hand in the SQL editor. The table has a select policy and no write policy, and no code path inserts into it.

Part two

The features

One chapter per feature. Each follows the same shape: intent, files, model, the server side, the web and iOS clients, how it works end to end, what keeps it fast and cheap, what breaks and how it is handled, and how to study it.

  • 5. Intake and the birth profile
  • 6. Chat
  • 7. Kundli
  • 8. The life timeline
  • 9. Daily readings and dosha alerts
  • 10. On-device answers, Siri, and widgets
  • 11. Profile, account, and admin
  • 12. The landing page and the web shell

Chapter five

5Intake and the birth profile

Everything Astrya does starts from one row: a name, a date, a time, a place and the two charts computed from them. Intake is the form that fills it, on the web and on the phone, and the birth profile is the row that every other feature reads. The one idea that shaped it is that the chart is computed once, at save time, and stored beside the details that produced it.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. A person who signs in has, at first, nothing but an auth user. The web app and the iOS app both notice that and send them to intake. When they submit, the server geocodes nothing and guesses nothing: the form has already resolved a place to coordinates and an IANA timezone, and the route hands those to the Swiss Ephemeris, computes a Vedic and a Western chart, and writes all of it into birth_profiles in one upsert. From then on the chat, the kundli, the timeline, the daily readings and the widget all read that stored chart. Nothing recomputes it unless the details change, or the chart's schema falls behind.

5.1Intent

A birth chart is only as good as the moment and place it is cast for. The intake form therefore refuses to submit until it holds a real latitude, longitude and timezone, and it gets them from a geocoder rather than from the person. Typing "Mumbai" produces a list of candidates with their timezones already attached, and picking one fills four hidden fields. Someone whose village the geocoder does not know can type coordinates by hand, and the timezone is still derived for them, offline, from the coordinates.

The second intent is that the computed chart belongs with the details. birth_profiles carries a chart jsonb column holding both traditions. Readers never call the ephemeris; they read the column. This is what lets the iOS app cache the chart in the App Group and answer Siri, App Intents and the widget without a network, and it is what makes a chart upgrade a read-time concern rather than a migration.

The third intent is that the two clients do the same work through the same function. The web form submits to a server action, the iOS form posts multipart form data to a route handler, and both end in saveBirthProfile. The route exists only because a native client cannot invoke a Next.js server action; its own comment says so.

5.2Files and folders

app/api/profile/route.ts              POST: the native intake door, returns JSON
app/api/profile/route.test.ts         401, 200, missing field, bad number
app/api/geocode/route.ts              GET ?q=: birthplace search, no auth
app/(app)/app/intake/page.tsx         the web intake screen
app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts       saveIntake, the server action
app/(app)/app/profile/page.tsx        the same form, prefilled, for editing
app/(app)/app/page.tsx                home: "Enter birth details" when there is no row
components/IntakeForm.tsx             the form, TimePicker, ManualCoords
components/PlaceAutocomplete.tsx      typeahead over /api/geocode
lib/geo.ts                            geocodePlace, resolveTimezone
lib/geo.test.ts                       two coordinates, two timezones
lib/data/birthProfile.ts              getBirthProfile, saveBirthProfile, ensureCurrentChart
lib/data/birthProfile.test.ts         the upgrade path and the read failure

supabase/migrations/0002_avatar.sql   avatar_url
