app/smoke.test.ts                     the app layout module loads

Everything that decides something is on the server. The two clients share no code and are written in two languages, but they are the same program: the iOS store's doc comment says it mirrors components/Chat.tsx, the intro prompt is one string copied verbatim, and the mode dot colours in ChatModels.swift are the hex values from the web component. When the two disagree, the web is the reference and the iOS file says so.

6.3Model

Two tables

-- supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql
create table public.conversations (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  user_id uuid not null references auth.users(id) on delete cascade,
  tradition text not null check (tradition in ('vedic','western')),
  title text,
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);
create index on public.conversations (user_id, created_at desc);

create table public.messages (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  conversation_id uuid not null references public.conversations(id) on delete cascade,
  role text not null check (role in ('user','assistant')),
  content text not null,
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);
create index on public.messages (conversation_id, created_at);

A conversation is an owner, a mode and a title; a message is a role and a body. There is no updated_at, no token count, no model name and no deep flag on either row. The transcript is the only thing stored, which is what lets any client, and the timeline's moment extraction, read it back without knowing how it was produced. Migration 0003_numerology_mode.sql drops and re-adds the tradition check so it accepts 'numerology'; the column is still called tradition although the TypeScript type that fills it is ChatMode. The two indexes are exactly the two queries: a user's conversations newest first, and a conversation's messages oldest first.

Row Level Security is owner-only on both. "conv self" checks user_id = auth.uid() for every operation. "msg self" has no owner column to check, so it joins: a message is visible or writable only if a conversation row with that id and the caller's user_id exists. Deleting a user cascades to their conversations and from there to their messages, which is what the account deletion route relies on.

The wire shape

The request body is four fields, typed inline in the route: conversationId (optional; absent starts a new conversation), tradition (a ChatMode), message and deep (optional). The response is text/plain; charset=utf-8, the model's text deltas concatenated with no framing, plus two headers: x-conversation-id, which is the id to send back on the next turn, and cache-control: no-store. There is no SSE, no JSON envelope and no final event; the stream closing is the end of the reading. Both clients read the header first and the bytes second.

The client-side types

On the web the transcript is Msg[], a role and a content string. On iOS it is [ChatMessage], which adds two things: a UUID identity assigned at creation, so SwiftUI keeps a row in place while its content grows during streaming, and a Source of .server or .onDevice. The doc comment on Source is worth reading in full: the source is shown in the transcript, not merely tracked, because the two behave differently in ways a person can feel, and an on-device answer is not saved with the rest of the reading. Conversation and StoredMessage are Decodable views of the two tables, read straight from Supabase; Conversation.mode maps an unknown tradition string to .vedic rather than failing to decode.

6.4The server side

The route, in order

app/api/chat/route.ts exports one POST and declares runtime = "nodejs", because the ephemeris is a WebAssembly module loaded from disk and the streaming SDK wants a full Node runtime. The handler runs in two phases: a preparation phase inside one try, where any failure returns a status and a sentence, and a streaming phase after the response has already begun, where a failure can only be written into the stream.

  1. Auth. createRouteSupabase(request) returns a bearer client if the request carries Authorization: Bearer, otherwise the cookie client. auth.getUser() with no user is a 401 with the body Unauthorized.
  2. Validation. An empty or whitespace message is a 400 Empty message; a tradition outside vedic, western, numerology is a 400 Invalid mode.
  3. The profile. One select on birth_profiles (RLS returns only the caller's row, so maybeSingle() with no filter is correct). A database error is a 500; a row with no chart is a 400 No chart. Complete intake first.; and ensureCurrentChart recomputes and rewrites a chart whose schema version is behind or which lacks derived facts, so an old profile gets the current prompt without the user doing anything.
  4. Today. Luxon renders the date in profile.timezone, and describeToday coarsens it to a part of day.
  5. The prompt. Numerology builds buildNumerologySystem plus a today block carrying the personal year and month. The two chart modes pick profile.chart[tradition], compute factsFor (undefined for Western), fetch the day's transit chart and describe it against the natal chart inside a try that degrades to no transit text, build buildChartSystem, append the timeline inside another try that logs and moves on, and build the today block with the deep-dependent word limit.
  6. The conversation. getOrCreateConversation either verifies the given id belongs to this user or inserts a row with the message's first 80 characters as its title.
  7. History. getMessages then selectHistory; the last retained turn gets the cache breakpoint; the new message is pushed on the end; and the new user message is written to messages before the model is called.

Anything thrown in those seven steps is caught once, logged as chat pre-stream error, and answered with a 500 whose body is a sentence a client can show. The "Conversation not found" thrown for a foreign id surfaces this way, as a 500 rather than a 403 or 404; the clients treat any non-2xx the same, so the distinction is not lost on anyone.

The stream

// app/api/chat/route.ts
const stream = new ReadableStream({
  async start(controller) {
    let full = "";
    try {
      const s = anthropic().messages.stream({ /* model, thinking, effort, system, messages */ });
      for await (const event of s) {
        if (event.type === "content_block_delta" && event.delta.type === "text_delta") {
          full += event.delta.text;
          controller.enqueue(encoder.encode(event.delta.text));
        }
      }
      const usage = (await s.finalMessage()).usage;
      console.log(`chat usage conv=${conversationId} model=${model} ...`);
    } catch (err) {
      console.error("chat stream error", err);
      controller.enqueue(encoder.encode("\n\n[The stars are momentarily clouded. Please try again.]"));
    } finally {
      if (full.trim()) await appendMessage(conversationId, "assistant", full, supabase);
      controller.close();
    }
  },
});

Only text_delta events are forwarded. Thinking blocks, which both models produce under adaptive thinking, are consumed and never sent, so the client sees the reading and not the reasoning. The accumulator full holds exactly what was streamed, and the finally stores it as the assistant turn only if it is non-blank. Two consequences follow. If the model fails before producing anything, the user turn is stored, the apology line goes down the wire, and no assistant row is written, so the next turn's history ends on a user message and selectHistory passes it through. If the model fails part-way, the partial text is stored and the apology is not, because the apology is enqueued to the client but never appended to full. A conversation therefore never contains the bracketed error text.

The data helpers

lib/data/chat.ts is four functions, each taking an optional db?: Db and building the cookie client when none is given. getOrCreateConversation is the only one with logic: given an id it selects it with both id and user_id filters and throws if nothing comes back, defence in depth on top of RLS, per its comment; without one it inserts and returns the new id. appendMessage is one insert. getMessages selects role, content and time for one conversation, ascending. listConversations selects id, tradition, title and time for the caller, descending, with no user filter because RLS supplies it.

6.5The clients

Web: the page

app/(app)/app/chat/page.tsx is a server component. It loads the birth profile and redirects to /app/intake when there is no chart, so the composer can never be shown to someone the route would reject. It reads ?c= from the search params. With an id it loads that conversation's messages and renders Chat with key={c}, the stored messages, and the conversation's own tradition as the initial mode (falling back to Vedic if the row is not in the list). Without one it renders Chat with key="new" and autostart set only when the user has no conversations at all. The key is what makes switching between readings in the sidebar remount the component rather than mutate it. The surrounding app/(app)/layout.tsx calls listConversations and hands the result to AppShell, whose sidebar links each one as /app/chat?c=<id>.

Web: the component

components/Chat.tsx is a client component holding the mode, the deep flag, the messages, the input and a busy flag in state, and the conversation id in a ref, because it changes during a fetch and must not trigger a render. send(textArg, silent) is the whole feature: append the user bubble (unless silent) and an empty assistant bubble, post the body, take the id from the header, then loop on res.body.getReader() with a TextDecoder in streaming mode and append each chunk to the last message. A non-OK response, a missing body or a thrown fetch all replace the last bubble with "Something went wrong. Please try again." In finally, busy clears and, if this was the first turn of a new conversation, router.refresh() re-runs the layout so the sidebar shows the new reading.

The composer is one input and three buttons: a mode pill that cycles Vedic, Western, Numerology; a Deep toggle, hidden below the sm breakpoint; and send, disabled while busy or while the input is blank. Enter sends. Under it is the one line of copy that appears in the same words on iOS: "For guidance and reflection. Not a substitute for professional advice." The empty state shows a greeting with the first name and the composer centred; once there is a message, the transcript scrolls above and the composer pins to the bottom with a safe-area inset. An assistant message renders through ReactMarkdown with remark-gfm inside the prose-reading styles; while it is still empty it shows "Reading your chart…" with a shimmer, and while it is still growing it shows a caret. An effect scrolls to the bottom on every change to messages.

iOS: the store

ChatStore is @Observable and @MainActor. Its state matches the web component's field for field (messages, input, mode, deep, a streaming flag, an error string, the conversation id) and adds the list of past conversations, a streamTask so a turn can be cancelled, and a chart whose didSet builds an OnDeviceReasoner. send(text, silent:, forceServer:) appends the bubbles, then in a task does one thing the web does not: if the turn is not silent, not forced to the server and the mode is Vedic, it asks the reasoner to route the question, and if the answer comes back .answered, writes it into the assistant bubble with source .onDevice and stops. The comment says why only Vedic: the router reads a Vedic chart, so a Western or numerology question has nothing local to answer from. Everything else goes to SancharaAPI.chatStream, and the loop over its events either records the conversation id or appends text.

The other entry points are short. start() loads the conversation list and, for a brand-new account with nothing to show, sends the intro prompt silently; the web does the same through autostart. newReading() cancels any stream and clears the transcript without touching the server, since the previous reading is already stored. open(conversation) cancels, adopts the conversation's id and mode, and reads its messages straight from the messages table through Supa.client. loadConversations() reads the conversations table the same way and keeps the last known list on failure rather than raising an error. expandLastAnswer() removes an on-device answer and resends the same question with forceServer, so a misrouted lookup costs one tap.

iOS: the views

ChatView is mounted by RootView with the ProfileStore, and owns the store plus the alerts, daily readings and suggestion engines that share its toolbar. Its body is the web layout in SwiftUI: an opening screen with the greeting and the composer when the transcript is empty, otherwise a ScrollViewReader transcript, the suggestion chips, and the composer. Three .task modifiers run on appear: one hands profile.chart to the store and calls start(); one loads the inbox and, only now that the user has a chart, asks for push permission; one warms the timeline cache for the widget. The composer is a vertical-axis TextField of one to four lines, the mode pill, the Deep pill and an arrow button disabled while streaming or blank, followed by the same disclaimer line. An assistant row is ShimmerText while empty, then MarkdownText with a StreamingCaret on the last row while streaming, and, when the source is on-device, a footer that says so and offers "Ask for a full reading". All three motion views take an active flag that is false under Reduce Motion.

The toolbar carries history (a clock), the wordmark, new reading, the inbox bell with an unread dot, and an avatar menu. HistoryView is a sheet listing chat.conversations with title, mode dot and time; tapping one calls open and dismisses; New calls newReading. Deep links and push taps are handled here too: an .ask(question) destination, which is how a question Siri declined arrives, starts a new reading and sends with forceServer.

MarkdownText exists because AttributedString(markdown:) handles inline syntax but collapses block structure. It splits the text into headings, paragraphs, bullets (with the ordinal kept for numbered items) and rules, gives each the spacing the web's CSS gives it, and parses only the inline runs with Foundation. A parse failure falls back to the plain text.

iOS: the API call

SancharaAPI.chatStream returns an AsyncThrowingStream<ChatEvent, Error>. The request is built by the shared request(_:method:), which asks Supa.accessToken() for the current session token and throws .unauthorized without one, so a signed-out client never reaches the network. The body is a ChatRequest with the same four fields as the web, and the timeout is 120 seconds because, as the comment says, readings can think for a while before the first byte. It uses URLSession.shared.bytes(for:). A non-2xx status reads the whole body as the error sentence (401 becomes .unauthorized); a 2xx yields .conversationId from the header, then bytes.

The byte loop handles the two problems a raw text stream has. A chunk boundary can split a multi-byte character, so bytes accumulate in undecoded until they decode as UTF-8. And a redraw per byte would repaint the transcript thousands of times for one reading, so decoded text is batched and yielded at 24 characters, with the remainder flushed at the end. onTermination cancels the task, which is how newReading() stops a reading in flight.

What is cached on the device

For chat, nothing durable. The transcript and the conversation list live in the store's memory and are re-read from Supabase on open. The chart the store hands to the reasoner comes from ProfileStore, which is the same chart ChartCache keeps in the App Group for Siri and the widget; that cache is described in chapter 10. An on-device answer is never written anywhere.

6.6How it works

A new conversation

The client sends no conversationId. The route inserts a conversation titled with the first 80 characters of the message, stores the user turn, and starts a stream whose history is that one turn. The response header carries the new id; the web stores it in the ref and iOS in conversationId, and both send it on every later turn. When the stream ends the web calls router.refresh() and iOS calls loadConversations(), so the new reading appears in the list. On this first turn the whole prefix is a cache write; the route's comment notes that a first turn is short enough that missing the cache costs little.

A later turn

The client sends the id. The route verifies ownership, loads every stored message, trims to the budget, marks the last retained turn as the cache breakpoint, appends and stores the new message, and streams. The mode is whatever the client sends; the row's tradition is not consulted and not updated. Both clients keep the mode with the conversation (the web page passes the row's tradition as the initial mode; iOS's open sets mode = conversation.mode), but nothing on the server prevents a client from continuing a Vedic conversation in numerology, and the stored transcript would then be a mix.

Reopening a past reading

The web reopens through the URL: the sidebar link sets ?c=, the page loads the messages on the server and renders them as initialMessages, and the new key remounts the component. iOS reopens through ChatStore.open, which reads the messages table directly with the Supabase client under RLS, no route involved. Both then continue with the later-turn flow above.

The intro reading

A brand-new account opens on a reading rather than an empty room. When the user has no conversations, the web mounts with autostart and iOS's start() finds an empty list, and each sends the same string silently: "Welcome me with a short reading for today based on my chart. Two or three short sections." Silent means the prompt itself is not shown as a user bubble, because, as the iOS comment puts it, the question is scaffolding the user never asked. It is stored, though: the route sees an ordinary message and writes it to messages, so reopening that conversation later shows the scaffolding line. On iOS a silent turn also bypasses the on-device router.

Deep

The toggle sends deep: true. The route switches to DEEP_READING_MODEL, keeps the default thinking effort, raises the length rule to 320 words and the ceiling to 3,000 tokens. Nothing about the conversation records that a turn was deep, and the next turn can be standard.

6.7Performance and cost

  • Every reading is billed on the server, and the mechanics are chapter 3: two cache breakpoints, low effort on standard readings, a 6,000 character history budget and a length rule. The measured mid-conversation turn is about $0.008 on Sonnet 5.
  • Two profile reads per turn, not more. The route selects birth_profiles once and, only when the timeline is loaded, loadTimeline reads what it needs. The transit chart is memoised per day and neighbourhood, so the ephemeris runs once per city per day rather than once per message.
  • Whole history loaded, then trimmed in memory. getMessages has no limit clause; every turn loads every message of the conversation and selectHistory discards most of it. On a conversation of hundreds of turns this is a larger read than it needs to be, though the index on (conversation_id, created_at) makes it cheap. A limit is the obvious improvement and has not been needed.
  • Time to first byte is thinking time. With adaptive thinking on, the model reasons before the first text_delta. Both clients show a shimmer rather than nothing, and iOS sets a 120 second request timeout for it.
  • Redraw cost is bounded on iOS. The 24 character batch in chatStream is the difference between a few hundred transcript updates per reading and several thousand. The web appends per network chunk, which the browser already coalesces.
  • The cheapest turn is one the phone answers. A Vedic lookup on a device with Apple Intelligence never posts at all. See chapter 10 for what qualifies.
  • The intro reading costs a turn per new account, and it is a first turn, so it is also the one that writes the cache rather than reading it.

6.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
No session401 Unauthorized. iOS never sends: request throws .unauthorized when there is no access token, and the bubble shows "Your session expired. Please sign in again."
Blank message400 Empty message. Both composers disable send on blank input, so this is a guard against a crafted request, not a UI state
Unknown mode400 Invalid mode
No chart on the profile400 No chart. Complete intake first. The web page redirects to intake before the composer renders
Chart written by an older releaseensureCurrentChart recomputes both charts and rewrites the row before the prompt is built
Transit computation failsCaught; the today block simply has no SKY TODAY section and the reading proceeds
Timeline load failsLogged as chat timeline context skipped; the stable half is sent without it
Conversation id belongs to someone elsegetOrCreateConversation throws; the route answers 500 with a sentence, and nothing is written. RLS would refuse the insert regardless
Model fails before the first byteThe user turn is stored, the client receives the bracketed apology, and no assistant row is written
Model fails mid-streamThe partial text is stored as the assistant turn; the apology is shown but not stored
Chunk boundary splits a multi-byte characteriOS holds bytes in undecoded until they decode; the web's TextDecoder in streaming mode does the same
User starts a new reading mid-stream (iOS)newReading cancels the task; onTermination cancels the request; the server still finishes and stores what it produced
Conversation list fails to load (iOS)The last known list stays; no banner, because the transcript is unaffected
A stored conversation has an unknown traditioniOS decodes it as Vedic; the web falls back to Vedic when the row is missing from the list
Reduce Motion is onGlow, shimmer and caret render static
On-device answer was wrong for the question"Ask for a full reading" removes it and resends with forceServer

Tests

The chat feature is covered by the tests of the modules it is built from, not by a test of the route or the views. There is no app/api/chat/route.test.ts, and no Swift test constructs a ChatStore or calls chatStream. What does exist:

  • lib/astrology/prompt.test.ts: everything the route puts in the system blocks, listed in section 3.7. For chat the load-bearing cases are that the stable half carries no date, sky or length rule, and that a Western chart gets Placidus houses and no Vedic technique.
  • lib/data/history.test.ts: the five budget rules, including that a window never begins on an assistant turn, which is what keeps a conversation whose last model call failed valid on the next turn.
  • lib/data/injection.test.ts: appendMessage writes { conversation_id, role, content } through the injected client and never builds the cookie client when one is given. This is the test that stands between an iOS turn and a silent RLS failure.
  • app/smoke.test.ts: the app layout module imports and exports a function. It proves the Next.js scaffold loads under Vitest and nothing more.
  • Swift: SuggestionTests, OnDeviceRoutingTests and ChartFactsTests exercise the three on-device files in the Chat folder. They belong to chapter 10; none touches the transcript, the store or the stream.
What is not pinned The two behaviours in the stream's finally, that a failed first turn stores no assistant row and that a partial reading is stored without the apology, are read from the code and not from a test. The same is true of the 24 character batch and the UTF-8 hold-back in chatStream. They are the first things to cover if the route or the client ever changes shape.

6.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. app/api/chat/route.ts, once straight through. It is the feature; everything else is a client of it.
  2. lib/data/chat.ts, for the ownership check and the four queries.
  3. supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql, the two tables and the two policies, then 0003_numerology_mode.sql.
  4. components/Chat.tsx, send first, then the composer.
  5. app/(app)/app/chat/page.tsx, for key and autostart.
  6. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChatStore.swift, send first, and the comment on chart.
  7. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift, the chatStream section at the end.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChatView.swift, the three .task modifiers and onDeviceFooter.

Check yourself

Why is the user message stored before the model is called rather than after, alongside the reply?

So that a failed model call still leaves the question in the transcript. The assistant row is written in finally only if text arrived, and selectHistory tolerates a history that ends on a user turn.

Why does the response carry the conversation id in a header rather than in the body?

The body is the reading, streamed as bare text with no framing. A header arrives before the first byte and does not need parsing out of the stream; both clients read it first and then loop on bytes.

The iOS app reads conversations and messages directly. Why is that safe?

Both tables are owner-only under RLS, and the Supabase client carries the user's session, so the read can only ever return that user's rows. Writes still go through the route, which is the only code that builds a prompt.

What happens to the intro reading's prompt text?

It is hidden in the client (silent) but stored on the server as an ordinary user message, so a reopened first conversation shows it.

Why does Chat take a key from the page?

Switching readings must remount the component so its state (messages, mode, the id in the ref) starts from the new conversation rather than carrying the old one's ref forward.

Why does the route forward only text_delta events?

Adaptive thinking produces thinking blocks first. The client should see the reading, not the reasoning, and the stored assistant turn should be what the user saw.

Chapter seven

7Kundli

The kundli is the chart as a thing to look at: a North Indian diagram of twelve fixed houses with the rashis rotating through them, drawn from the stored chart and never interpreted. The same JSON becomes a two-page PDF on the server and a tappable SwiftUI drawing on the phone, and the one idea that shaped both is that the lagna is the only input the layout needs.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. Nothing here is generated by a model. Every number on the page or the screen comes from the vedic half of birth_profiles.chart, which chapter 5 showed being computed and stored at intake. The server renders that JSON with pdf-lib into a document meant to be printed and kept. The iOS app decodes the same JSON into NatalChart, resolves it into twelve KundliHouse values, and draws them with a geometry table that is, polygon for polygon, the one the PDF uses. On the web there is no on-screen diagram: the home and profile pages link to the PDF.

7.1Intent

The PDF renderer's own comment states the intent for the document: "A birth chart is not a report; in most families it is a piece of paper that gets kept, and this is the version people print, mail to a relative, or hand to an astrologer." So it is set like a document, a serif for the name and the data, a sans for labels, one accent used sparingly, in two pages ordered by the questions people ask. Page one is the diagram and the three values a chart is identified by. Page two is the full table, read both ways: by graha and by house.

The screen's intent is narrower and KundliView states it: "help someone find where a planet sits and what a house holds." The diagram shows glyphs and rashi numerals only; degrees, nakshatras and retrogrades live in the table under it and in the sheet that opens when a house is tapped. The table repeats everything the diagram encodes in plain rows that take unbounded Dynamic Type, so nobody has to read the diagram to get at the numbers.

A third intent runs under both: the diagram must be correct in the way that does not look wrong. KundliTests puts it plainly: "a chart with the signs rotated one house off is still a perfectly handsome diagram." The tests therefore pin the rotation rule and the geometry, and leave the pixels to a snapshot that only checks something was drawn.

7.2Files and folders

app/api/kundli/route.ts              GET: the PDF, as an attachment
lib/pdf/kundli.ts                    generateKundliPdf, HOUSE_POLY, prettyDate, shortDate
lib/pdf/kundli.test.ts               pages, WinAnsi, missing dasha, UTC dates
lib/astrology/numerology.ts          computeNumerology, computeNameNumber (read by the route)
lib/data/birthProfile.ts             ensureCurrentChart (called by the route)
app/(app)/app/page.tsx               "Download your kundli (PDF)"
app/(app)/app/profile/page.tsx       "Download kundli (PDF)"

mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/
├── ChartData.swift                  ChartBundle, NatalChart, ChartPlanet, Rashi, KundliHouse, PeriodProgress
├── KundliChartView.swift            KundliGeometry, KundliFrame, the drawing
└── KundliView.swift                 the screen, HouseDetailSheet, Export as PDF
mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift   "Open my kundli", app and extension
mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/DeepLink.swift           drains the pending destination
mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift        the App Group cache
mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift       kundliPDF()
mobile/Sanchara/Tests/KundliTests.swift                  rotation, geometry, spoken text
mobile/Sanchara/Tests/KundliSnapshotTests.swift          renders a PNG to look at
mobile/project.yml                   which of these the widget target compiles

The route reads the row and delegates everything else. The PDF module and ChartData.swift are the two places where the diagram's geometry is written down, once for each coordinate system, and each carries a comment pointing at the other.

7.3Model

The input to the PDF

// lib/pdf/kundli.ts
export type KundliInput = {
  name: string;
  birthDate: string;
  birthTime: string;
  place: string;
  timezone: string;
  chart: Chart; // the vedic chart
  numerology: { mulank: number; bhagyank: number; namank: number };
};

Chart is the type from lib/astrology/types.ts: ascendant sign and degree, the planets with sign, degree, house, retrograde flag and nakshatra, Moon and Sun signs, the optional ayanamsa and the optional dasha. The renderer uses the house field on each planet as stored; it does not recompute houses from longitudes, which is why the route runs ensureCurrentChart first. The three numerology values are computed by the route from the birth date and the full name.

The same JSON in Swift

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift
struct ChartBundle: Decodable, Equatable, Sendable {
    let vedic: NatalChart
    let western: NatalChart?
}

struct NatalChart: Codable, Equatable, Sendable {
    let tradition: String
    let ascendant: Ascendant
    let planets: [ChartPlanet]
    let moonSign: String
    let sunSign: String
    let ayanamsa: Double?
    let dasha: DashaInfo?
    let derived: DerivedFacts?
    ...
}

The file's comment says it "mirrors lib/astrology/types.ts field for field", with one deliberate omission: the twelve cusp longitudes in the TypeScript houses array are not decoded, because the drawing needs house numbers, not cusps, and houses on the Swift side is a computed property that resolves them. derived is optional at the top level so a chart cached before the derived facts shipped still decodes; the comment's rule is that "a widget or a Siri intent must never fail to decode a chart it has already stored."

ChartPlanet adds three computed views of a row. glyph maps each graha to an astronomical symbol, with Rahu and Ketu borrowing the ascending and descending node signs, and a long comment on why the Moon is U+263D rather than U+263E: on iOS the latter resolves to the emoji font and draws a solid crescent heavier than the eight line-art glyphs beside it, and the text-presentation selector does not fix it. degreeText prints 14°32', the form every printed kundli uses. spokenDescription is one sentence with no abbreviations, used for VoiceOver and handed to the on-device model as a fact.

Rashis and houses

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift
extension NatalChart {
    var ascendantRashi: Rashi { Rashi(english: ascendant.sign) ?? .mesha }

    var houses: [KundliHouse] {
        let ascIndex = ascendantRashi.rawValue
        return (1...12).map { number in
            let rashi = Rashi(rawValue: (ascIndex + number - 1) % 12) ?? .mesha
            return KundliHouse(
                number: number,
                rashi: rashi,
                planets: planets.filter { $0.house == number }
            )
        }
    }
}

This is the whole rule of a North Indian chart, and the comment above it says so: houses are fixed on the page and the rashis rotate, so house 1 always holds the lagna's rashi and each house after it takes the next sign. Rashi is an enum from mesha (0) to meena (11) carrying both the English name the ephemeris returns and the Sanskrit name a kundli is labelled with, plus number, the 1 to 12 numeral printed in each house. The PDF has the same two tables as constants, SIGNS and RASHI, and the same modular arithmetic in drawChart and the bhava table.

KundliHouse carries the number, its rashi, the planets filed under it, isLagna, a one-line domain ("Self, body, how you begin" for house 1, and so on), and an accessibilityLabel that ends in "Empty." for an unoccupied house. The twelve domain strings are duplicated in the PDF as BHAVA_MEANING, and the PDF's comment gives the reason: "so the paper and the screen say the same thing."

7.4The server side

GET /api/kundli

The route runs on the Node runtime. It resolves the caller through createRouteSupabase, so a bearer token from the phone and a cookie from the browser both evaluate as the user under RLS, and answers 401 with no user. It then selects the profile row with maybeSingle(). A read error is logged and answered 500 with "Something went wrong loading your profile. Please try again." A row without a chart, or no row, is a 400: "Complete your birth details first." Then ensureCurrentChart runs, so a chart stored before the whole-sign change is rewritten before it is drawn. 

// app/api/kundli/route.ts
const chart = (p.chart as { vedic: Chart }).vedic;
const fullName = `${p.first_name} ${p.last_name}`.trim();
const num = computeNumerology(String(p.birth_date));

const pdf = await generateKundliPdf({
  name: fullName,
  birthDate: String(p.birth_date),
  birthTime: String(p.birth_time).slice(0, 5),
  place: String(p.place_name),
  timezone: String(p.timezone),
  chart,
  numerology: { mulank: num.mulank, bhagyank: num.bhagyank, namank: computeNameNumber(fullName) },
});

return new Response(Buffer.from(pdf), {
  headers: {
    "content-type": "application/pdf",
    "content-disposition": 'attachment; filename="sanchara-kundli.pdf"',
    "cache-control": "no-store",
  },
});

Only the Vedic chart is rendered; there is no Western kundli. The response is an attachment named sanchara-kundli.pdf with no-store, since the document is personal and the chart may have been upgraded moments before. The README describes this folder as "Chart JSON and PDF", but on 30 September 2026 the handler has a single GET that returns the PDF. The chart JSON reaches the iOS app by a direct Supabase read, described in 7.5, not through this route.

The page

generateKundliPdf creates a document with four standard fonts, Times Roman and Helvetica in regular and bold, and sets the title to the name followed by "Vedic kundli", the author to "Astrya" and the subject to "Vedic birth chart". Pages are 595 by 842 points (A4) with a 56-point margin; those three numbers are the constants W, H and M. The palette is written with its contrast ratios: the brand orange (ACCENT) is 3.3:1 on white and is used only for rules and fills; text in orange uses ACCENT_TEXT, a darkened variant at 5.3:1; MUTED grey is 5.4:1. The inner chart lines are a mid grey, GRID, because full ink at 1.2 points "drew a heavy black cage around type set at 9pt".

pdf-lib has no character-spacing control, so the small-capital labels are drawn by tracked(), one character at a time, advancing by each glyph's width plus a spacing. trackedWidth() computes the same sum so a label can be centred or right-aligned. Every page gets a header (a 4-point accent band along the top, "ASTRYA" tracked at the left, a right-hand label, a rule) and a footer (a rule and one line of 7.5-point text).

Page one

  1. The name at 27 points serif bold, then "Born 12 September 1994 at 04:35" and a provenance line: place, timezone and, when the chart has an ayanamsa, "Lahiri ayanamsa 24.17°".
  2. identityBand: a 62-point tinted band with a 3-point accent bar at its left edge, split into three columns headed LAGNA, CHANDRA and SURYA. Each shows the Sanskrit rashi at 15 points serif bold with the English name beneath; the lagna's caption also carries its degree.
  3. drawChart, a 414-point square centred horizontally with its bottom edge 168 points from the foot of the page.
  4. Two captions under the square explaining that houses hold fixed positions with the top diamond always first, that the numeral in each house is its rashi from 1 Mesha to 12 Meena, and that an asterisk marks a retrograde graha.
  5. The footer: "Computed with the Swiss Ephemeris by Astrya. For guidance and reflection, not a substitute for professional advice."

Drawing the square

// lib/pdf/kundli.ts
const HOUSE_POLY: [number, number][][] = [
  [[0.25, 0.75], [0.5, 1], [0.75, 0.75], [0.5, 0.5]],   // 1  top kite
  [[0, 1], [0.5, 1], [0.25, 0.75]],                      // 2
  [[0, 1], [0.25, 0.75], [0, 0.5]],                      // 3
  [[0, 0.5], [0.25, 0.75], [0.5, 0.5], [0.25, 0.25]],    // 4  left kite
  ...
  [[1, 1], [0.75, 0.75], [0.5, 1]],                      // 12
];

The twelve houses are unit-square polygons with y running up, pdf-lib's convention. The comment says they are "the same twelve polygons the iOS app draws (KundliGeometry), expressed for the other coordinate system." anchor(house) gives the point a house's contents are centred on: the centroid for the four kites, and for the eight triangles the centroid pulled 14 percent of the way towards the middle of the square, because a triangle's centroid sits two-thirds of the way out to its long outer edge and contents centred there overhang the border. The comment calls this "the same trap the app's chart fell into on its first render."

drawChart first washes the lagna house with a pale tint using drawSvgPath (flipping y with H - p.y because the SVG path convention runs down), so the orientation is legible without a caption. It strokes the border in ink at 1 point, then the two diagonals and the four diamond edges in GRID at 0.6. For each house it computes the rashi number as ((ascIndex + house - 1) % 12) + 1, prints it at 9 points serif 7 points above the anchor (accent text for the lagna, muted otherwise), and then lays the occupants out two to a row, 11 points per row, as 7.5-point bold sans labels such as Sa* 11° Ju 17°. Two per row is so "a four-graha house has to fit inside the narrowest triangle without the abbreviations shrinking below legibility." The abbreviations come from ABBR (Su, Mo, Me, Ve, Ma, Ju, Sa, Ra, Ke).

Plain ASCII on purpose The standard PDF fonts are WinAnsi-encoded, and pdf-lib throws on a character outside that set rather than substituting one. The retrograde symbol and a superscript r are both outside it, so the chart marks retrograde with an asterisk and the table with "(R)". A single stray character would not degrade the page; it would fail the whole route with a 500. The test named for this feeds the renderer every retrograde, every house occupied and a name with accented letters.

Page two

The header's right-hand label is the name in capitals. Four sections follow, each opened by section(), a tracked accent-text label with a rule running from it to the margin.

  • GRAHAS AND NAKSHATRAS. Five columns at fixed offsets from the margin (graha at 0, rashi at 104, degree at 214, house at 274, nakshatra at 324), 19-point rows, alternate rows tinted because "a tint on alternate rows does the work five vertical rules would". A row reads Sa Saturn (R), the Sanskrit rashi, 11°36' from degreeText, the house number, and the nakshatra in muted sans, or a dash when absent.
  • BHAVAS. The same data the other way round: twelve 17-point rows, one per house, giving the rashi by the rotation rule, the grahas in it joined by commas (or a dash), and the BHAVA_MEANING line. House 1 is printed "1 · Lagna" in accent text. The comment says this table answers "what's in my seventh", the question people bring to a printed chart, and fills the lower half of the page that the first draft left empty.
  • VIMSHOTTARI DASHA, only when the chart has a dasha: two rows, Mahadasha and Antardasha, with the lord at 12 points serif bold and the span right-aligned as "Apr 2012" to "Apr 2032" via shortDate.
  • NUMEROLOGY. Three columns, MULANK, BHAGYANK and NAMANK, each a 22-point serif bold number in accent text with a note beside it: "Root, from the day of birth", "Destiny, from the whole date", "Name, from its letters".

The footer repeats the identity line: "Astrya · name · born date at time, place."

Dates

// lib/pdf/kundli.ts
export function prettyDate(iso: string) {
  const [y, m, d] = iso.slice(0, 10).split("-").map(Number);
  if (!y || !m || !d) return iso;
  // Constructed in UTC and read back in UTC: a bare date formatted in a local
  // zone west of Greenwich comes back as the day before.
  return new Date(Date.UTC(y, m - 1, d)).toLocaleDateString("en-GB", {
    day: "numeric", month: "long", year: "numeric", timeZone: "UTC",
  });
}

prettyDate and shortDate are exported "for the test, not for callers": the UTC pinning is the kind of thing that silently regresses, and it cannot be asserted through the finished PDF because pdf-lib compresses its content streams. A malformed date is passed through unchanged rather than printed as "Invalid Date".

7.5The clients

Web

The web app has no chart drawing. app/(app)/app/page.tsx shows "Download your kundli (PDF)" under the "Start a reading" button once a profile exists, and app/(app)/app/profile/page.tsx shows "Download kundli (PDF)" above the edit form. Both are plain anchors to /api/kundli; the browser sends the session cookie, the route answers with an attachment, and the browser saves it.

iOS: where the chart comes from

ProfileStore.loadChart() selects the chart column from birth_profiles for rows where it is not null, decodes it as ChartBundle, keeps the vedic half as NatalChart and writes it to ChartCache.shared.save(chart:). The cache is a JSON file named chart.json in the App Group container, written atomically with complete file protection, and read back on the next launch before any network call. ChatView and ProfileView both present KundliView(details:chart:) as a sheet, guarded by if let details = profile.details, let chart = profile.chart, so the sheet cannot open on a device that has never loaded a chart.

iOS: KundliView

The screen is a NavigationStack titled "Your kundli" with a Done button, holding five blocks in a scroll view capped at 480 points wide.

  • Summary. The full name in a serif title, a birth line built from the details ("12 September 1994 at 04:35" using the device's long date style) and the place, then three stat columns, Lagna, Chandra and Surya, each with the Sanskrit rashi and an English caption; the lagna's caption is its degree. Lagna leads "because the whole diagram is oriented from it."
  • Rashi chart. KundliChartView bound to selectedHouse, under a caption that teaches the diagram: houses sit in fixed places, the numeral in each is its rashi counted from Mesha, tap a house to see what it holds.
  • Vimshottari dasha, when present: Mahadasha and Antardasha with "until Apr 2032", formatted by ChartFacts.calendarDay in UTC.
  • Grahas. One row per planet: glyph, name with a retrograde mark, nakshatra, then "Vrishchika 2°42'" and "House 1" on the right. Each row is a button that opens the house the planet sits in. This table is "the accessible path through the chart and the one that survives the largest text sizes."
  • Footer. "Export as PDF", an error line, and when the chart has an ayanamsa, "Sidereal, Lahiri ayanamsa 24.17°. Computed with the Swiss Ephemeris."

Tapping a house, on the diagram or through a planet row, sets selectedHouse, and a sheet(item:) presents HouseDetailSheet at medium or large detents: the house number (or "House 1 · Lagna"), the Sanskrit and English rashi, the domain line, and either the planets with degree and nakshatra or the sentence "No graha sits here. An empty house is read through its lord and the aspects reaching it", pointing the person back to the reading.

iOS: KundliChartView

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/KundliChartView.swift
enum KundliGeometry {
    static func polygon(house: Int) -> [CGPoint] {
        let tl = CGPoint(x: 0, y: 0), tr = CGPoint(x: 1, y: 0)
        let bl = CGPoint(x: 0, y: 1), br = CGPoint(x: 1, y: 1)
        let t = CGPoint(x: 0.5, y: 0), r = CGPoint(x: 1, y: 0.5)
        let b = CGPoint(x: 0.5, y: 1), l = CGPoint(x: 0, y: 0.5)
        let c = CGPoint(x: 0.5, y: 0.5)
        let tlq = CGPoint(x: 0.25, y: 0.25), trq = CGPoint(x: 0.75, y: 0.25)
        let blq = CGPoint(x: 0.25, y: 0.75), brq = CGPoint(x: 0.75, y: 0.75)

        switch house {
        case 1: return [tlq, t, trq, c]
        case 2: return [tl, t, tlq]
        case 3: return [tl, tlq, l]
        case 4: return [l, tlq, c, blq]
        ...
        default: return [tr, trq, t]
        }
    }
}

The geometry is a unit square with y running down, SwiftUI's convention, named at its nine significant points so "the table below reads like the drawing". labelAnchor is the PDF's anchor with a pull-in of 0.12 instead of 0.14, and its comment tells the story the PDF's comment refers to: the first render clipped the retrograde marks off Mercury and half of Jupiter. path(house:in:) scales a polygon into a rect. KundliFrame is a Shape that strokes every line of the chart as one path: the rect, the two diagonals, and the diamond through the four midpoints.

The view is a GeometryReader that takes the smaller of width and height as the side and stacks three layers. First, twelve HouseRegion shapes, each filled (accent at 0.16 when selected, 0.05 for the lagna, a 2 percent white wash on even houses so the regions read as distinct without twelve borders), outlined only for the lagna (accent at half strength) and the selected house (full accent, 1.25 points), each a tappable accessibility button carrying the house's label. Second, the frame stroked in Theme.chartLine at 1 point, hit-testing off. Third, the contents positioned at each anchor: the rashi numeral in a serif at a caption-relative ScaledMetric of 11, and the glyphs two per row at a footnote-relative 15, with a small accent "R" overlaid top-right of a retrograde planet. Both metrics scale with Dynamic Type but are relative to small styles, since "the diagram has fixed geometry, so the scaling is capped."

The comment on stroke(for:) records a visual decision worth keeping: the first version gave the lagna a full-strength outline, and "the result was a large glowing diamond that read as 'this house is selected'", which is the meaning selection needs for itself. The lagna is now marked, not spotlit.

iOS: the intent and the widget target

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift
struct OpenKundliIntent: AppIntent {
    static var title: LocalizedStringResource = "Open my kundli"
    static var openAppWhenRun = true

    func perform() async throws -> some IntentResult {
        ChartCache.shared.setPendingDestination("kundli")
        return .result()
    }
}

This is the one intent that opens the app, because "a diagram is the answer, and Siri has nowhere to put one." It is kept in a file of its own with no dependency beyond ChartCache, because it is compiled into the widget extension as well as the app: a Control Center button runs it from a process with no Supabase session. It writes the destination to the App Group's UserDefaults suite rather than memory, since the control that ran it may be in a different process from the app it is about to launch. 

# mobile/project.yml, target SancharaWidgets
sources:
  - path: SancharaWidgets
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/ReadingCard.swift
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/BirthProfileDetails.swift
  - path: Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift

The widget target lists six app files by path, and the comment in project.yml gives the rule: "Each of these is dependency-free on purpose; pulling in anything that imports Supabase would drag the whole client into a process that has no session to use it with." The widget target declares no package dependency at all. That is why ChartData.swift imports only Foundation, why PeriodProgress lives in it ("the period widget needs it too, and this file is one of the few compiled into both targets"), and why KundliChartView, which imports SwiftUI and Theme, is not in the list: the widget decodes the chart and reads its facts but does not draw the diagram. The widgets themselves are in chapter 10; the project layout is in chapter 14.

7.6How it works

The diagram's geometry

Read the picture against the tests. "The four kites are an eighth of the square each, the eight triangles a sixteenth. Together that is the whole square and no more" is testTheTwelveHousesTileTheSquareExactly, computed by the shoelace formula. testHouseOneIsAtTheTopAndSevenAtTheBottom asserts house 1's anchor has y below 0.35 and house 7's above 0.65, with 4 on the left and 10 on the right, which the comment calls "the one orientation rule a North Indian chart cannot get wrong". And testEveryLabelAnchorSitsInsideItsOwnHouse runs a winding test on every anchor, since the kites are concave and a centroid test would not do.

Downloading the PDF

  1. On the web the anchor navigates to /api/kundli with the session cookie. On iOS, "Export as PDF" calls SancharaAPI.kundliPDF(), which attaches the Supabase access token as a bearer header; isPreparingPDF relabels the button "Preparing…" and blocks a second tap.
  2. The route reads the row, refuses without a user or a chart, and upgrades a stale chart.
  3. It computes the three numerology numbers from the birth date and full name.
  4. generateKundliPdf lays out both pages and returns the bytes.
  5. The browser saves sanchara-kundli.pdf. iOS writes the data to <first-last>-kundli.pdf in the temporary directory and presents a ShareSheet with that URL; a thrown error becomes the footer's error line.

Opening the kundli from Siri or a control

  1. OpenKundliIntent.perform() writes "kundli" to the pendingDestination key in the App Group UserDefaults suite and returns; openAppWhenRun brings the app forward.
  2. ChatView calls DeepLink.shared.drain() whenever the scene phase becomes active. drain() reads and clears the key in one step through takePendingDestination(), "so a destination is never handled twice", and sets pending = .kundli.
  3. ChatView observes DeepLink.shared.pending, sets showKundli = true and clears pending. The sheet reads profile.chart, which on a warm launch came from ChartCache before any network call.

A widget tap reaches the same place by URL: DeepLink.handle(url:) maps the host kundli of a sanchara:// URL to the same destination, with no App Group hop, and RootView routes such URLs to DeepLink rather than to the auth store so they are not mistaken for an expired sign-in link.

7.7Performance and cost

  • No ephemeris on the hot path. Rendering reads stored JSON. The only time the route computes anything astronomical is the one-off upgrade of a pre-version-2 chart, which chapter 5 describes.
  • Standard fonts, no embedding. The four StandardFonts ship with every PDF reader, so the document carries no font bytes and the test fixture's two pages are small. The price is WinAnsi, which is why the renderer is ASCII-only.
  • No caching, by design. cache-control: no-store. The document is personal, cheap to regenerate, and may reflect an upgrade that happened during this request.
  • The screen draws from disk. ProfileStore.load() reads chart.json from the App Group before its network refresh, so the kundli sheet opens from cache on a warm launch and works offline. The chart is about 4 KB.
  • Houses resolved once per read. NatalChart.houses is a computed property that filters the planet list twelve times; with nine planets that is trivial, and the view calls it inside a ForEach rather than storing it.
  • The widget stays small. Compiling six dependency-free files into the extension keeps the Supabase client, SwiftUI drawing code and the network out of a process that must render in a tight memory budget and cannot sign in.
  • No model call, no cost. Nothing in this chapter calls Claude. The kundli is the one feature that is free at any volume beyond Vercel function time and a few kilobytes of Postgres reads.

7.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
