Tests

  • lib/pdf/kundli.test.ts (Vitest, five tests, real pdf-lib): "produces a two-page document" reloads the bytes and checks the page count and title; "never emits a character the standard fonts cannot encode" renders the fixture and then a crowded variant with every planet retrograde, every house occupied and an accented name; "survives a chart with no dasha and no ayanamsa"; "renders dates in UTC wherever the server is" sets process.env.TZ to America/Los_Angeles, Asia/Kolkata, Pacific/Kiritimati and UTC in turn and asserts the same strings, including the two dates either side of a UTC day boundary; and "passes a malformed date through rather than printing Invalid Date". The file's comment says what is deliberately not tested: "Nothing here checks how it looks; that is what eyes are for."
  • mobile/Sanchara/Tests/KundliTests.swift (XCTest, twelve tests, no UI). Rotation: testHouseOneHoldsTheLagnaRashi, testRashisRunForwardFromTheLagnaAndWrapAtPisces (Scorpio lagna: houses 2, 5, 6 and 12 hold Dhanu, Meena, Mesha and Tula), testAnAriesLagnaPutsEachRashiInItsOwnNumberedHouse, testPlanetsAreFiledUnderTheirOwnHouse. Formatting: testDegreesArePrintedInDegreesAndMinutes (14.5 is 14°30', 29.983 is 29°58'), testEveryGrahaHasItsOwnGlyph (nine distinct single-character glyphs across ChartFacts.bodyNames), testSpokenDescriptionNamesEverythingVoiceOverNeeds, testAnEmptyHouseSaysSoRatherThanReadingAsBlank. Geometry: testTheTwelveHousesTileTheSquareExactly, testKitesAndTrianglesHaveTheAreasTheyShould, testHouseOneIsAtTheTopAndSevenAtTheBottom, testEveryLabelAnchorSitsInsideItsOwnHouse.
  • mobile/Sanchara/Tests/KundliSnapshotTests.swift: one test, testRendersTheChart, renders KundliChartView at 340 points through ImageRenderer at scale 3, asserts the PNG is over 2,000 bytes, writes it to kundli-chart.png in the temporary directory and prints its path as KUNDLI_SNAPSHOT_PATH. The comment explains the restraint: "a snapshot test that fails whenever anyone touches a colour is a test that gets deleted."

The PDF's HOUSE_POLY and anchor have no tests of their own; the Swift geometry tests cover the same polygons in the other coordinate system, and the PDF test covers what could take the route down.

7.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift, the rotation rule in NatalChart.houses and the glyph comment.
  2. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/KundliChartView.swift, KundliGeometry first, then the three layers of the view.
  3. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/KundliTests.swift, to see which properties of the drawing are pinned and why the pixels are not.
  4. lib/pdf/kundli.ts, HOUSE_POLY, anchor and drawChart against the Swift versions, then the two pages top to bottom.
  5. lib/pdf/kundli.test.ts, the WinAnsi test and the timezone test.
  6. app/api/kundli/route.ts, the three refusals and the upgrade call.
  7. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift and DeepLink.swift, then the SancharaWidgets target in mobile/project.yml.

Check yourself

Why is the lagna the only input the layout needs?

Because the houses never move. House 1 is always the top kite; the rashi in each house is (asc + house - 1) mod 12; and each planet already carries its house number from the stored chart. Everything else on the diagram is a lookup.

Why does the route call ensureCurrentChart when the drawing code never computes a house?

That is exactly why. The renderers trust planet.house as stored, so a chart written under the old house rule would print numerals and planets that disagree. The upgrade rewrites the row before either renderer sees it.

Why is the triangle's anchor pulled towards the centre, and why by two different amounts?

A triangle's centroid sits two-thirds of the way out to its long edge, so a numeral over a row of glyphs centred there crosses the border. Each renderer chose its own pull-in for its own type sizes: 0.12 on screen, 0.14 on paper.

Why does the PDF mark retrograde with an asterisk when the app uses a symbol?

The PDF uses the standard WinAnsi fonts and pdf-lib throws on any character outside that encoding. The app renders with system fonts and can use the symbol. Neither is wrong; the encoding decided it.

Why can the widget extension compile ChartData.swift but not KundliChartView.swift?

ChartData imports only Foundation and is listed by path in the widget target. The view imports SwiftUI and Theme and is not listed. The extension decodes the cached chart to answer questions; it does not draw the diagram.

Why does the intent write to UserDefaults instead of presenting the kundli itself?

It may be running in the widget extension, a process that can present nothing. The app drains the key when it comes to the front, and reads-and-clears in one step so the destination is never handled twice.

Chapter eight

8The life timeline

A person's whole life as Vimshottari divides it, drawn from the stored chart, with the things that actually happened to them pinned into the periods they fell in. The server computes and explains; the phone renders. Nothing reaches the map that the user did not type or confirm.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. The timeline is the largest feature in the app and the only one that stores what the user says about their own life rather than what the ephemeris computes. That fact shapes nearly every decision below: why the tables are owner-only in both directions, why a scan of the chat history proposes and never writes, why an extracted moment carries the sentence it was read from, and why a date the user gave to the year is never rendered to the day.

8.1Intent

A dasha is abstract until something real sits inside it. The chart already knows that a person's Saturn period runs from 2019 to 2038; what makes that a life map rather than a table is that they moved house in 2021 and changed jobs in 2024, and both fell in the Mercury sub-period. The timeline answers "why was that year like that" against their year, not a generic one. It does four things:

  • Lays out the twelve mahadashas from birth and places every pinned moment in the mahadasha and antardasha it happened in.
  • Writes a plain-language theme and meaning for each period, for someone who has never heard the word dasha, and caches them until the moments inside a period change.
  • Fills itself in from the user's own chat messages, proposing dated moments with the quote that gave them away, each one confirmed or discarded by hand.
  • Tells the chat, so a reading about 2021 knows what 2021 held.

The idea that shaped it is in the comment at the top of app/api/timeline/scan/route.ts: "this is inferred content about someone's own life, and silently filing an AI's guess about a death or a divorce onto their timeline would be a bad thing to be wrong about." Every path onto the map goes through a confirmation the user taps.

8.2Files and folders

lib/timeline/
├── build.ts                 buildTimeline, the types, eventsHashFor, NOW_LORD
├── load.ts                  loadBirthRow, loadTimeline, loadScanState (server-only)
├── explain.ts               the meaning prompt, parseExplanations, explainTimeline
├── extract.ts               the scan prompt, parseExtractedEvents, selectTranscript
├── describe.ts              describeTimelineForPrompt, the block the chat sees
└── *.test.ts                build, describe, explain, extract (47 tests)

app/api/
├── timeline/route.ts        GET: the whole map plus scan state
├── timeline/explain/route.ts POST: write or rewrite every meaning
├── timeline/scan/route.ts   POST: propose moments from chat history
└── life-events/route.ts     POST: pin one or a batch; DELETE: unpin one

supabase/migrations/
├── 0006_life_events.sql     life_events, life_event_scans
└── 0007_period_readings.sql period_readings

mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Timeline/
├── TimelineStore.swift      DashaBand, LifeEvent, CandidateEvent, CandidateResolver,
│                            TimelinePayload, TimelineDate, TimelineStore, NewLifeEvent
├── TimelineView.swift       the sheet: NowCard, BandRow, EventRow, the scan offer
├── AddMomentView.swift      pin one moment by hand
├── CandidatesView.swift     confirm what the scan found
└── PeriodExplainerView.swift what a dasha is, in five static paragraphs

mobile/Sanchara/Tests/
├── TimelineTests.swift
├── CandidateResolverTests.swift
└── PeriodProgressTests.swift

build.ts, describe.ts and the pure halves of explain.ts and extract.ts have no Node-only imports, which is why they are tested without a database or a model. load.ts, explainTimeline and extractLifeEvents are server-only and are the only places that touch Supabase or Claude. The dasha arithmetic lives in lib/astrology/dasha.ts; this chapter only calls its vimshottariTimeline. There is no web page for the timeline as of this date: the four route handlers are the whole web surface, and the iOS app is the only client that draws it.

8.3Model

The three tables

life_events holds what the user pinned. The comment in supabase/migrations/0006_life_events.sql calls it "the one table in the schema holding what the user tells us about their own life rather than what the ephemeris computes, so it is owner-only in both directions": one policy for all operations, with using and with check both on user_id = auth.uid(), and no admin read policy. 

-- supabase/migrations/0006_life_events.sql
create table public.life_events (
  id uuid primary key default gen_random_uuid(),
  user_id uuid not null references auth.users(id) on delete cascade,
  occurred_on date not null,
  precision text not null default 'day' check (precision in ('day', 'month', 'year')),
  title text not null check (char_length(title) between 1 and 120),
  note text check (char_length(note) <= 2000),
  source text not null default 'manual' check (source in ('manual', 'extracted')),
  created_at timestamptz not null default now()
);
create index on public.life_events (user_id, occurred_on);

occurred_on is a date, not a timestamp, because "people remember 'March 2021', not an hour, and a false hour would imply the chart says more than it can." precision records how much of the date the user knew; a month-precision event is stored on the 1st, a year-precision one on 1 January, and the precision travels with it. source separates a typed moment from a confirmed extraction, "so a bad extraction run can be undone without touching hand-entered events."

life_event_scans is one row per user (user_id as primary key, scanned_at, found_count), so the offer to mine the chat history appears once, and again only when the user has said enough new things since.

period_readings, from 0007_period_readings.sql, holds the written meanings: one row per mahadasha plus one whose lord is the literal now, keyed on (user_id, lord, period_start). The now row's period_start is the current antardasha's start, "so it goes stale by itself when the sub-period changes." events_hash records which pinned moments the text was written against. The column checks allow 80 and 800 characters; the parser trims to 60 and 600, so the database limit is headroom, never the thing that fails.

The types

// lib/timeline/build.ts
export type Timeline = {
  birthDate: string;
  today: string;
  periods: TimelinePeriod[];
  events: TimelineEvent[];
  now: TimelineNow | null;
  /** Any period, or the now summary, needs (re)writing. */
  needsExplaining: boolean;
};

TimelinePeriod is a DashaPeriod (lord, ISO start and end, nested antardashas) plus isCurrent, isPast, eventCount, theme, meaning and stale. TimelineEvent carries the stored columns plus the two fields that are "the whole point of the feature": mahadasha and antardasha, the lords running when it happened, or null when the event sits outside the computed span. TimelineNow names the current pair and the antardasha's span. The GET route spreads a ScanState (scanned, messagesSinceScan) over the top.

On iOS the same shape is TimelinePayload, decoding into DashaBand, LifeEvent and NowPeriod. Every field that arrived after the first release decodes with decodeIfPresent and a default, "so an app update and an API deploy need not land in the same minute." CandidateEvent is the scan's proposal: an optional occurredOn, a precision that may be unknown, a title, and the evidence quote.

8.4The server side

Building the map without the ephemeris

Vimshottari needs one number: the Moon's sidereal longitude at birth. The chart stores each planet as a sign plus a degree within it, so moonLongitudeFrom reassembles signIndex * 30 + degree: "This is what lets the timeline load without re-running the Swiss Ephemeris, which costs a WASM init per process." It returns null for a chart with no Moon or an unrecognised sign, and the caller treats null as "cannot draw."

loadTimeline in load.ts reads the birth row, reassembles the Moon, fetches life_events and period_readings in parallel, and hands everything to buildTimeline. today is the UTC date: "A dasha band is years wide, so a few hours of skew cannot move an event into the wrong period, unlike the daily readings, where the local date is the whole point."

buildTimeline calls vimshottariTimeline for the raw twelve periods. Each stored event is placed in the period whose [start, end) contains occurred_on, then in the antardasha inside it, and the list is sorted oldest first. Each period gets its event count, its current and past flags, and the stored reading for its (lord, start); the now summary is looked up under NOW_LORD = "now" and the antardasha start. Then: 

// lib/timeline/build.ts
const fresh = !!row && row.events_hash === eventsHashFor(p, events);
...
const needsExplaining = periods.some((p) => p.stale) || (now !== null && now.meaning === null);

A period is stale when it has no reading or when the moments inside it changed since the reading was written. The test is the hash: 

// lib/timeline/build.ts
export function eventsHashFor(period, events): string {
  const keys = events
    .filter((e) => within(e.occurredOn, period.start, period.end))
    .map((e) => `${e.occurredOn}:${e.title.toLowerCase()}`)
    .sort();
  if (keys.length === 0) return "0";
  let h = 2166136261;
  const text = keys.join("\n");
  for (let i = 0; i < text.length; i++) {
    h ^= text.charCodeAt(i);
    h = Math.imul(h, 16777619) >>> 0;
  }
  return h.toString(16).padStart(8, "0");
}

A 32-bit FNV-1a over the sorted date:title keys. Sorting first means the order rows come back from Postgres "can never make a fresh explanation look stale." A small non-cryptographic hash is enough because "this detects change, it does not protect anything," and keeping it pure "keeps this module free of Node-only imports."

Writing the meanings

explain.ts turns the map into one model call. buildExplainSystem addresses the user by first name, tells the model to "write for someone who has never heard the word dasha and will not be told it," and sets the rules that matter on a life map: past chapters in the past tense, the current one in the present, a future one "as what it tends to bring, never as fate, and never as a warning." When the user has pinned a moment inside a chapter, "name it and say in one clause how it fits the chapter. Never invent a moment they did not give you." Jargon is banned outright: no malefic, benefic, exalted, lord, house, transit, dasha, antardasha.

buildExplainTask lists every period as LORD | START | years (state), the current period's sub-periods, and each pinned moment under its period, ending with a now line. The reply is one line per chapter, LORD | START | THEME | MEANING, plus a final line whose LORD is the word now.

parseExplanations is the safety property. It builds a map of every legitimate lord|start key from the computed periods and the now row, and keeps a line only when its key is in that map: "a hallucinated period can never appear on the map." The first line for a key wins, the theme is cut at MAX_THEME = 60, and everything after the third pipe is the meaning, "pipes and all," cut at MAX_MEANING = 600.

explainTimeline calls READING_MODEL with max_tokens: 3000, the shared LOW_EFFORT output config, and the system block marked cache_control: { type: "ephemeral", ttl: "1h" }; any error returns an empty array. POST /api/timeline/explain (maxDuration = 90) asks the model only if needsExplaining, then upserts every returned row on user_id,lord,period_start, stamping each period's events_hash and "0" for the now row, whose freshness "is the antardasha start in its key." One stale period rewrites all of them: "the extra output costs less than a second call would." The route reloads and returns the GET shape, so the client replaces its state and never merges.

Mining the history

selectTranscript decides what the model sees: the user's messages oldest first, each prefixed with the date it was written, whitespace collapsed so one message stays one line, anything under 12 characters dropped, and a hard stop at maxChars = 24_000 that ends before a message rather than through it. Oldest first is deliberate: the early "here's my situation" messages are "where most life history gets told."

buildExtractionSystem says what counts (a job, a move, a marriage or separation, a birth, a death, an illness, a business begun or closed) and what does not: never an event inferred from a question, a hypothetical, or "something you read in an astrological reading." Relative phrases resolve from the message date ("last March" in a 2025-06 message is 2024-03), ages from the birth date ("when I was 25" is the birth year plus 25), always at the precision given. An event that cannot be dated even to a year is kept with ?. The title is in the person's own framing, "'Left the job in Chicago', not 'Career upheaval under Saturn'," and the model must quote the words that told it as EVIDENCE. The reply is DATE | PRECISION | TITLE | EVIDENCE, one per line, or NONE.

parseExtractedEvents trusts none of that. Pipe-delimited lines rather than JSON, "for the reason recorded in lib/alerts/compose.ts: the model reliably breaks JSON string escaping and the whole reply is lost, where a malformed line here costs one event." The date is resolved by shape (YYYY-MM-DD, YYYY-MM, YYYY) and checked to be a real calendar day; the PRECISION field is read and discarded, because "a model that writes '2019 | day | ...' does not know the day, whatever it claims in the second field." Anything before birth or after today is dropped, duplicates are removed on lower-cased date:title (title alone for undated events), titles are cut at 120 characters and evidence at 160. Dated candidates return oldest first, then the undated ones.

POST /api/timeline/scan (maxDuration = 120) reads only the user's own messages, at most MAX_MESSAGES = 400: "Assistant turns are full of astrological narrative that reads like biography and would extract as fiction." Section 8.6 walks through the rest.

Pinning

POST /api/life-events takes { events: [...] }, at most MAX_BATCH = 50, "one confirmation sheet's worth"; a manual add is the same call with one element. The date must match YYYY-MM-DD, must not be before the birth date ("That date is before you were born.") or after today ("The timeline only holds what has happened."), and the title must be non-empty, cut at 120; the note is cut at 2000. Precision falls back to day and source to manual. Dates are checked here as well as in the extractor "because this route is reachable directly, and an event before someone was born would render as a period that does not exist." DELETE filters on both the id and the caller's user_id, belt and braces over RLS.

The block the chat sees

describeTimelineForPrompt renders the timeline as text, or returns "" so the caller can append it unconditionally. In Vedic mode it opens with a Now: line naming the current period and sub-period, then lists up to MAX_MOMENTS = 30 moments, most recent first, each with the pair it fell in. Western readings have no dashas, so they get the moments alone, and nothing at all when there are none. app/api/chat/route.ts appends it to stableSystem in the chart branch (Vedic and Western) inside a try/catch that logs "chat timeline context skipped" and carries on. It lives in the cached block because "it only changes when they pin or remove a moment."

8.5The clients

The web app has no timeline screen. Everything below is the iOS feature, reached from the chat toolbar as a sheet (ChatView presents TimelineView()) and from a tap on the period widget, which opens sanchara://timeline and lands as DeepLink.Destination.timeline.

The store

TimelineStore is an @Observable, main-actor class with four states: loading, ready, needsProfile, failed(String). load() calls SancharaAPI.timeline(), applies the payload, and then, if needsExplaining, spawns explain() as a separate task, so the bands are on screen before the meanings exist. A failure there is silent, because "the timeline without meanings is still the timeline." A server reply containing "birth details" maps to needsProfile. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Timeline/TimelineStore.swift
static let rescanThreshold = 10
var canOfferScan: Bool { !scanned || messagesSinceScan >= Self.rescanThreshold }
var isRescan: Bool { scanned }

Never scanned: offer. Scanned, and at least ten user messages since: offer again with different copy. scan() sets scanned = true and messagesSinceScan = 0 locally on success, so the offer disappears even when nothing was found. confirm resolves the chosen candidates through CandidateResolver, posts them as one batch, clears the list and reloads. add is the same with one event. remove is optimistic: the row disappears under the finger, the delete is sent, and the reload restores it if the delete failed.

What is cached

Every applied payload also writes ChartCache.CachedPeriod: the current lord, the running antardasha and its span, the period's theme and the now row's meaning, then calls WidgetRefresh.reloadPeriod(). TimelineStore.warmCache() does the same fetch without a screen, and ChatView runs it once per launch so "the widget fills in without the timeline ever being opened." The widget, the shared PeriodProgress helper and the App Group file are chapter 10's; the timeline is the one writer of that file's period entry.

The views

TimelineView is titled "Your life." Bands are laid out "in information proportion, not time proportion: a period is as tall as what happened in it. A 20-year mahadasha with nothing pinned should not dwarf the year the user got married." Time is carried by the year range and the "N years" caption. From the top: the scan offer when canOfferScan; the NowCard when there is a now; one BandRow per period; a footer naming the technique and offering "What are these periods?"

NowCard is "the one place the meaning of now is spelled out": the pair, the years, a progress capsule, and the now meaning, or "Writing what this means for you..." while isExplaining. BandRow shows the lord, a NOW pill on the current period, the years, the theme, and the moment count; expanding reveals the meaning and the sub-periods. A stale meaning is shown at 60 percent opacity, "dimmed, and about to be replaced." Future periods sit at 45 percent because they "recede rather than disappear." Each EventRow shows the title, the date at the precision given, the "during" label naming the pair, and a Remove item behind a confirmation dialog.

AddMomentView asks for a title, a date bounded from birth to today, a segmented precision ("That day", "That month", "That year") and an optional note, and on save snaps the date to the precision so the server "stores a real date either way; this keeps it from implying a day the user never gave." PeriodExplainerView is five static paragraphs, "static on purpose," so the page explaining the idea can "be trusted the way a caption is trusted."

CandidatesView is where proposals become moments, and its doc comment is the policy: "Nothing reaches the timeline without passing through here. These are inferred claims about someone's own life, a wrong one is not a wrong prediction, it is the app telling a person something untrue about their father's death, so every candidate is opt-in rather than opt-out." Each row shows the title, its date or "When was this?", and the evidence quote in italics. Dated candidates arrive pre-ticked ("the work of confirming a good list should be one tap, not twelve"); undated ones show a year menu from the birth year to this year instead. The add button is live only when CandidateResolver.canAdd says so: something is chosen and every chosen undated candidate has a year.

TimelineDate pins every formatter to UTC, because "parsing in the device's zone would slide a morning event onto the previous day west of Greenwich," and LifeEvent.dateLabel picks a day, month or year formatter by precision "so a year-precision memory never claims a day it does not have."

8.6How it works

Opening the timeline

  1. TimelineView.task calls store.load(), which calls GET /api/timeline. The route loads the timeline and the scan state in parallel; a missing chart or an unreadable Moon answers 400 with "Complete your birth details first."
  2. The store applies the payload, writes the period cache, and shows the bands. If needsExplaining it starts explain() in the background.
  3. On appear, if there is no now card the scroll jumps to the current band: "'where am I now' is the question people arrive with."

Writing meanings

  1. explain() calls POST /api/timeline/explain. The route checks needsExplaining itself, so a needless call costs two reads and no model call.
  2. explainTimeline runs; parseExplanations keeps only lines naming a real period or the now row; the rows are upserted with their hashes.
  3. The route returns the GET shape, the store applies it, and the card that said "Writing what this means for you..." shows the now meaning.

Because the now row is keyed on the antardasha start, the day a sub-period turns over the lookup finds no row, now.meaning is null, needsExplaining is true, and the next open rewrites the map with a fresh now line. No cron and no expiry column; the key is the expiry.

Pinning a moment by hand

  1. The plus button opens AddMomentView, bounded to the birth date and today the store already holds.
  2. Save snaps the date to the chosen precision and posts one NewLifeEvent with source: "manual"; the route validates against the birth row again and inserts.
  3. The store reloads. The new moment sits in its band; that band's hash no longer matches its reading, so it is stale, needsExplaining is true, and the reload's explain() rewrites every meaning, this time naming the moment.

The scan

Following the numbered arrows in the figure:

  1. The offer's button calls store.scan(), with a 120-second timeout on the phone and maxDuration = 120 on the server.
  2. The route loads the birth row; without one it answers 400.
  3. It selects the user's conversation ids, then their own messages, oldest first, at most 400. No conversations short-circuits: the scan is recorded with found_count = 0 and an empty list returns.
  4. selectTranscript trims to 24,000 characters; extractLifeEvents sends them at max_tokens: 2000. An empty transcript returns before any call.
  5. The model replies with pipe-delimited lines or NONE.
  6. parseExtractedEvents validates, deduplicates, trims and orders.
  7. The route drops any candidate whose lower-cased occurred_on:title is already pinned. An undated candidate "has no date to match on, so a pinned moment with the same name is taken to be the same moment."
  8. recordScan upserts life_event_scans on user_id; an error here is logged, not raised.
  9. The candidates come back with nothing written to life_events; the store's non-empty list presents CandidatesView.
  10. The user unticks what is wrong, picks a year for anything undated, and taps add. confirm posts the batch with source: "extracted" to /api/life-events and reloads.

What makes the scan idempotent

Pressing the button twice, or a week later, cannot damage the map, for four reasons that stack. The scan route never inserts into life_events; its only write is the one-row-per-user upsert into life_event_scans, which overwrites rather than accumulates. The parser deduplicates within a reply. The route filters against what is already pinned, so "a re-scan after more chatting proposes only what is genuinely new." And the store clears its candidate list after a confirmation or a cancel, so a stale sheet cannot be re-submitted. The one thing not guarded is a user who confirms the same candidate in two separate sheets: life_events has no unique constraint, and the next scan would suppress the candidate, not the duplicate row. That is the user's own action on their own data, and Remove is one long press away.

The chat sees the timeline

On every chat turn in a chart mode the route calls loadTimeline and appends describeTimelineForPrompt to stableSystem. That block is part of the cached prefix, so "why was 2021 so hard" is answered against a prompt that lists what the user pinned in 2021 and which pair it fell in. When a moment is pinned or removed the block changes and the cache misses once; until then it is free. A failure to load is logged and the turn proceeds without it.

8.7Performance and cost

  • No ephemeris on the read path. moonLongitudeFrom reassembles the Moon from the stored chart, so GET /api/timeline is two parallel Postgres reads plus arithmetic. The WASM engine is never initialised for the timeline.
  • One model call per rewrite, not per period. Twelve periods and the now line come from a single max_tokens: 3000 call at low effort, with the system prompt cached for an hour. Rewrites happen only when the hash or the now key changes; opening the timeline ten times a day with nothing new makes zero model calls.
  • The scan is bounded three ways. 400 messages from the database, 24,000 characters to the model, 2,000 output tokens back, and only user turns, which are the shorter half of any transcript.
  • The chat pays two reads per turn. loadTimeline runs on every chart turn to build the block, which costs input tokens only when it changes.
  • The phone caches one thing. The period entry for the widget. The map itself is a few kilobytes, fetched on every open, always current.
  • Long timeouts where earned. Explain and scan carry a 120-second client timeout and 90- and 120-second maxDuration on the server; both run longer than a chat turn.

8.8Edge cases and tests

Tests

Vitest, in lib/timeline/, 47 tests, none needing a database or a model:

  • build.test.ts: twelve periods for a full life; exactly one current period; an event placed in its mahadasha and antardasha and counted into its period; sorted oldest first regardless of insertion order; null periods beyond the span; the precision and source fallbacks; moonLongitudeFrom reassembling Taurus 15.5 as 45.5 and returning null for a missing Moon or "Ophiuchus".
  • describe.test.ts: the Vedic block names the current period and each moment's pair; the Western block has moments but no dasha language, and is empty with none; exactly thirty most recent moments; dates at the precision given.
  • explain.test.ts: the hash is stable in any order, is 0 for an empty period, and ignores moments outside; the parser reads a period line and the now line, drops lines that do not name a real period, keeps pipes in the meaning, trims to 60 and 600, and skips a line missing either; the prompts list every lord and the pinned moment.
  • extract.test.ts: NONE; the date shape trusted over a wrong PRECISION field; events outside the person's life and impossible dates dropped; malformed lines skipped without losing good ones; a fourth field read as evidence; an undated event kept after the dated ones; selectTranscript stamping the date, dropping short messages and stopping at the budget; the prompt containing "birth year plus 25" and "write ? for the date".

XCTest, in mobile/Sanchara/Tests/:

  • TimelineTests: a band's length in whole years; progress clamped outside the period and zero for a zero-length band; the "during" label for a pair, a mahadasha alone, and neither; a date rendered only to the precision given ("a year label must not leak a day or month number"); malformed dates parsing to nil rather than today.
  • CandidateResolverTests: a dated candidate passes through unchanged; an undated one needs a year and becomes 1 January at year precision; add is blocked until every chosen undated candidate has a year; the year range clamps a clock set before the birth date; the payload decodes with and without the explain fields.
  • PeriodProgressTests: the shared fraction at start, midpoint and end, clamped; years remaining never negative; DashaBand.progress agreeing with the helper; the cached period surviving a round trip through ChartCache and removed by clear().

8.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/astrology/dasha.ts, for vimshottariTimeline and why the first period is a balance.
  2. lib/timeline/build.ts, the join, the now key and eventsHashFor.
  3. lib/timeline/load.ts, how the Moon is recovered and why today is UTC.
  4. lib/timeline/explain.ts and app/api/timeline/explain/route.ts, the prompt rules, the parser and the upsert.
  5. lib/timeline/extract.ts and app/api/timeline/scan/route.ts, the transcript, the parser that trusts nothing, and the duplicate filter.
  6. app/api/life-events/route.ts, the second validation of every date.
  7. lib/timeline/describe.ts and the block in app/api/chat/route.ts.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Timeline/TimelineStore.swift, the tolerant decoders, CandidateResolver and the store.
  9. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Timeline/CandidatesView.swift, the confirmation policy.

Check yourself

Why can the timeline load without the Swiss Ephemeris when every other chart feature needs it?

Vimshottari needs only the Moon's sidereal longitude at birth, and the stored chart carries the Moon's sign and degree. moonLongitudeFrom reassembles signIndex * 30 + degree, which saves a WASM init per process.

How does the "where you are now" meaning expire when the sub-period changes, with no expiry column?

Its row is keyed on the antardasha start. When the sub-period turns over the lookup uses a new start, finds no row, and needsExplaining becomes true.

Why are the events sorted before hashing?

Postgres does not promise an order, and a different order would give a different hash for the same moments, making a fresh reading look stale and costing a needless model call.

Why does the scan route never write to life_events?

The candidates are an inference about someone's own life, and a wrong one about a death or a divorce would be filed as fact. Every candidate passes through CandidatesView, and only the user's confirmation calls /api/life-events.

Why is the PRECISION field read and then discarded?

The date's own shape is the evidence. A model that writes a year and claims day precision does not know the day; trusting the shape keeps the map from drawing a marker it cannot justify.

Chapter nine

9Daily readings and dosha alerts

Twice a day a scheduled job writes each person a reading for the half of the day they are in, and sends a push only when something in their sky actually changed. The clock is the user's, not the server's; the astrology is decided in code, not by the model; and a condition that merely continues is never announced twice.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It is the one feature in Astrya that runs with nobody looking. A chat route that misbehaves is seen and retried by the person in front of it; a cron job that misbehaves wakes people at night, bills a model call nobody asked for, or says "Mangal Dosha has begun" to someone whose chart has not changed since the day they were born. Most of the design below exists to make each of those impossible rather than unlikely.

9.1Intent

Two things are pushed to a person, both written from their own chart against today's sky. A reading, morning and night, kept by date so the app shows an archive rather than a notification that vanishes. And a dosha alert, sent only when an affliction starts or ends: a Sade Sati stepping from rising to peak, Saturn leaving the eighth from the Moon, a node arriving over the ascendant. A standing condition is recorded and shown, but it does not nag.

Three decisions shape everything else. The slot a reading belongs to is decided by the user's local hour, so one fixed UTC schedule serves every timezone. The dosha engine (lib/astrology/doshas.ts) decides deterministically which conditions hold, and Claude only writes the words. And natal conditions, fixed at birth, never alert after a user's first read, because a change in one is an artifact of the computation moving under them and never an event.

9.2Files and folders

app/api/cron/
├── daily/route.ts               the scheduled job: readings, then the dosha sweep
└── alerts/route.ts              the older entry point; runs only the dosha sweep

app/api/devices/
├── route.ts                     POST registers a device token, DELETE removes it
└── route.test.ts                session user wins over the body, environment defaults

lib/alerts/
├── slots.ts                     morning or night from the local hour; is the slot due
├── predictions.ts               the twice-daily reading, one per user per slot
├── run.ts                       the dosha sweep: diff, record, partition, alert, push
├── compose.ts                   the alert copy, the reply parser, the fallback
└── *.test.ts                    slots, partitioning, parsing, fallbacks

lib/push/apns.ts                 token-based APNs client over HTTP/2
lib/astrology/doshas.ts          detectConditions, the signatures the sweep diffs
vercel.json                      the two cron schedules

supabase/migrations/
├── 0004_alerts_push.sql         device_tokens, transit_conditions, alerts
└── 0005_daily_readings.sql      daily_readings

mobile/Sanchara/Sources/
├── Services/Push.swift          PushStore, the app delegate, ApsEnvironment
├── Features/Alerts/
│   ├── DailyStore.swift         DailyReading, grouping by date, the widget cache
│   ├── AlertsStore.swift        SancharaAlert, open, markRead
│   ├── InboxView.swift          the bell sheet: Daily and Alerts tabs, DailyReadingView
│   └── AlertsView.swift         AlertDetailView, severityColor
└── Features/Chat/ChatView.swift where a tapped push becomes an open sheet

mobile/Sanchara/Tests/
├── ApsEnvironmentTests.swift    the entitlement parser
└── DailyReadingTests.swift      grouping and labels

docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md              the one-time setup: APNs key, env vars, entitlement

The split inside lib/alerts is by what can be tested without a network. slots.ts is two pure functions; compose.ts exports its parser and fallback separately from the model call; run.ts pulls its two hardest decisions out into partitionForAlerts and deriveHadNatalRows. Everything that touches Supabase or APNs stays in the orchestrating functions.

9.3Model

A condition and its signature

// lib/astrology/doshas.ts
export type Severity = "info" | "caution" | "warning";

export type Condition = {
  kind: string;
  signature: string;
  label: string;
  severity: Severity;
  scope: "natal" | "transit";
  /** A plain factual sentence, the grounding the model is allowed to interpret. */
  detail: string;
};

The signature is the whole mechanism. It folds in the phase and the sign involved, so that Saturn moving on, or Sade Sati stepping from rising to peak, reads as a new condition rather than the same one continuing. Natal detectors produce signatures such as mangal_dosha:7 or kaal_sarp:<sign>; transit detectors produce sade_sati:<phase>:<sign>, kantaka_shani, node_over_moon_<node>, ashtama_surya, guru_6_8_12, retrograde_<planet> and the rest, each suffixed with the sign that makes it specific. The sweep never compares labels or kinds. It compares signatures against the set of open rows, and the difference is the news.

The copy

// lib/alerts/compose.ts
export type AlertCopy = {
  title: string;
  body: string;
  detail: string;
};

// lib/alerts/slots.ts
export type Slot = "morning" | "night";
export const NIGHT_STARTS_AT_HOUR = 14;

One copy type serves both a reading and an alert: a title, one plain sentence for the lock screen, and a markdown detail ending in a section titled "In simple words". The prompt asks for under 38 characters of title and under 140 of body; the parser cuts at 60 and 200, so a reply a few characters over is trimmed rather than discarded.

Four tables

TableColumns that matterUniquenessApp may
device_tokenstoken (primary key), user_id, platform (only ios), environment (sandbox or production), updated_atthe token itselfread, write and delete its own rows, though the route uses the admin client (see 9.4)
transit_conditionskind, signature, label, severity, scope, detail, started_on, ended_onpartial unique index on (user_id, signature) where ended_on is nullread its own rows
alertsseverity, kinds text[], title, body, detail, created_at, read_atnone beyond the idread, and update (to mark read)
daily_readingsfor_date, slot, tradition (default vedic), title, body, detail, created_at, read_atunique (user_id, for_date, slot)read, and update (to mark read)

Every table has Row Level Security on, and every policy is owner-only. The jobs write with the service-role client, which bypasses RLS; the policies let the app read its own inbox and mark things read through PostgREST with no route handler in between. transit_conditions is the state the feature hangs on: a row is open while ended_on is null, and the partial unique index allows one open row per signature per user, so two runs racing cannot open a condition twice. daily_readings.for_date is the user's own local date, with the migration's reason: "my reading for the 26th" has to mean their 26th.

Why ended_on and not a delete A closed row is history. The unique index is partial precisely so a condition can end, stay on record with its dates, and open again under the same signature when Saturn returns. The table is a ledger of every affliction the engine has seen, not a set of current ones.

9.4The server side

Two routes, one current

// vercel.json
"crons": [
  { "path": "/api/cron/daily", "schedule": "30 2 * * *" },
  { "path": "/api/cron/daily", "schedule": "30 15 * * *" }
]

Both schedules hit /api/cron/daily, at 02:30 and 15:30 UTC, which is 08:00 and 21:00 in India. The handler runs runDuePredictions(), then runDailyAlerts(), and answers with both summaries. /api/cron/alerts is the older route: it runs only the dosha sweep, is not named in vercel.json, and survives for running that half by hand. Its docstring still says the cron fires it, and the comment in 0004_alerts_push.sql names it too; both are out of date. Read vercel.json as the truth about what is scheduled. 

// app/api/cron/daily/route.ts
export const runtime = "nodejs";
export const maxDuration = 300;

export async function GET(request: Request) {
  const secret = env.cronSecret();
  if (!secret || request.headers.get("authorization") !== `Bearer ${secret}`) {
    return new Response("Unauthorized", { status: 401 });
  }
  ...
}

Vercel sends Authorization: Bearer $CRON_SECRET on scheduled invocations, and the same header runs the job by hand with curl. A missing secret refuses everything; "no secret means open" would expose a job that spends model tokens for every user to anyone who finds the path. The runtime is Node because the Swiss Ephemeris runs as WebAssembly, and the five-minute maxDuration is the budget for up to 500 users, each of whom may cost a chart computation and a model call.

Slots

// lib/alerts/slots.ts
export function slotForHour(localHour: number): Slot {
  return localHour < NIGHT_STARTS_AT_HOUR ? "morning" : "night";
}

export function isSlotDue(localHour: number, alreadyWritten: Slot[]): Slot | null {
  const slot = slotForHour(localHour);
  return alreadyWritten.includes(slot) ? null : slot;
}

Anything before 14:00 local is that day's morning reading; anything after is that night's. isSlotDue asks one question: does this user still need the reading for the slot they are in right now? It does not backfill. A user in the evening with no morning reading gets tonight's, not a stale "good morning" hours late, which the test does not backfill a slot the user has moved past pins.

The reading

runDuePredictions selects every birth_profiles row with a computed chart, up to 500, and calls writeDueReading for each inside its own try; one user failing is counted and logged, and the loop continues. Per user:

  1. The Vedic chart is required; a profile without one counts as skipped.
  2. The zone is profile.timezone, or UTC when null. nowLocal and forDate come from Luxon in that zone.
  3. The slots already written for that user and date are read from daily_readings, and isSlotDue(nowLocal.hour, slots) decides. Null means nothing to do.
  4. transitChart computes today's sky at the user's birthplace, and detectConditions(chart, transits) finds the same afflictions the alert job tracks, so the reading and the alerts never contradict each other.
  5. composePrediction writes the copy and the row is inserted with tradition: "vedic". A unique violation (Postgres code 23505) is returned as null, "already done", not thrown: the index is the safety net for two runs racing.
  6. The reading is pushed to every device with kind: "daily" and a thread id of sanchara-daily-morning or sanchara-daily-night.
// lib/alerts/predictions.ts
const flagged = args.conditions
  .filter((c) => c.severity !== "info" && c.scope !== "natal")
  .slice(0, 4)
  .map((c) => `- ${c.label}: ${c.detail}`)
  .join("\n");

const stable = buildChartSystem({ firstName, tradition: "vedic", chart, derived: factsFor(chart), numerology });
const todayBlock = buildTodaySystem({ today, transits, maxWords: 150 });
...
system: [
  // Identical for this user on every run, so it is a cache read whenever
  // two of their readings land within the hour.
  { type: "text", text: stable, cache_control: { type: "ephemeral", ttl: "1h" } },
  { type: "text", text: todayBlock },
],

The prompt has three parts. The stable block is the same chart system prompt the chat uses, with the derived facts and numerology, marked for a one-hour prompt cache. The today block is the gochara description, called with includeAfflictions: false because the job lists the flagged conditions itself; asking for them twice was the double-listing fixed in 0.3.0. The task is one of two briefs, morning ("look forward, not back") or night ("reflect on the day, then say what tomorrow morning opens with"), followed by at most four flagged conditions, to be worked in only where they bear on the day and without alarm. Natal conditions are left out of that list for the same reason they never alert: they are not about today. The model is READING_MODEL (claude-sonnet-5) at LOW_EFFORT with 1,600 output tokens. If the call fails or the reply cannot be parsed, fallbackPrediction stores a plain reading that greets the user by first name and points at the app, so the slot is never empty.

The dosha sweep

runDailyAlerts has the same outer shape: the same profile query, a per-user try, and a summary of processed, alerted, pushesSent, failures and pushConfigured. Per user, runAlertsForUser works in a fixed order, and the order is the correctness.

  1. Compute today's sky with computeChart at the user's latitude and longitude, the same way the chat route does. Then detectConditions(natal, transit), indexed by signature.
  2. Count the user's natal rows in transit_conditions. This is the only fact that decides whether a natal condition may alert, and it fails closed (below).
  3. Load the open rows (ended_on is null). started is every current condition whose signature is not open; ended is every open row whose signature is not current.
  4. Insert every started row with started_on as the user's local date, and close every ended row the same way. The code warns at each step that these are the raw, unfiltered lists: a natal filter inlined here would stop natal rows being written, and the same condition would re-detect as "starting" forever.
  5. Only now partition. partitionForAlerts keeps everything when the user had no natal rows (a first read, where the whole natal set is genuinely news) and otherwise routes natal conditions to silent. Then keep only caution and warning (NOTIFIABLE), and at most six (MAX_CONDITIONS_PER_ALERT), because past that an alert becomes a list.
  6. If nothing is left, return without an alert. Otherwise the severity is the highest among the started conditions, or the ended ones if nothing started; composeAlert writes the copy; one row goes into alerts with the kinds involved; and the push goes out.
// lib/alerts/run.ts
export function deriveHadNatalRows(result: { count: number | null; error: { message: string } | null }): boolean {
  if (result.error) return true;
  return (result.count ?? 0) > 0;
}

Supabase returns count: null alongside an error, so the naive (count ?? 0) > 0 would read a failed count as "no natal rows", treat an existing user as a first-time reader, and let their natal conditions through unfiltered. That is the bug the 0.3.0 move to whole-sign houses would have caused: the detectors shifted, some natal signatures changed, and "Mangal Dosha has begun" would have gone to people whose chart has not changed since birth. On error the function assumes the opposite of the naive read. It does not throw, because suppressing a natal alert costs nothing real (the condition still shows in the app and the next run reconciles), while aborting the user would drop their legitimate transit alerts for the day.

Composing the alert

composeAlert uses DEEP_READING_MODEL (claude-opus-4-8), again at low effort and 1,600 tokens; an alert is a title, a sentence and about 180 words, so the thinking budget is kept short on purpose. The system prompt gives the model a short chart summary (ascendant, Sun and Moon signs, the running dasha, every planet with sign and house), the conditions under "Newly active now" and "No longer active" with their factual detail lines, and four rules: never invent a placement, dosha, date or number not listed; no guaranteed outcomes and no medical, legal or financial guarantees; calm and practical, a heads-up and not doom; a remedy that is traditional and harmless. 

// lib/alerts/compose.ts
export function parseAlertCopy(text: string): AlertCopy | null {
  const title = text.match(/^\s*TITLE:\s*(.+)$/m)?.[1]?.trim();
  const body = text.match(/^\s*BODY:\s*(.+)$/m)?.[1]?.trim();
  const detailIndex = text.search(/^\s*DETAIL:\s*$/m);
  const detail = detailIndex === -1 ? undefined : text.slice(text.indexOf("\n", detailIndex) + 1).trim();
  if (title && body && detail) {
    return { title: title.slice(0, 60), body: body.slice(0, 200), detail };
  }
  return parseAlertJson(text);
}

The reply is line-delimited rather than JSON because detail is markdown. Asked for JSON, the model sometimes writes real newlines inside the string, which is not valid JSON, and the reply is lost; the comment records two of a batch of seven live readings coming back that way. Delimited text has no escaping to get wrong. JSON is still accepted as a second parse for the old shape. When neither parses, or the call throws, fallbackCopy still names the condition: "Sade Sati (peak phase) is active", or "has eased" when only endings remain.

The APNs client

lib/push/apns.ts talks to Apple directly over HTTP/2 with token-based authentication; there is no push library. isPushConfigured() is true only when all of APNS_KEY_ID, APNS_TEAM_ID, APNS_BUNDLE_ID and APNS_PRIVATE_KEY are set. Without them the jobs still write readings and alerts; they just send nothing, and the summary says pushConfigured: false. APNS_PRIVATE_KEY is the full text of the .p8 file; the env accessor turns literal \n sequences back into newlines so the key survives a single-line variable. 

// lib/push/apns.ts
const HOSTS: Record<PushEnvironment, string> = {
  sandbox: "https://api.sandbox.push.apple.com",
  production: "https://api.push.apple.com",
};

function providerToken(): string {
  const now = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  if (cachedToken && now - cachedToken.issuedAt < 45 * 60) return cachedToken.value;
  const header = base64url(JSON.stringify({ alg: "ES256", kid: env.apnsKeyId() }));
  const payload = base64url(JSON.stringify({ iss: env.apnsTeamId(), iat: now }));
  const key = createPrivateKey(env.apnsPrivateKey()!);
  const signature = signWithKey("sha256", Buffer.from(`${header}.${payload}`), {
    key,
    dsaEncoding: "ieee-p1363",
  });
  ...
}

The provider token is a JWT signed with the .p8 key: kid in the header, iss (the team id) and iat in the payload. Apple accepts one for an hour and rate-limits regeneration, so the module keeps it for 45 minutes across sends and warm invocations. The one subtlety is dsaEncoding: "ieee-p1363": ES256 wants the raw r and s pair, and Node signs DER by default. ApnsClient keeps one HTTP/2 session per environment, so a run that notifies many users pays for one TLS handshake; the session's error handler exists because an unhandled socket error would take the process down.

send posts to /3/device/<token> with apns-topic set to the bundle id, apns-push-type: alert and apns-priority: 10. The payload is an aps block (title and body, the default sound, a thread-id defaulting to sanchara-alerts, interruption-level: active) plus two custom keys the app reads on a tap: alert_id and kind. A ten-second timeout cancels the stream. The result reports unregistered: true on a 410 or the reasons Unregistered and BadDeviceToken; both jobs delete those tokens after the loop, because Apple only tells you a token is dead when you use it.

The job never chooses the environment. Each device_tokens row carries the environment the app reported at registration, and send opens the session for that row. A sandbox token and a production token for the same user coexist, each going to the only host that will accept it.

Registering a device

POST /api/devices takes { token, environment }. The token must match /^[0-9a-fA-F]{64,200}$/: APNs token length has grown over the years, so the route checks the alphabet and a sane range rather than pinning 64 characters. The environment is sandbox only when the body says exactly that; anything else becomes production. The row is upserted on token with platform: "ios" and the user id from the verified session, never from the body. It is written with the admin client on purpose: a device handed from one account to another has to change owner, and the owner-only RLS policy would correctly refuse that update. DELETE matches both the token and the session user, so one account cannot unregister another's device.

9.5The clients

The web app

There is no web surface for readings or alerts in 0.3.0: nothing under app/ or components/ reads daily_readings or alerts. The web app's part is the route handlers it hosts. The inbox is an iOS feature, and the phone reads the tables straight through PostgREST under RLS.

PushStore and the entitlement

Push.swift holds three things. PushStore is a @MainActor observable singleton with the device token, an authorization flag, and pending, the notification just tapped. SancharaAppDelegate exists because SwiftUI has no hook for APNs callbacks: it receives the token, turns a tap into a TappedNotification (an id and a kind, daily or alert, from the payload's custom keys), and shows a banner even when the app is open, since these are infrequent and time-relevant. And ApsEnvironment answers the one fact the server needs right about a device. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Push.swift
static func parse(profileText: String) -> String {
    guard let open = profileText.range(of: "<plist"),
          let close = profileText.range(of: "</plist>", range: open.upperBound..<profileText.endIndex)
    else { return sandbox }
    let plist = String(profileText[open.lowerBound..<close.upperBound])
    guard let data = plist.data(using: .utf8),
          let root = try? PropertyListSerialization.propertyList(from: data, format: nil) as? [String: Any],
          let entitlements = root["Entitlements"] as? [String: Any],
          let value = entitlements["aps-environment"] as? String
    else { return sandbox }
    return value == production ? production : sandbox
}

The environment is read from the signing entitlement, not the build configuration. The 0.2.0 changelog records why: a Release build run from Xcode still carries aps-environment: development and mints a sandbox token, and reporting "production" for it made every push to that device fail silently. So the app reads embedded.mobileprovision from its own bundle, which is what APNs reads too. That file is a CMS-signed blob with a plist inside. Rather than parse CMS, the code decodes the bytes as Latin-1 (every byte maps to a character, so the binary envelope cannot make the decode fail), finds the span between <plist and </plist>, and reads the entitlements dictionary. production is returned only when the profile says exactly that; a simulator build has no profile and cannot receive pushes, so sandbox is the harmless answer for every failure. mobile/project.yml sets the entitlement to development; the README and the TestFlight workflow switch it to production before archiving, and the app reports whichever it finds.

Registration is asked for in ChatView, not at launch, with the reason in the code: by then the user has a chart, so "we'll tell you when a dosha starts" means something. requestAuthorization asks once and calls registerForRemoteNotifications; the delegate hands the token to received, which formats it as hex and calls register, which posts it with ApsEnvironment.current whenever there is a session. A failed registration is swallowed; the next launch retries. AuthStore.signOut calls unregister before the session is gone, so the next person to sign in on that phone does not receive the previous account's readings.

The two stores

DailyStore loads the newest 60 rows of daily_readings, ordered by for_date then created_at, both descending, and groups them into DailyReadingDay values: newest day first, morning above night within a day, each labelled "Today", "Yesterday" or a long date. After every successful load it saves the newest reading into the App Group cache as a CachedReading and calls WidgetRefresh.reload(), because the widget and the Siri snippet read this and neither can run the query themselves. AlertsStore loads the newest 50 alerts. Both stores expose unreadCount, open(id:) and markRead. open looks in the loaded list first and reloads only on a miss, because the push usually arrives before the app has reloaded. markRead writes read_at, the only write RLS allows the app, and a failure leaves the badge until the next load.

The inbox

InboxView is one sheet with two tabs, Daily and Alerts, each with an unread badge, so the bell means "things for you". The Daily tab is sectioned by day with a sun or moon glyph per row; the Alerts tab is a flat list with a severity dot (severityColor: the accent for warning, amber for caution, muted otherwise). A tap opens DailyReadingView or AlertDetailView, which render the markdown detail and end with one button, "Ask Astrya about this", that closes the inbox, starts a new reading and sends a question built from the title and body. The sheet's .task marks the item read as it opens.

9.6How it works

A scheduled run

  1. Vercel Cron sends GET /api/cron/daily with the bearer secret. The handler checks it and calls the two jobs in order: readings first, then the sweep.
  2. Each job opens one admin client and one ApnsClient, loads up to 500 profiles with a chart, and loops. The readings job decides a slot per user and writes and pushes only when it is due; the sweep diffs, records, partitions, and alerts only on a change.
  3. Both clients are closed in finally, and the handler answers with the two summaries. A top-level error becomes a 500 with a logged cause; a per-user failure is only a count.

What the two UTC runs mean in other places

The schedule was chosen for India, where the two runs land at 08:00 and 21:00. Everyone else gets whatever those two instants are on their own clock, run through slotForHour (offsets as of late September):

Zone02:30 UTC is15:30 UTC isReadings per day
India, UTC+5:3008:00, morning21:00, night2
London, UTC+103:30, morning16:30, night2
New York, UTC-422:30 the day before, night11:30, morning2
Los Angeles, UTC-719:30 the day before, night08:30, morning2
Tokyo, UTC+911:30, morning00:30 the next day, morning1
Sydney, UTC+1012:30, morning01:30 the next day, morning1

A user far enough east has both runs land in the morning half of two consecutive local days: the second writes tomorrow's morning reading just after midnight, and the next morning's run finds it written and skips. They never get a night reading. The London user's morning reading is written at 03:30 and waits on the lock screen until they wake. docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md names the remedy: on a Vercel plan that allows sub-daily crons, 0 * * * * makes every timezone exact at no extra model cost, because each hourly run writes only for users whose current slot is missing. The code is already written for that schedule; only vercel.json would change.

A condition starts

Saturn enters the sign twelfth from a user's natal Moon. detectConditions now returns a sade_sati signature carrying the phase and the sign, a warning with scope transit. No open row has that signature, so it is in started and is inserted with today's local date; it is transit, so the partition keeps it; it is notifiable; Opus writes a title, a sentence and a page; the alerts row is inserted with kinds: ["sade_sati"]; and every device gets the push in the sanchara-alerts thread. Tomorrow the same signature is open and current, so it is in neither list and nothing happens. When Saturn moves on, the row is closed, the next phase's signature opens, and the alert reads as a change of phase rather than a repeat.

A tap

The delegate's didReceive reads alert_id and kind from the payload and sets PushStore.shared.pending. ChatView observes it, clears it, switches inboxTab to match, calls the store's open(id:), and presents the inbox. Because open sets selected, the detail sheet is already up when the inbox appears.

9.7Performance and cost

  • Idempotent by construction. The unique index on daily_readings and the partial unique index on transit_conditions mean an extra run, a retry, or two runs racing write nothing twice. The route's own comment says it: extra runs mostly find nothing to do.
  • No model call for a skipped slot. The slot check happens before the transit chart and before the model, so a run that lands in an already-written half of a user's day costs one small query.
  • The chart block is cached. The stable system prompt carries cache_control with a one-hour TTL and is byte-identical for a user on every run, and it matches the chat's chart block, so a reading and a conversation in the same hour share it.
  • Sonnet for the reading, Opus for the alert. The reading runs twice a day for everyone; the alert runs only on a change, so the more expensive model is reserved for the rarer message. Both run at low effort with a 1,600-token cap.
  • One APNs session per environment per run, and one provider token per 45 minutes, shared across every device in the loop.
  • Dead tokens are pruned as they are found, so the loop does not grow with every uninstalled app.
  • Bounded work. Both jobs cap the profile query at 500 and the route at five minutes. There is no pagination; past 500 users with charts, the limit is the next thing to change.
  • The phone caches the newest reading in the App Group so the widget and Siri answer with no network, and the cache's isFresh keeps a widget from insisting on yesterday's morning reading.

9.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
No CRON_SECRET configuredBoth cron routes answer 401 to everyone, including Vercel. The job cannot be left open by omission
No APNs variablesReadings and alerts are still written and appear in the inbox; pushesSent is 0 and pushConfigured is false
Profile has no timezoneTreated as UTC for the slot and the stored date
Profile has no Vedic chartSkipped by both jobs; nothing is written
Second run in the same half of a local dayisSlotDue returns null; counted as skipped; no chart, no model call
Two runs race on the same slotThe insert fails with 23505 and is treated as "already done", not as a failure
Evening, and no morning reading existsTonight's reading is written; the morning is never backfilled
Model call fails or returns something unparseableA deterministic fallback is stored: a greeting for a reading, the condition's name for an alert. The slot or alert is never empty
Model replies in the old JSON shapeStill accepted by parseAlertJson, including inside a fenced or chatty reply
Natal condition's signature changes for an existing userThe row is opened and the old one closed, silently. No alert, no push
Natal count query failsFails closed: the user is treated as having natal rows, so natal conditions cannot alert; transit alerts still go out
A user's first sweepEvery standing condition, natal included, is news and may alert once, subject to severity
Only info conditions changedRecorded in transit_conditions; no alert row, no push
More than six notifiable changesThe first six are named; all are still recorded
One user throws mid-loopCounted in failures, logged with the user id; the loop continues
Apple says the token is unregistered or badDeleted after the loop; no retry
APNs does not answer in ten secondsThe stream is cancelled and the send reported as a timeout; the token is kept
Sandbox token sent to the production host, or the reverseCannot happen from the job: the host comes from the row, and the row's environment from the entitlement the app was signed with
Release build run from Xcode, or a simulatorReports sandbox: the embedded profile says development, or there is no profile at all
Body claims a different user_idIgnored; the session user owns the row
Device changes hands between accountsThe admin-client upsert moves the row to the new owner; sign-out unregistered it first anyway
Push arrives before the inbox has loadedopen(id:) misses, reloads, and finds it

Tests

On the server, five Vitest files, 24 tests, none of which touch a network, a database or the model:

  • lib/alerts/slots.test.ts: slotForHour splits the day at 14; isSlotDue returns the current slot when missing, null when written, and never backfills.
  • lib/alerts/run.test.ts: partitionForAlerts alerts natal conditions only on a first read and keeps transit conditions alertable either way; deriveHadNatalRows is false at zero, true at three, and true when the count query errors (the test is named "fails CLOSED").
  • lib/alerts/compose.test.ts: parseAlertCopy reads the delimited shape with newlines intact, still accepts JSON, returns null for a refusal, and rejects a reply missing its detail; parseAlertJson finds the object in a fenced reply; fallbackCopy names the condition and reads as relief when one has ended.
  • lib/alerts/predictions.test.ts: fallbackPrediction greets by first name, keeps the body under 140 characters, ends with "In simple words", and gives night a different title from morning.
  • app/api/devices/route.test.ts: 401 with nothing written for no session; 400 for a malformed token; the row is stored against the session user, not the body, with onConflict: "token"; an unknown environment defaults to production; DELETE filters by both token and user.

On the phone, two XCTest files:

  • ApsEnvironmentTests builds a fake profile with non-text bytes either side of the plist, as CMS wrapping produces, and checks that development is sandbox, production is production, a profile without the entitlement is sandbox, no plist is sandbox, a wrong-case value is not trusted as production, and the test bundle itself (no embedded profile, the simulator case) reports sandbox.
  • DailyReadingTests: newest day first, morning before night within a day, "Today" and "Yesterday" labels, and the slot flags.
What is not tested The orchestration in runAlertsForUser and writeDueReading, the diff against open rows, the APNs client and the JWT signing have no automated tests. They are exercised by calling /api/cron/daily with the secret and reading the summary, as docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md describes.

9.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/alerts/slots.ts, the whole timezone story in twenty lines.
  2. lib/astrology/doshas.ts, the header comment on signature, then one natal and one transit detector.
  3. lib/alerts/run.ts, top to bottom: partitionForAlerts, deriveHadNatalRows, then runAlertsForUser and the comments at each write.
  4. lib/alerts/predictions.ts, writeDueReading and the three-part prompt in composePrediction.
  5. lib/alerts/compose.ts, the rules in the system prompt and why parseAlertCopy is not JSON.
  6. lib/push/apns.ts, providerToken and send.
  7. supabase/migrations/0004_alerts_push.sql, for the partial unique index and its comment.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Push.swift, ApsEnvironment.parse and the comment above PushStore.environment.
  9. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Alerts/DailyStore.swift, group and the cache write in load.

Check yourself

Why is the slot decided by the user's local hour rather than by which cron fired?

One fixed UTC schedule then serves every timezone, and the same code works unchanged under an hourly schedule. The cron decides when to look; the user's clock decides what is due.

Why are natal rows still inserted into transit_conditions if they can never alert?

The diff is against open rows. A natal condition never recorded is absent from the open set on every run, so it would be in started forever. Recording it once is what makes it silent.

What does deriveHadNatalRows return when the count query fails, and why?

True. That routes natal conditions to silent, which costs nothing; the other direction is a push saying a birth condition began today.

Why does the reply use TITLE:, BODY: and DETAIL: lines instead of JSON?

The detail is markdown with real newlines, and the model sometimes writes them raw inside a JSON string, which does not parse. Delimited lines have no escaping to get wrong.

Where does the job learn which APNs host to use for a device?

From the environment column of that device's row, set at registration from the aps-environment entitlement in the app's embedded provisioning profile. Neither the job nor the build configuration guesses.

Why does the device route write with the admin client when RLS already lets a user manage their own tokens?

A phone that changes hands has to move its token to the new account, and an owner-only policy correctly refuses that update. The user id still comes from the session, never the body.

A user in Tokyo never gets a night reading. Why, and what fixes it?

Both UTC runs land before 14:00 local on two consecutive days, so both are morning slots and the second is skipped. An hourly cron fixes it with no code change, because isSlotDue already writes only what is missing.

Chapter ten

10On-device answers, Siri, and widgets

A birth chart is fixed at birth and about four kilobytes. Once the phone holds a copy, the questions that are lookups ("where is my Saturn", "what is in my seventh", "which dasha am I in") can be answered by the phone itself, out loud through Siri, on the Home Screen, or in the chat, with no session, no network and no paid call. One cache in an App Group makes all three surfaces possible.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. ChartCache writes the chart, the birth details, the latest daily reading and the current dasha period into the App Group container after every successful load in the app. The widget extension and the App Intents only ever read from it. On top of the cache sit Apple's on-device model, which restates the chart when a question is a retrieval and hands everything else to Claude, and a small deep-link protocol that lets a process with no UI tell the app where to land.

10.1Intent

A large share of what people ask Astrya is not interpretation. The doc comment at the top of mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/OnDeviceReasoner.swift puts it plainly: every one of those questions "was a paid Claude call answering from a chart the phone could have read itself". The same split runs through Siri. Positions and periods are records, so an intent answers them instantly and for nothing. Readings are written by Claude, so an intent can hand back one already written but never commissions a new one: a voice phrase should not silently spend money or take thirty seconds. The widgets follow the rule in its strictest form. They never fetch. A widget that waits on a network call is a widget that shows a placeholder at exactly the moment someone glanced at it.

10.2Files and folders

mobile/Sanchara/Sources/
├── Services/
│   ├── ChartCache.swift             the App Group cache: chart, details, reading, period, destination
│   └── WidgetRefresh.swift          asks WidgetKit to rebuild a timeline after a cache write
├── Features/Chat/
│   ├── ChartFacts.swift             the chart restated as neutral rows for the on-device model
│   ├── OnDeviceReasoner.swift       the router, the classifier and the lookup tool
│   ├── OnDeviceSuggestions.swift    follow-up questions written on the device, with starters
│   ├── ChatStore.swift              where a chat turn meets the router
│   └── ChatView.swift               the on-device footer, suggestion chips, deep-link handling
├── Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift  NatalChart, DerivedFacts, PeriodProgress (shared with the extension)
└── Intents/
