Nothing here has a web counterpart. The web app has no widget, no Siri surface and no on-device model; a question typed into components/Chat.tsx always goes to /api/chat. The iOS chat is the only client that routes.

10.3Model

What the cache holds

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift
final class ChartCache: @unchecked Sendable {
    static let shared = ChartCache()
    static let appGroup = "group.com.shivvyas.astra"

    private let queue = DispatchQueue(label: "com.shivvyas.astra.chartcache")
    private let directory: URL

    private init() {
        let shared = FileManager.default.containerURL(
            forSecurityApplicationGroupIdentifier: Self.appGroup
        )
        // Without the entitlement (a simulator build with no provisioning, say)
        // the app still works, it just stops sharing with the widget.
        directory = shared ?? URL.cachesDirectory
        try? FileManager.default.createDirectory(at: directory, withIntermediateDirectories: true)
    }

    private func write<T: Encodable>(_ value: T, to name: String) {
        queue.async {
            guard let data = try? JSONEncoder().encode(value) else { return }
            try? data.write(to: self.url(name), options: [.atomic, .completeFileProtection])
        }
    }

    private func read<T: Decodable>(_ type: T.Type, from name: String) -> T? {
        queue.sync {
            guard let data = try? Data(contentsOf: url(name)) else { return nil }
            return try? JSONDecoder().decode(type, from: data)
        }
    }
}

Four JSON files live in the group container, each behind a save-and-load pair. Writes are asynchronous on one serial queue and reads are synchronous on the same queue, so a read issued after a write sees the write. Every file is written atomically with complete file protection. Nothing secret goes in: no token, no session. Those stay in the Keychain, where supabase-swift already keeps them.

FileTypeWritten byRead by
chart.jsonNatalChart, the Vedic half of birth_profiles.chartProfileStore.loadChart()ProfileStore on launch, IntentData, PlacementQuery, PeriodProvider as a fallback
details.jsonBirthProfileDetailsProfileStore.load()ProfileStore on launch, IntentData
reading.jsonCachedReading: id, slot, title, body, forDate, savedAtDailyStore.load(), IntentData.fetchReadings()ReadingProvider, IntentData.latestReading()
period.jsonCachedPeriod: lord, antardasha, four ISO dates, theme, nowMeaning, savedAtTimelineStore.cache(_:)PeriodProvider

CachedReading.isFresh is true when savedAt is today or within the last 86,400 seconds: "A widget should not keep insisting on yesterday's morning reading." CachedPeriod carries what the chart alone cannot, the server's one-line theme for the mahadasha and the nowMeaning, which only /api/timeline writes. A fifth value is not a file. pendingDestination is one short string in UserDefaults(suiteName: appGroup), because it is written from the widget extension milliseconds before the app launches and the suite gives both processes the same store without coordination. takePendingDestination() reads and clears in one step, so a destination is never handled twice. clear() removes all four files and the key, and runs on sign-out.

The chart the phone reasons over

NatalChart in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift mirrors lib/astrology/types.ts: ascendant, nine ChartPlanet rows (name, sign, degree, house, retrograde, nakshatra), Moon and Sun signs, ayanamsa, an optional DashaInfo and an optional DerivedFacts. The derived block mirrors lib/astrology/derived.ts: per planet the houses it rules, its dignity, combustion and distance from the Sun, aspects and conjunctions; per house its lord and where that lord sits; per running dasha level the lord's placement. The top-level block is optional for the reason the type's comment gives: "A widget or a Siri intent must never fail to decode a chart it has already stored." Doshas are deliberately not carried into the app's type; they are interpretive, and the on-device model is only allowed to restate records.

The two entities

SancharaEntities.swift defines the only two things Siri, Shortcuts and Spotlight may know about. DailyReadingEntity is what a reading intent returns and what Spotlight indexes; its attributeSet puts the body in contentDescription so a half-remembered phrase finds the reading again. PlacementEntity is one body's position built from a ChartPlanet, with the Sanskrit sign from Rashi(english:), and a spoken sentence: "Saturn is in Meena, Pisces, at 3°15', in house 5, nakshatra Uttara Bhadrapada. It is retrograde." The file's comment sets the boundary: "Nothing interpretive is an entity. A prediction is not a record and should not be searchable as though it were one."

10.4The server side

There is none. No route handler, table or cron job exists for the widget, for Siri or for the on-device model. Everything they read was put in the cache by the app from three sources that belong to other chapters: the birth_profiles.chart jsonb column, read directly through supabase-swift under the owner-only RLS policy (roughly 4 KB, changing only when birth details are re-saved); the daily_readings table, read the same way; and GET /api/timeline, whose payload carries the period bands with their themes and the now meaning. The on-device model is Apple's, not Astrya's: FoundationModels is a system framework, no weights ship in the bundle, and no request leaves the phone. Where the model declines or is absent, the question goes to /api/chat exactly as it did before the feature existed.

10.5The clients

Filling the cache

Three stores write, each at the moment its data is confirmed. ProfileStore.load() first seeds chart and details from the cache so the kundli draws immediately, then fetches and saves both on success; if the fetch fails and the cache had both, the state is still .ready. DailyStore.load() saves the newest of 60 readings and calls WidgetRefresh.reload(). TimelineStore.cache(_:) finds the band marked isCurrent, picks the antardasha whose range contains payload.today, saves a CachedPeriod and calls WidgetRefresh.reloadPeriod(). Because the period file is only written when a timeline arrives, ChatView calls TimelineStore.warmCache() once per launch, so the period widget fills in without the timeline screen ever being opened. WidgetRefresh is two static calls to WidgetCenter.shared.reloadTimelines(ofKind:), for "SancharaReading" and "SancharaPeriod", and its comment carries the discipline: reloads are rate-limited by the system, "so this is called when a reading actually changes, never on every load."

ChartFacts: the chart as a reference table

ChartFacts turns positions into sentences and does nothing else. It has no dependency on FoundationModels, which is what makes it testable on any machine. The wording is the load-bearing part. Apple's on-device model refuses anything it reads as fortune telling, a result found by testing against the real model, so every row uses a neutral vocabulary and never names a tradition, a practice or a meaning:

Chart conceptWord in the rowExample
housesection"section 5"
nakshatrasegment"segment Uttara Bhadrapada"
lordshipcontrols, controlled by"Controls sections 7, 12"
dignitystrength ratingexalted "highest", debilitated "lowest", moolatrikona or own sign "high", otherwise "standard"
combustionwithin N degrees of Sun"Within 5 degrees of Sun"
aspect, conjunctionlinked to sections, filed alongside"Linked to sections 1", "Filed alongside Jupiter"
ascendant, Sun, Moonreference point, Entry A, Entry B"Reference point: Scorpio 14°30', which sets section 1."
mahadasha, antardashacurrent major period, current minor period"Current major period: Venus, running 1 April 2012 to 1 April 2032."

Four lookups exist. body(named:) returns one planet's row and demands at least two characters, because hasPrefix("") is true of every row and an empty argument from the model "would otherwise come back as whichever body happened to be first, a wrong answer delivered with complete confidence". section(_:) lists everything filed under one house, names its controller when derived facts exist, and says "No entries are filed under it." when nothing is. currentPeriod() renders both dasha levels with their date ranges and, from the derived block, where the running lord is filed. header() is the three lines every answer is indexed by, and everything() joins them all. Two helpers are shared beyond the file: sectionNumber(in:) finds a house number written as "seventh", "house 7", "7th house" or a bare "12th", and only accepts 1 to 12; calendarDay(_:format:) parses and formats a yyyy-MM-dd string in UTC, because formatting a bare ISO date anywhere west of Greenwich hands back the day before, "the kind of wrong that nobody reports and everybody notices."

OnDeviceReasoner: the router

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/OnDeviceReasoner.swift
func route(_ question: String) async -> Route {
    #if canImport(FoundationModels)
    if #available(iOS 26.0, *), Self.isAvailable {
        // A question that names no part of the table cannot be a lookup, so
        // the cheap check runs before the model does.
        guard Self.mentionsTheTable(question) else { return .escalate }
        guard await isLookup(question) else { return .escalate }
        if let answer = await answer(question) { return .answered(answer) }
    }
    #endif
    return .escalate
}

Three gates, cheapest first. mentionsTheTable is a string scan with no model call: true if the question contains one of the nine body names, a house number sectionNumber(in:) can find, or one of a short list of period and derived words ("dasha", "mahadasha", "antardasha", "period", "lagna", "ascendant", "rising", "nakshatra", "moon sign", "sun sign", "rashi", "lord", "ruler", "rules", "exalted", "debilitated", "combust"). The comment records two words tried and dropped, "aspect" and "strength": every question they usefully caught already routed via a body name or an ordinal, so they added only false positives such as "what aspects of my life".

isLookup is the first model call. It opens a LanguageModelSession on SystemLanguageModel(guardrails: .permissiveContentTransformations) with the instruction "You sort incoming questions into two buckets. You never answer them." and asks for a @Generable struct, Classification, whose single field is isRetrieval: Bool. Constrained decoding means the answer is always one of two values, never prose to be parsed; sampling is greedy. Any thrown error, a refusal included, returns false, and the question goes to Claude, "which is where it went before".

answer is the second model call, and it uses a tool rather than a stuffed prompt. TableLookupTool is registered as lookupTableEntry with four optional arguments: body (constrained to the nine names by .anyOf), section (constrained to 1...12), currentPeriod and wholeTable. The instructions tell the model to call the tool before answering, report only what it returned, say the table does not record something when the tool returns nothing, never explain what an entry means, and answer in two or three plain sentences. Generation runs at temperature 0.3 with at most 220 response tokens. Every tool result is prefixed with the header, because "which section is that" is the follow-up to almost every row. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/OnDeviceReasoner.swift
let text = response.content.trimmingCharacters(in: .whitespacesAndNewlines)
// An answer that never consulted the table is a guess. Drop it.
guard !text.isEmpty, tool.wasCalled else { return nil }
return text

wasCalled is a flag behind an NSLock, written on whatever executor the framework calls the tool from and read on the main actor afterwards. An answer composed without a single lookup is thrown away rather than shown.

Where the chat meets the router

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChatStore.swift
// A lookup the phone can answer from the chart it already holds
// never reaches the network. The opening reading and an explicit
// "read this properly" always do.
if !silent, !forceServer, mode == .vedic, let reasoner {
    if case .answered(let local) = await reasoner.route(text) {
        guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
        if let last = messages.indices.last, messages[last].role == .assistant {
            messages[last].content = local
            messages[last].source = .onDevice
        }
        isStreaming = false
        return
    }
}

ChatStore.chart has a didSet that builds the reasoner, and its comment limits the scope: "Vedic only: the router reads a Vedic chart, so a Western or numerology question has nothing local to answer from and goes straight to Claude." A locally answered turn is marked ChatMessage.Source.onDevice and is never saved to the conversation. ChatView renders it with a footer, "Read from your chart on this device", and under the last one a button, "Ask for a full reading", which calls expandLastAnswer(): the local answer is removed and the same question re-sent with forceServer: true. The disclosure and the escape hatch are both required; if the router guesses wrong, one tap undoes it.

OnDeviceSuggestions: what to ask next

SuggestionEngine owns the chips under a reading. ChatView calls refresh(after:mode:) whenever streaming stops with a non-empty assistant turn. On a device where SystemLanguageModel.default.availability == .available, the engine sends the last 1,200 characters of the reading (the tail carries the conclusion) to a session instructed "You rewrite and summarise text. You never add facts of your own, and you never answer the questions you raise." and asks for a @Generable FollowUpQuestions: three first-person questions the passage leaves unanswered, trimmed, capped at 90 characters, cut to three. The prompt never mentions astrology: asked to act as an astrologer's assistant the model refuses outright; asked to list what a passage leaves unanswered, it answers well. Everywhere else, and whenever generation throws, starters(for:) supplies three written questions per ChatMode, so the feature degrades rather than disappears. onDeviceStatus turns the availability enum into a sentence for the profile screen, and OnDeviceReasoner.isAvailable reads the same flag.

The intents

IntentAvailableOpens the appWhat it does
TodaysReadingIntentiOS 18noReturns a DailyReadingEntity from IntentData.latestReading() and speaks "title. body". Throws noReadingYet when there is none.
ShowReadingCardIntentiOS 26noThe same reading as a card: speaks the title and shows ReadingSnippetIntent, which renders ReadingCard. A separate intent because perform() has one concrete return type.
ChartPositionIntentiOS 18noTakes a PlacementEntity parameter ("Where is Saturn in my kundli") and speaks its sentence. The entity query already resolved it from the cache.
CurrentPeriodIntentiOS 18noReads IntentData.chart()?.dasha and speaks both levels with their end months in UTC. Throws noChartYet without a chart.
AskSancharaIntentiOS 26noFree-form question through OnDeviceReasoner.route; speaks an answer, or queues the question via DeepLink.shared.queue(question:) and says it needs a full reading.
OpenKundliIntentiOS 17yesWrites "kundli" to pendingDestination and returns. The only intent that opens the app, because "a diagram is the answer, and Siri has nowhere to put one."

Errors are sentences, not codes, because Siri reads them aloud: SancharaIntentError.noReadingYet says "There's no reading yet. Astrya writes one each morning and night." IntentData is the data layer for all of them: chart() and details() read the cache and nothing else, since a chart fixed at birth makes a cache hit always correct; latestReading() returns the cached reading if isFresh, otherwise the first row of a ten-row daily_readings query, which also rewrites the cache "for the widget, which cannot make this call". Nothing throws; a failed query is an empty list.

SancharaShortcuts is the AppShortcutsProvider: six AppShortcuts, the last two behind #available(iOS 26.0, *). Every phrase contains \(.applicationName), an App Intents rule that also covers a user who renames the app. "What's my reading in Astrya", "What dasha am I in on Astrya", "Where is my Saturn in Astrya" (the parameter is spoken inside the phrase), "Open my Astrya kundli", "Show my Astrya reading" and "Ask Astrya about my chart" all work with nothing set up. Ten is Apple's ceiling; the file's comment says fewer is better.

DeepLink and the sanchara:// scheme

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/DeepLink.swift
func drain() {
    guard let raw = ChartCache.shared.takePendingDestination() else { return }
    if raw == "kundli" {
        pending = .kundli
    } else if raw == "timeline" {
        pending = .timeline
    } else if raw.hasPrefix("ask:") {
        let question = String(raw.dropFirst("ask:".count))
        if !question.isEmpty { pending = .ask(question) }
    }
}

Intents run without a UI, sometimes in a different process, so none of them can present anything. They write a destination into the App Group and the app drains it when it next comes to the front, mirroring PushStore.pending on purpose: "one habit, two sources." A widget tap is the other way in. PeriodWidgetView sets .widgetURL(URL(string: "sanchara://timeline")), the scheme is declared under CFBundleURLTypes in mobile/project.yml, and RootView.onOpenURL hands a URL whose scheme is sanchara to DeepLink.shared.handle(url:) and everything else to the auth store, which would otherwise report an expired link. handle(url:) accepts the hosts timeline and kundli and ignores unknown paths rather than guessing. ChatView consumes the destination on onAppear (a cold launch sets it before the view exists), on scenePhase == .active (which calls drain()), and on onChange(of: DeepLink.shared.pending). .kundli and .timeline present the matching sheet; .ask starts a new reading and sends the question with forceServer: true.

The widget extension

// mobile/SancharaWidgets/ReadingWidget.swift
/// The next 6am or 6pm. Asking to refresh twice a day, rather than every
/// fifteen minutes, is what keeps the widget inside its energy budget while
/// still being right whenever anyone looks.
static func nextBoundary() -> Date {
    let calendar = Calendar.current
    let now = Date()
    for hour in [6, 18] {
        if let candidate = calendar.date(bySettingHour: hour, minute: 0, second: 0, of: now),
           candidate > now {
            return candidate
        }
    }
    let tomorrow = calendar.date(byAdding: .day, value: 1, to: now) ?? now.addingTimeInterval(86_400)
    return calendar.date(bySettingHour: 6, minute: 0, second: 0, of: tomorrow)
        ?? now.addingTimeInterval(43_200)
}

SancharaWidgets is the @main WidgetBundle in ReadingControl.swift. It vends ReadingWidget, PeriodWidget and, on iOS 18, ReadingControl. Both widgets use a StaticConfiguration and a provider whose timeline holds exactly one entry. ReadingWidget (kind SancharaReading; small, medium and accessoryRectangular) reads loadReading() and rebuilds at the next 06:00 or 18:00, the cadence of the content. The Home Screen families draw the shared ReadingCard with a body limit of 3 lines on small and 5 on medium; the Lock Screen family draws two lines of its own because the Lock Screen tints everything to one colour. The empty state names the next action: "Open Astrya to get your first reading."

PeriodWidget (kind SancharaPeriod; the same three families plus accessoryInline) prefers loadPeriod() and, failing that, the dasha pair inside the cached chart with no theme, "shown without a theme rather than as an empty state, the dates are true". It computes today as a UTC calendar day, the API's convention, and derives the bar and the "N years left" caption from PeriodProgress.fraction and yearsRemaining in ChartData.swift, which lives there "because the period widget needs it too, and this file is one of the few compiled into both targets". The timeline rebuilds after the next local midnight so the bar moves by a day when a day has passed. ReadingControl is a ControlWidget of kind SancharaReadingControl: one ControlWidgetButton whose action is OpenKundliIntent(), labelled "Kundli", displayed as "Open kundli". The type and kind keep "Reading" in their names and the file's leading comment still describes the reading; what the button does today is open the kundli, from the extension's own process.

What the extension compiles, and why each file is dependency-free

# mobile/project.yml
SancharaWidgets:

  sources:

    # Compiled into the extension as well as the app. Each of these is
    # dependency-free on purpose: pulling in anything that imports Supabase
    # would drag the whole client into a process that has no session to use
    # it with.
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Design/ReadingCard.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Kundli/ChartData.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/BirthProfileDetails.swift
    - path: Sanchara/Sources/Intents/OpenKundliIntent.swift
FileImportsWhy the extension needs it
Design/Theme.swiftSwiftUIThe four colour tokens the widget backgrounds and text use
Design/ReadingCard.swiftSwiftUIThe card both the widget and the Siri snippet draw

10.6How it works

A question in the chat

  1. ChatStore.send appends the user turn and an empty assistant turn, then, when the preconditions hold, awaits reasoner.route(text).
  2. mentionsTheTable rejects "should I take the job" without a model call. "Where is my Saturn" passes on the body name; "who rules my 7th" passes on the ordinal and on "rules". isLookup then escalates "what does my Saturn mean" as not a retrieval.
  3. answer opens a session with TableLookupTool. The model calls lookupTableEntry(body: "Saturn"), receives the header and Saturn's row, and restates them in two or three sentences.
  4. The assistant turn is filled and marked .onDevice; isStreaming goes false; SuggestionEngine.refresh runs on the answer like any other reading. Nothing was sent anywhere. If any gate fails, SancharaAPI.chatStream streams the reading from /api/chat and the turn is saved server-side.

A Siri phrase

  1. "Where is my Saturn in Astrya" matches the ChartPositionIntent shortcut. iOS resolves the spoken parameter through PlacementQuery.entities(matching:), which matches the lowercased string against the planet name or Sanskrit sign of each placement in ChartCache.shared.load(). perform() returns the entity and its sentence; the app is not opened.
  2. "Ask Astrya about my chart" runs AskSancharaIntent, which reads the chart from the cache and runs the same three gates. On .escalate it writes "ask:" + question to pendingDestination and says the question is saved. The next time ChatView becomes active, drain() turns it into .ask(question) and the view sends it with forceServer: true.
  3. "What's my reading in Astrya" runs TodaysReadingIntent. A fresh cached reading answers with no query; a stale one triggers a ten-row fetch that also rewrites the cache.

A widget refresh, a tap, and sign-out

  1. DailyStore.load() writes reading.json and calls WidgetRefresh.reload(). WidgetKit, within its rate limit, asks ReadingProvider.getTimeline for a new timeline; the provider reads the cache synchronously and returns one entry with a .after(nextBoundary()) policy. With the app closed, the night cron job's reading reaches the widget only after the app, or an intent's fetch, has rewritten the cache.
  2. A tap on the period widget opens sanchara://timeline; RootView recognises the scheme and handle(url:) sets pending = .timeline directly, with no App Group hop. A tap on the Control Center button runs OpenKundliIntent inside the extension, which writes "kundli" to the group's UserDefaults; openAppWhenRun = true brings the app forward, and ChatView presents the kundli sheet on onAppear or on becoming active.
  3. AuthStore.signOut() unregisters the push token, signs out of Supabase, then calls ChartCache.shared.clear(), because the cached files "are on disk in the app group, and the widget would go on showing them to whoever signs in next." The next widget rebuild draws the empty state.

10.7Performance and cost

  • Zero network on every read path. Widgets, entity queries and IntentData.chart() read a JSON file from disk synchronously. The only intent that can touch the network is a reading intent with a stale cache, capped at ten rows.
  • Model calls are gated by a string scan. The two on-device sessions run only for questions that name a body, a house or a period word, so an interpretive question costs nothing locally and goes straight to the server.
  • Every on-device answer is a Claude call not made, and it is never written to messages on the server, so there is no storage cost either.
  • Two widget rebuilds a day, plus one after midnight. Explicit reloads are sent only when a cache file actually changed.
  • Bounded generation. The lookup answer is capped at 220 tokens at temperature 0.3; the classifier is greedy and returns one Boolean; the suggestion prompt sees at most 1,200 characters.
  • No extension bloat. The widget binary compiles six app files and no package. Keeping the Supabase client out is what keeps its launch inside WidgetKit's budget.

10.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
No App Group entitlement (a simulator build without provisioning)ChartCache falls back to the caches directory. The app works; sharing with the widget stops.
Chart cached before derived facts shipped, or a server key the app does not declarederived decodes as nil and every row falls back to the position line; conditions is ignored and the decode succeeds.
Device west of GreenwichDates parse and format in UTC in ChartFacts.calendarDay, PeriodProgress and PeriodProvider.todayISO, so a dasha ending on the 1st never reads as the 31st.
Model refuses, is not downloaded, or Apple Intelligence is offisLookup returns false or answer returns nil; the question goes to Claude. The profile screen says which state the device is in.
Model answers without calling the tool, or passes a one-letter body nameDropped: wasCalled is false; body(named:) returns nil rather than the first row.
Router answers a question that wanted a reading"Ask for a full reading" removes the local answer and re-sends with forceServer.
Western or numerology modeNever routed; the reasoner only reads a Vedic chart.
Siri asked before any chart exists, or asked for meaningnoChartYet spoken as a sentence; an interpretive question is queued as ask: and sent to Claude on next launch. Siri never spends money or waits.
Widget added before any reading or timeline is loadedAn empty state naming the next action; the period widget draws the dasha pair from the cached chart with no theme.
Widget tap on a cold launch, or an unknown sanchara:// hostonAppear handles the destination set before onChange could fire; unknown hosts are ignored rather than guessed.
Sign-out on a shared deviceAll four files and the pending key are removed; the next widget rebuild shows the empty state.

Tests

All in mobile/Sanchara/Tests, all runnable on the simulator, none needing Apple Intelligence. The model itself is not tested: it cannot be on a machine without Apple Intelligence, and its answers are not deterministic. What is tested is everything that decides whether it runs.

  • OnDeviceRoutingTests: the cheap filter admits "where is my saturn", "what's in house 12", "what is my lagna" and the derived vocabulary ("which planet rules my chart", "is anything combust"), and rejects "should I take the job", "when will I get married" and the two dropped words. sectionNumber reads "7th house", "house 12", "my fourth house" and "the 1st", and rejects "my 40th birthday". A body row carries sign, degree, section, segment and retrograde; lookups are case-insensitive; "Pluto" and "" return nil; an empty section says so; the period row names both lords and "1 April 2032". testNothingHandedToTheModelNamesAstrology sweeps everything() for fourteen forbidden words including "house", "planet", "dasha" and "nakshatra". If the framing slips, the feature stops answering silently, and this is the test that notices.
  • ChartFactsTests: with a derived block, a body row says "Controls sections 7, 12", "Strength rating: highest" and "Linked to sections 1"; a combust Mars 4.6 degrees from the Sun renders "Within 5 degrees of Sun" and "Filed alongside Jupiter"; a section row names its controller; the period row names where the running lord is filed. Without a derived block every row still renders and the "controlled by" and "filed in section" sentences stay silent. A second forbidden-word sweep covers every row builder.
  • DerivedFactsTests: a chart JSON with no derived key decodes with it nil; one with the block decodes the planet, house and dasha values in place; an undeclared conditions key does not fail the decode; a DerivedPeriod with no placement decodes to nil.
  • SuggestionTests: every mode has three non-empty starters; where the model is unavailable refresh falls straight back to them with isThinking false; onDeviceStatus is never empty; where the model reports itself available, refresh settles within a minute on at most three usable questions.
  • PeriodProgressTests: testCachedPeriodSurvivesTheRoundTrip writes a CachedPeriod through the real cache and reads it back equal; testClearRemovesTheCachedPeriod proves clear() removes it.
A gate no test can run docs/SIRI_AND_WIDGETS.md section 6 describes a manual check against the real model on a device with Apple Intelligence on: six lookup questions, each of which must be answered on device, not refused, and restated rather than interpreted. As of this version the document marks its status as not yet run. The vocabulary in ChartFacts was arrived at by testing, and the table has grown since; the guardrail can trip back into "May contain sensitive content" on wording no unit test can see.

10.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift, for the container, the four files, the queue and the pending key. Everything else reads or writes through it.
  2. mobile/project.yml, the SancharaWidgets target, for the six app files the extension compiles and the comment on why.
  3. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChartFacts.swift, for the neutral vocabulary and the UTC date rule.
  4. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/OnDeviceReasoner.swift, for the three gates and the tool.
  5. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChatStore.swift, send and expandLastAnswer, for where the router meets a turn.
  6. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/SancharaIntents.swift then SancharaShortcuts.swift, for what Siri can do and the phrases.
  7. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Intents/DeepLink.swift and RootView.swift, for the two ways a destination arrives.
  8. mobile/SancharaWidgets/ReadingWidget.swift and PeriodWidget.swift, for the timeline policies and the fallbacks.
  9. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/OnDeviceRoutingTests.swift, which reads as the routing rules in plain English.

Check yourself

Why does the widget never fetch, even when its cache is a day old?

A widget that waits on the network shows a placeholder at the moment someone looks. The app and the intents refresh the cache; the widget only reads, and asks to be rebuilt at the next 06:00 or 18:00.

Which intent opens the app, and why is it the only one compiled into the widget extension?

OpenKundliIntent. A diagram is the answer and Siri cannot show one. The Control Center button runs it from the extension's process, so it must be compiled there, and it must import nothing but ChartCache.

Why does the on-device table say "section" and "segment" instead of "house" and "nakshatra"?

Apple's on-device model refuses anything it reads as fortune telling. Framed as a reference table of positions it answers; testNothingHandedToTheModelNamesAstrology pins the wording.

What happens to an on-device answer that never called lookupTableEntry?

It is discarded. wasCalled is false, answer returns nil, and the question escalates to Claude. An answer composed from memory is a guess.

Why is pendingDestination in UserDefaults rather than a JSON file like the rest of the cache?

It is one short string written from the extension milliseconds before the app launches; the shared suite gives both processes the same store with no coordination, and takePendingDestination reads and clears it in one step.

Why does a Siri question that needs interpretation get queued instead of answered?

Answering would mean a thin restatement or a paid call the person did not ask for and a wait Siri will not sit through. The question is written as ask: and the app sends it to Claude with forceServer on next launch.

Why must NatalChart.derived be optional?

A chart cached in the App Group before derived facts shipped has no derived key, and the widget and intent processes cannot report a decode failure; a required field would turn an old cache into a blank widget.

Chapter eleven

11Profile, account, and admin

The profile is the birth chart's front door: the details that computed it, a photo, the kundli, and the two account controls that end things. Beside it sits an admin area that can read everything about a user and change nothing.

11.1Intent

This chapter describes the profile, account deletion and the admin area as they are on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. The profile screen has one job on both clients: show the birth details on file and let a person change them or their photo. Changing the birth data recomputes the chart; that sentence appears verbatim on the web page and in the iOS header, because it is the one consequence a person should know before they edit. Account deletion exists because App Store Review Guideline 5.1.1(v) requires it, and it was built as one route that both clients call. The admin area exists so the author can see what a user's chart, profile and transcripts look like when something goes wrong, without a database console and without any way to alter a user's data from the browser.

Three decisions shape the code. A photo is optional and never travels alone: it rides as one part of the same multipart request as the birth details, and the server overwrites the stored avatar only when a photo part is present. Deletion takes no user id from the request, so a caller can only ever delete themself, and the database's cascades do the rest. And the admin has read access through the service-role client, but the only actions on offer send a password reset email or mint a one-time login link; nothing on any admin page writes to a user's rows.

11.2Files and folders

app/(app)/app/profile/page.tsx        the web profile: IntakeForm with initial values
app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts       saveIntake: avatar upload, then saveBirthProfile
app/api/profile/route.ts              POST: the same work for the phone, returns JSON
app/api/profile/route.test.ts
app/api/account/route.ts              DELETE: deletes the calling user
app/api/account/route.test.ts
lib/data/birthProfile.ts              BirthProfileRow, getBirthProfile, saveBirthProfile
components/IntakeForm.tsx             the form, including the photo control
components/AppShell.tsx               sidebar: Profile link and Sign out
components/AdminShell.tsx             admin sidebar: Users and Sign out
app/admin/
├── layout.tsx                        passthrough, force-dynamic
├── login/page.tsx                    email and password, nothing else
├── actions.ts                        adminSignIn, adminSignOut, sendPasswordReset, generateLoginLink
├── page.tsx                          the user list
├── users/[id]/page.tsx               one user: account, birth profile, chats
└── conversations/[id]/page.tsx       one transcript

├── guard.ts                          isAdminUser, requireAdmin (chapter 4)
└── data.ts                           adminListUsers, adminGetUser, adminGetTranscript

├── Features/Profile/AvatarBadge.swift    the top-bar control: photo or initials

├── Features/Intake/BirthProfileDetails.swift

mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ProfilePhotoTests.swift

The profile has no route of its own for reading. The web page reads through getBirthProfile with the cookie client, and the phone reads birth_profiles directly over PostgREST under the owner-only policy. Only writes go through a route or an action, because a write computes the chart with the Swiss Ephemeris and that runs on the server.

11.3Model

birth_profiles

One row per user, enforced by unique (user_id). The columns are the eight birth fields, the computed chart as jsonb, updated_at, and since migration 0002 a nullable avatar_url. user_id references auth.users(id) on delete cascade. 

// lib/data/birthProfile.ts
export type BirthProfileRow = {
  id: string;
  user_id: string;
  first_name: string;
  last_name: string;
  birth_date: string;
  birth_time: string;
  place_name: string;
  lat: number;
  lng: number;
  timezone: string;
  avatar_url: string | null;
  chart: { vedic: unknown; western: unknown } | null;
};

The iOS counterpart is BirthProfileDetails, a Codable struct whose coding keys map the snake-case columns. It deliberately omits the chart: ProfileStore fetches the details and the roughly 4 KB chart in two queries, the second only once the first says a chart exists. Two computed properties matter here. fullName joins and trims the names. birthTimeShort takes the first five characters of the Postgres time, which arrives as HH:mm:ss, giving the HH:mm shape the picker and POST /api/profile both use. The web page does the same with String(p.birth_time).slice(0, 5).

The avatar

The photo lives in a Supabase Storage bucket called avatars, at the path <user id>/avatar.<ext> where the extension is png, webp or jpg by content type. What the row stores is the bucket's public URL for that path with a ?v=<timestamp> query appended; the comment says "cache-bust so a replaced photo shows immediately". Nothing in the migrations creates the bucket or a storage policy: uploads use the service-role client, and reads are plain fetches of the public URL by the browser, AsyncImage, and the admin pages. The code therefore assumes the bucket exists and is public, which is a one-time dashboard step rather than a migration.

The upload contract

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift
struct BirthProfileInput {
    var firstName = ""
    var lastName = ""
    var birthDate = ""      // yyyy-MM-dd
    var birthTime = ""      // HH:mm
    var placeName = ""
    var lat: Double = 0
    var lng: Double = 0
    var timezone = ""
    /// Optional profile photo, already downscaled to JPEG. The route uploads it
    /// to the `avatars` bucket and only overwrites the stored URL when one is
    /// sent, so leaving this nil keeps the existing photo.
    var photoJPEG: Data?
}

multipartBody(boundary:) writes the eight text fields as form parts named exactly as the columns, then, only if photoJPEG is non-empty, a part named photo with filename avatar.jpg and content type image/jpeg. The same field names are what the web IntakeForm posts, with a file input named photo that accepts PNG, JPEG and WebP. One server contract, two producers.

admins and the admin rows

admins has two columns, user_id and created_at, and no write policy. AdminUserRow in lib/admin/data.ts is what the user list renders: id, email, created and last sign-in from Auth, first and last name, place, birth date and avatar from birth_profiles, and a conversationCount.

11.4The server side

Saving the profile

Two entry points do the same work. saveIntake in app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts is a server action the web form posts to; it reads the user from the cookie client, redirects to /login if there is none, uploads the photo if one was sent, calls saveBirthProfile, and redirects to /app. POST /api/profile exists because, as its comment says, a native client cannot invoke a server action. It does the same work with the bearer-aware client, validates the eight required fields and that lat and lng are finite numbers (400 otherwise), and returns { ok: true } instead of redirecting. Both contain an identical uploadAvatar. 

// app/api/profile/route.ts
let avatarUrl: string | null = null;
const photo = form.get("photo");
if (photo instanceof File && photo.size > 0 && photo.type.startsWith("image/")) {
  avatarUrl = await uploadAvatar(user.id, photo);
}
// lib/data/birthProfile.ts, inside saveBirthProfile
// Only overwrite the avatar when a new one was uploaded.
if (input.avatarUrl) row.avatar_url = input.avatarUrl;

const { error } = await supabase.from("birth_profiles").upsert(row, { onConflict: "user_id" });

These two fragments are the whole avatar rule. No photo part, or an empty one, or one that is not an image, means avatarUrl stays null; and a null avatarUrl means the upsert row has no avatar_url key at all, so the existing value is left standing. saveBirthProfile always recomputes both charts (computeChart for vedic and western in parallel) and writes them with the row, so any save, including one whose only purpose is a new photo, is a chart computation. An upload that fails returns null from uploadAvatar and the save proceeds without touching the photo; the failure is not reported to the client.

Deleting the account

// app/api/account/route.ts
export async function DELETE(request: Request) {
  const supabase = await createRouteSupabase(request);
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (!user) return new Response("Unauthorized", { status: 401 });

  const admin = createAdminSupabase();
  const { error } = await admin.auth.admin.deleteUser(user.id);
  if (error) {
    console.error("account deletion error", error);
    return Response.json({ error: "Could not delete your account. Please try again." }, { status: 500 });
  }

  return Response.json({ ok: true });
}

Sixteen lines, and the comment above them carries the two rules: the user id comes from the verified session, never from the request body, so a caller can only ever delete themself; and the rows cascade from auth.users. What actually goes, and in what order:

  1. auth.admin.deleteUser removes the row in auth.users. This is the only statement the route issues.
  2. Postgres runs the on delete cascade on every foreign key that points at it. In schema order that is admins, profiles, birth_profiles, conversations, device_tokens, transit_conditions, alerts, daily_readings, life_events, life_event_scans and period_readings.
  3. messages cascades from conversations, one level down, so transcripts go with their conversations.

Two things do not go. The avatar object in the avatars bucket is not deleted; no code removes it, and the public URL that pointed to it was in the row that just cascaded away. And the Apple token is not revoked, the gap described in 4.6. Both are worth knowing before an App Store review that exercises deletion.

What the admin can read

lib/admin/data.ts has three functions, all built on createAdminSupabase, all read-only.

  • adminListUsers calls auth.admin.listUsers for the first page of up to 1000 users, selects six columns from every birth_profiles row and the id, user_id pair of every conversation, joins them in memory into AdminUserRow, and sorts newest first. Three queries regardless of user count.
  • adminGetUser calls auth.admin.getUserById, selects the whole birth_profiles row for that user, and lists their conversations newest first with id, tradition, title and created date. The returned user carries a confirmed boolean derived from email_confirmed_at.
  • adminGetTranscript selects one conversation and its messages in creation order, with role, content and timestamp.

The chart jsonb comes back as part of select("*") in adminGetUser, but the page renders only the six birth fields from it; the chart is never shown. Nothing in the file inserts, updates or deletes.

What the admin can do

app/admin/actions.ts has four server actions. adminSignIn and adminSignOut are covered in 4.8. The other two both begin with await requireAdmin():

  • sendPasswordReset calls resetPasswordForEmail on the cookie client with a redirect to /login. The comment says why this is the shape: the admin never sees or stores the password. The user page repeats this next to the buttons: "Passwords are stored only as secure one-way hashes and cannot be displayed."
  • generateLoginLink calls auth.admin.generateLink with type magiclink for the user's email and a redirect to /app, then redirects back to the user page with the link in the query string, where it is printed with the instruction to open it in a private window. This is the one capability that lets the author act as a user, and it acts through a normal session under RLS, not through the admin client.

11.5The clients

Web

app/(app)/app/profile/page.tsx is a server component. It calls getBirthProfile, redirects to /app/intake if there is no row, and renders a heading, the sentence about recomputing, a "Download kundli (PDF)" link to /api/kundli, and the IntakeForm with action={saveIntake} and every field pre-filled from the row. The form is the same component intake uses (chapter 5); an initial prop switches its button label to "Update my details" and seeds the photo preview from avatarUrl. Choosing a file replaces the preview with an object URL before anything is uploaded.

The sidebar in AppShell has the Profile link, highlighted when the path is /app/profile, and the Sign out form at the bottom. There is no account deletion on the web. The route accepts the cookie client and would work, but no page calls it; deletion was built for the App Store requirement and only the phone offers it today.

iOS: ProfileView

ProfileView is a sheet in a NavigationStack, presented over the chat, and its header comment names it the native counterpart of the web page. It takes a ProfileStore and reads AuthStore from the environment. Top to bottom:

  • The header: a 64-point circular PhotosPicker showing the current photo via AsyncImage, or "Add photo", with a small camera badge and a progress overlay while uploading; then fullName in the light 28-point face and the recompute sentence.
  • The detail card: three rows, Born, Place, Timezone. formattedBirth parses birthDate with a POSIX formatter and renders it long-style with birthTimeShort; the comment gives the target shape, "15 June 1995 at 10:30", the moment as it was where they were born.
  • Buttons: "View your kundli" (disabled and dimmed until profile.chart is loaded), "Edit birth details", and, only when there is no chart yet, "Download kundli (PDF)". The comment explains the ordering: the kundli leads now that there is one to look at in the app, and the PDF is reachable from inside the chart instead of as a second competing button.
  • Suggestions: a status line from SuggestionEngine.onDeviceStatus and a note that follow-up questions are written on the device.
  • Account: Sign out, which calls auth.signOut() and dismisses; and "Delete my account" in the accent colour, which sets confirmDelete and raises an alert with a Cancel and a destructive Delete.

"Edit birth details" presents IntakeView with the current details; on completion the sheet reloads the store and closes. That path is chapter 5's. Changing the photo is deliberately not that path. The header comment says it: routing a photo change through the birth-details form would mean re-submitting a chart to swap a picture.

iOS: the photo

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/PhotoField.swift
func avatarJPEG(maxDimension: CGFloat = 1024, quality: CGFloat = 0.8) -> Data? {
    let longest = max(size.width, size.height)
    guard longest > 0 else { return nil }

    let scale = min(1, maxDimension / longest)
    let target = CGSize(width: (size.width * scale).rounded(), height: (size.height * scale).rounded())
    ...
    return resized.jpegData(compressionQuality: quality)
}

UIImage.avatarJPEG is the one image function in the app. It scales the longest side down to 1024 points, never up (min(1, ...)), renders at scale 1 so the output is not multiplied by the screen density, and encodes JPEG at 0.8. The comment gives the reason: a modern phone photo is several megabytes and is shown at 72 points, and sending the original would make intake feel broken on a slow connection for no visible gain. SancharaPhotoField, the intake form's circular control, wraps PhotosPicker, which runs out of process; the app never asks for photo library permission because only the one chosen image is handed over. ProfileView uses PhotosPicker directly for the same reason.

AvatarBadge is the 28-point control in the chat top bar: the photo when there is one, otherwise the first letters of the first and last name, and when even those are absent a star glyph. The comment's rule is "never an empty grey circle". AsyncImage uses the initials circle as its placeholder, so the badge never flashes empty while the image loads.

iOS: what is cached

ProfileStore.load() begins by reading the chart and the details from ChartCache in the App Group, so the profile sheet and the badge draw at once; the network fetch that follows refreshes both and writes them back. If the fetch fails and a cache exists, the state is still ready; only a device that has never loaded a profile sees the failure screen. On sign-out ChartCache.clear() removes the chart, details, reading and period files, so the next account on the device does not inherit this one's name or photo URL. The avatar image itself is fetched by AsyncImage from the public URL and is not written to the App Group.

The admin pages

Three pages, each opening with await requireAdmin() and wrapping itself in AdminShell, a sidebar with one menu entry, Users, and a Sign out form. app/admin/page.tsx lists users newest first with avatar or initials, name or "(no profile yet)", email, join date and chat count. app/admin/users/[id]/page.tsx shows an Account card (names, email, joined, last sign-in, email confirmed, user id), the two action buttons, a Birth profile card (date, time, place, timezone, latitude, longitude) or "No birth details entered yet", and the list of chats linking to app/admin/conversations/[id]/page.tsx, which renders the transcript as bubbles labelled User and Astrya. Every page is read from lib/admin/data.ts; there is no form on any of them that changes a user's row.

11.6How it works

Change a photo

ProfileView.uploadPickedPhoto runs when photoItem changes. It loads the item as Data, decodes a UIImage, and calls avatarJPEG; if any step fails the message is "That image could not be read. Try another." It then builds a BirthProfileInput from the details already on file, using birthTimeShort for the time, attaches the JPEG, and calls SancharaAPI.saveProfile. The method's comment states the reason for sending the whole profile: the route takes it as a whole and only replaces the avatar when a photo part is present. On success profile.load() refreshes the details, and the new URL, with its fresh ?v= query, makes AsyncImage fetch the replaced image rather than a cached one. A defer block clears the picker item and the uploading flag on every exit.

Delete the account

  1. The alert's destructive button calls deleteAccount(), which sets isDeleting so the button cannot be tapped twice.
  2. SancharaAPI.deleteAccount() sends DELETE /api/account with the bearer token and checks the status; a non-2xx becomes an error shown under the buttons.
  3. The route deletes the auth user and the cascades run (11.4).
  4. On success the view calls auth.signOut(). Its first step, PushStore.shared.unregister(), has nothing left to act on: the user is gone and the device_tokens row has already cascaded away. The call is wrapped in try?, so whatever the server answers, nothing is lost and nothing is shown. Then the Supabase session is signed out and ChartCache is cleared, which removes the deleted person's chart from the widget and Siri.
  5. The sheet dismisses and RootView shows AuthView.

An admin session

  1. The author opens /admin. The middleware finds no session and redirects to /admin/login.
  2. adminSignIn signs in with a password, checks admins, and redirects to /admin. A non-admin account is signed out again and told "Not authorized".
  3. The users page calls requireAdmin then adminListUsers; each user links to their page, each chat to its transcript. Every read goes through the service-role client because the rows belong to other people.
  4. To see the app as a user, "Generate login link" mints a magic link and prints it. Opened in a private window, it goes through /auth/callback like any other link and produces an ordinary session for that user. From then on the private window is that user, under RLS, with no admin powers.

11.7Performance and cost

  • A photo change costs a chart. saveBirthProfile always runs computeChart twice, so swapping a picture invokes the ephemeris. It runs in process inside the route and completes in the same request, so the cost is server time rather than a slow round trip, but it is the reason the profile route declares runtime = "nodejs". Splitting a photo-only endpoint off would remove it.
  • Uploads are bounded on the phone. 1024 points on the longest side at 0.8 quality; testDownscalesLargePhotos asserts a 3000 by 2000 source comes out under half a megabyte. The web form sends whatever file was chosen, with no client-side resize, and the route accepts any image/* of any size.
  • Reads are cached on the phone. The profile sheet draws from the App Group cache before the network answers, and the details query selects nine columns without the chart, which is fetched separately and only when needed.
  • The public URL carries a cache-buster. The ?v= timestamp is stored in the row, not computed on read, so every client sees the same URL until the next upload and the image is cached by the browser and by URLSession until then.
  • Admin reads are three queries for the list, regardless of user count, joined in memory; and listUsers asks for one page of 1000, so past that the list is silently truncated. The user page is three more, and the chart jsonb rides along in select("*") unused.
  • Deletion is one statement. The cascades run inside Postgres; the route does not enumerate tables and does not need to change when a table is added, provided the new table references auth.users with on delete cascade as all eleven do today.

11.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
Profile page opened with no birth rowWeb redirects to /app/intake; iOS ProfileStore goes to needsIntake and SignedInView shows intake instead
Save with no photo partavatarUrl is null, the upsert row omits avatar_url, the existing photo stays
Save with an empty file input on the webphoto.size > 0 fails; treated as no photo
A non-image file named phototype.startsWith("image/") fails; treated as no photo
Storage upload failsuploadAvatar returns null; the birth details still save; the failure is logged nowhere and not reported
Picked image cannot be decoded on iOS"That image could not be read. Try another." and no request is sent
Same photo path uploaded twiceupsert: true on the storage call replaces the object; the new ?v= query defeats caches
Photo changed from JPEG to PNG on the webA second object at avatar.png is created; the old avatar.jpg is left in the bucket
Missing required field on POST /api/profile400 with the field name; saveBirthProfile not called
lat not numeric400; saveBirthProfile not called
Chart computation throws500 with "Could not compute your chart. Please try again."; the avatar, if any, was already uploaded
Delete without a session401; deleteUser never called
deleteUser returns an error500 with a generic message; the error is logged; nothing was deleted because the one statement failed
Delete succeeds, then push unregister failsExpected: the user is gone and the token row cascaded; try? swallows it
Avatar object after deletionRemains in the bucket under the old user id; nothing references it
Non-admin account signs in at /admin/loginSigned out immediately, "Not authorized"
Admin row removed mid-sessionThe next page load's requireAdmin redirects to login
User with no birth profile in the admin list"(no profile yet)" with initials from the email
More than 1000 usersThe list shows the first page only

Tests

  • app/api/account/route.test.ts mocks createRouteSupabase and createAdminSupabase and pins three things: an unauthenticated request returns 401 and deleteUser is not called; an authenticated request returns 200 with { ok: true } and deleteUser is called with exactly the session's user id ("deletes only the calling user"); and an error from deleteUser becomes a 500.
  • app/api/profile/route.test.ts pins the 401 with no user, a 200 for a valid payload with saveBirthProfile receiving the parsed lat as a number and the bearer client as its second argument, a 400 when timezone is missing, and a 400 when lat is not a number.
  • mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ProfilePhotoTests.swift, whose header says what it guards: the contract with /api/profile, which only overwrites the avatar when a photo part is present. testDownscalesLargePhotos: a 3000 by 2000 image comes out with its longest side at 1024, its aspect ratio at 1.5, and under 500,000 bytes. testLeavesSmallPhotosAlone: a 400 by 400 image is not upscaled. testMultipartCarriesThePhotoWhenOneIsPicked: the body contains a part named photo with filename avatar.jpg, the image/jpeg content type, the image bytes, the text fields, and a terminating boundary. testMultipartOmitsThePhotoPartWhenNoneIsPicked: with no JPEG there is no photo part at all, with the assertion message "sending an empty photo part would clear a profile photo the user meant to keep".

Not covered by a test: uploadAvatar itself, the admin data functions, and the admin pages. The first would need a storage stub; the second and third depend on the Auth admin API.

11.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/data/birthProfile.ts, for saveBirthProfile and the one line that decides whether the avatar changes.
  2. app/api/profile/route.ts, then app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts, to see the same work done twice for two callers.
  3. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift, the BirthProfileInput struct and multipartBody at the bottom.
  4. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/PhotoField.swift, for avatarJPEG.
  5. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Profile/ProfileView.swift, uploadPickedPhoto and deleteAccount in particular.
  6. app/api/account/route.ts and supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql side by side, following every on delete cascade.
  7. lib/admin/data.ts and app/admin/actions.ts, to confirm for yourself what the admin can and cannot do.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ProfilePhotoTests.swift, which reads as the upload contract in plain English.

Check yourself

How does the server know whether to keep or replace the avatar?

By the presence of a photo part that is a non-empty image. If there is none, avatarUrl is null and saveBirthProfile omits avatar_url from the upsert, so the column keeps its value.

Why does the iOS photo change send all eight birth fields?

Because the route takes the profile as a whole and there is no photo-only endpoint. The view builds the input from the details already on file so nothing changes but the picture.

What does DELETE /api/account delete, and what does it leave?

It issues one auth.admin.deleteUser; every table referencing auth.users cascades, and messages cascades from conversations. It leaves the avatar object in storage and does not revoke the Apple token.

Why does the profile save recompute the chart when only the photo changed?

Because saveBirthProfile has one path and it always computes both charts before the upsert. The cost is accepted for simplicity; a separate avatar endpoint would avoid it.

Which admin action changes a user's row?

None. The two actions send a password reset email and mint a magic link; both go through Auth, and neither touches a table.

Why is the upload done with the admin client while the row write is done as the user?

There is no storage policy for users, so the bucket write needs the service role; the path is built from the verified user id. The row is under RLS, so the user's own client is enough and keeps the rule that a user writes only their own row.

Chapter twelve

12The landing page and the web shell

Four colours on :root, one photograph of a planet, and a sidebar that lists past readings. Everything a person sees before and around a reading is in this chapter, and the native app copies its tokens from the same stylesheet so that a screen on the phone and a page in the browser read as one product.

This chapter describes the web surface of Astrya as it is on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It covers the root layout, the marketing page at /, the signed-in shell under /app, the auth form, the two build-time configuration files that shape the whole site, and, for comparison, the iOS design system that translates the site's tokens into SwiftUI. The chapters before this one each own a feature; this one owns the frame the features sit in.

12.1Intent

The web app has two audiences and one theme. A stranger arriving at / should understand in one screen that the astrology is computed, not guessed, and be one tap from an account. A signed-in person should never see the marketing again: / sends them straight to /app, where the shell shows their readings, a button to start another, and a link to their profile. Both audiences see the same dark cosmic theme, because there is only one. The site has no light mode, and the iOS app is pinned dark in its Info.plist for the same reason: a reading card that turned white inside Siri while the app stayed black would look like someone else's product.

Three decisions shaped the code. The theme is four CSS custom properties and nothing else, so it can be copied by hand into Swift and checked by a test. The landing page's animation is loaded on the client only, after the HTML has arrived, so the first paint is a photograph and never a blank canvas. And the shell is the only client component in the signed-in frame; every page inside it is a server component that reads Supabase directly and hands props down.

12.2Files and folders

app/
├── layout.tsx                   html, body, metadata, viewport; imports globals.css

Three files in components/marketing/ are dead. Hero.tsx, HowItWorks.tsx and CTA.tsx are the first landing page, built with next/image; nothing imports them today. The history of the folder tells the story: a WebGL scene built with three.js and GSAP replaced them, then commit c0a209d ("Hero: remove 3D WebGL, keep planet image with animated text") took the scene out again and kept the photograph and the animated headline. three and @types/three are still in package.json, and the comment at the top of CosmicLanding.tsx still says WebGL, but no file imports three.js, so nothing of it reaches a bundle.

12.3Model

The theme is four numbers

/* app/globals.css */
@import "tailwindcss";

:root {
  --bg: #0a0a0b;
  --fg: #f4f1ea;
  --muted: #9a978f;
  --accent: #e8663d;
}

@theme inline {
  --color-bg: var(--bg);
  --color-fg: var(--fg);
  --color-muted: var(--muted);
  --color-accent: var(--accent);
  --font-display: ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;
}

Near-black, warm off-white, a grey with the same warmth, and one orange. The @theme inline block is how Tailwind 4 learns about them: it turns each --color-* variable into a utility family, so bg-bg, text-fg, text-muted and bg-accent exist without a configuration file. There is no tailwind.config.* in the repo. postcss.config.mjs is one line, registering @tailwindcss/postcss, and everything else about the theme is CSS.

The rest of globals.css is small and each rule carries its reason. html, body set the background and foreground from the tokens and overflow-x: hidden with max-width: 100%, because a landing page with a headline wider than a phone otherwise scrolls sideways. Form controls get max-width: 100%; min-width: 0, with the comment that iOS date and time inputs have a large intrinsic width that otherwise overflows the screen. Below 768 pixels every input is forced to 16px, because iOS Safari zooms the page when a smaller control takes focus. Then the animations: fade-up, fade-in, glow-pulse, heartbeat, blink and shimmer, exposed as .animate-* classes with three delay steps of 0.08, 0.16 and 0.24 seconds. .prose-reading styles the Markdown that the chat renders (chapter 6). A prefers-reduced-motion block turns off fade-up, fade-in and glow; the heartbeat, shimmer and caret animations are not in that block and keep running.

The Swift copy

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift
enum Theme {
    /// `--bg: #0a0a0b`
    static let bg = Color(hex: 0x0A0A0B)
    /// `--fg: #f4f1ea`
    static let fg = Color(hex: 0xF4F1EA)
    /// `--muted: #9a978f`
    static let muted = Color(hex: 0x9A978F)
    /// `--accent: #e8663d`
    static let accent = Color(hex: 0xE8663D)

    /// Tailwind `border-white/10`, used for every hairline on the site.
    static let hairline = Color.white.opacity(0.10)
    /// Tailwind `bg-white/[0.04]`, the fill behind form controls.
    static let fieldFill = Color.white.opacity(0.04)
    /// Tailwind `rounded-lg` is 0.5rem.
    static let cornerRadius: CGFloat = 8
    static let chartLine = Color.white.opacity(0.22)
    /// The site's easing: `cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1)`.
    static let ease = Animation.timingCurve(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1, duration: 0.6)
}

The file's own header says it is "translated verbatim from app/globals.css", and the doc comment above each token quotes the CSS line it copies. The Color(hex:) initialiser exists so a token can be written the way it appears in CSS, as a 24-bit literal in sRGB. Two things are added that the CSS never names as tokens: the hairline and the field fill are Tailwind opacity utilities on the site (border-white/10, bg-white/[0.04]), and Swift has to give them a name. chartLine is heavier than the hairline, at 0.22 white, and the comment explains why: the kundli square "all but vanished on an OLED screen in daylight" at 0.10. Text.eyebrow() reproduces the site's eyebrow style (text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.3em] text-muted) as 12 points with 3.6 points of tracking, which is 0.3em at that size.

The route tree

12.4The server side

The root layout

// app/layout.tsx
export const metadata: Metadata = {
  title: "Astrya: Your chart, read by the stars",
  description: "Sign up, share your birth details, and chat with an astrologer powered by your real birth chart.",
};

export const viewport = {
  width: "device-width",
  initialScale: 1,
};

export default function RootLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body className="min-h-screen antialiased">{children}</body>
    </html>
  );
}

That is the whole file, plus the import "./globals.css" above it. It is a server component with no data access; the title and description are the only metadata the site declares, and no page overrides them. The viewport export is what keeps iOS from rendering the site at a desktop width and shrinking it.

The landing route

// app/page.tsx
export default async function Landing() {
  const supabase = await createServerSupabase();
  const { data: { user } } = await supabase.auth.getUser();
  if (user) redirect("/app");
  return <CosmicLanding />;
}

/ is a server component that does one thing before rendering: it asks Supabase who the visitor is, using the cookie client from lib/supabase/server.ts. A signed-in visitor is redirected on the server and never receives the landing HTML. Everyone else receives <CosmicLanding />, which is a client component, so the server's contribution to the page is the root layout and whatever the client component's fallback renders on the server (12.5).

The middleware

middleware.ts runs updateSession from lib/supabase/middleware.ts on every request except static assets, images, the favicon and .well-known/. It creates a server client over the request cookies, calls getUser() so the session cookie is refreshed, and then applies one rule: a request to a path starting with /app, or with /admin other than /admin/login, with no user is redirected to /login or /admin/login. This is why none of the pages under (app) check for a session themselves. They check for a birth profile, which is a different question, and one the middleware cannot answer.

The signed-in layout and home

// app/(app)/layout.tsx
export const dynamic = "force-dynamic";

export default async function AppLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const conversations = await listConversations();
  return <AppShell conversations={conversations as never}>{children}</AppShell>;
}

force-dynamic tells Next never to cache any page under this layout; every request renders fresh, which is right for a frame whose sidebar lists the person's own conversations. listConversations (in lib/data/chat.ts) selects id, tradition, title, created_at ordered newest first, with no user filter in the query: Row Level Security supplies it (chapter 13). The as never cast is there because the query returns loosely typed rows and the shell declares a stricter Conv type; it is a cast, not a validation, and if a column were renamed the shell would receive undefined rather than an error.

The home at app/(app)/app/page.tsx calls getBirthProfile() and branches on the result. With no profile it shows "Let's build your chart" and a button to /app/intake in the foreground colour. With a profile it shows the avatar if there is one, "Hello," followed by the first name, an accent-coloured "Start a reading" button to /app/chat, and a plain link to /api/kundli that downloads the PDF (chapter 7). The avatar is a plain <img>, with an ESLint disable comment above it, because it is a public Supabase Storage URL and next/image would need the host allow-listed. Behind both states sits Glow, a blurred radial gradient from a violet at 22% opacity through the accent at 6% to transparent. The same gradient string appears in app/(auth)/layout.tsx and app/(app)/app/intake/page.tsx; it is copied, not shared.

The two configuration files

// next.config.ts
const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  // swisseph-wasm ships a .wasm binary; keep it external so Next bundles it for the server runtime.
  serverExternalPackages: ["swisseph-wasm"],

  async headers() {
    return [
      {
        source: "/.well-known/apple-app-site-association",
        headers: [{ key: "content-type", value: "application/json" }],
      },
    ];
  },
};

Two settings, each for a different chapter. serverExternalPackages keeps the Swiss Ephemeris package out of the bundler so its .wasm file is loaded from node_modules at run time (chapter 2). The header fixes a platform quirk: Apple requires the app-site-association file to be served as JSON, the file has no extension, and Next would otherwise serve it as application/octet-stream. That file is what lets iOS open universal links in the app (chapter 14). The middleware matcher excludes .well-known/ so the session code never runs for it.

12.5The clients

The landing, in two client components

// components/marketing/CosmicLanding.tsx
"use client";
import dynamic from "next/dynamic";

const CosmicHero = dynamic(() => import("./CosmicHero"), {
  ssr: false,
  loading: () => (
    <div className="min-h-[100svh] bg-cover bg-center" style={{ backgroundImage: "url(/images/planet-sun.jpg)" }} />
  ),
});

export function CosmicLanding() {
  return <CosmicHero />;
}

CosmicLanding exists only to do this import. ssr: false means the server never renders CosmicHero; the HTML for / contains the loading element, a full-height div with the planet photograph as its background, and nothing else. The browser downloads the hero's chunk, which carries GSAP and its ScrollTrigger plugin, and swaps it in. The design intent in the file's comment is that "nothing looks blank" while that happens, and the photograph does that job.

CosmicHero is data plus one effect. The data is a HERO constant (eyebrow "Computed astrology, never guessed", title "ASTRYA", one line of copy, two links: "Get your reading" to /signup and "Log in" to /login) and three SECTIONS ("THE SCIENCE", "YOUR CHART", "YOUR FUTURE"), the last with a "Create your free account" button. The hero section is min-h-[100svh] with the planet photograph as a CSS background; each dark section is another full viewport in bg-bg. The title is split into one <span class="ch"> per character and starts with visibility: hidden as an inline style, so the letters are not seen before they animate. 

// components/marketing/CosmicHero.tsx
useEffect(() => {
  const reduce = window.matchMedia?.("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches;
  if (reduce) {
    if (titleRef.current) titleRef.current.style.visibility = "visible";
    return;
  }
  gsap.registerPlugin(ScrollTrigger);
  const ctx = gsap.context(() => {
    if (titleRef.current) {
      gsap.set(titleRef.current, { visibility: "visible" });
      gsap.from(titleRef.current.querySelectorAll(".ch"), {
        y: 120, opacity: 0, duration: 1.1, stagger: 0.05, ease: "power4.out",
      });
    }
    gsap.utils.toArray<HTMLElement>(".reveal").forEach((el) => {
      gsap.from(el.children, {
        y: 60, opacity: 0, duration: 1, stagger: 0.12, ease: "power3.out",
        scrollTrigger: { trigger: el, start: "top 78%" },
      });
    });
  }, root);
  return () => ctx.revert();
}, []);

The effect reads the reduced-motion preference first and, if set, makes the title visible and does nothing else: no plugin registration, no tweens, no scroll listeners. Otherwise the eight letters rise 120 pixels over 1.1 seconds with a 50 millisecond stagger, and each .reveal block's children rise 60 pixels when the block's top crosses 78% of the viewport height. Everything is created inside gsap.context scoped to the root element, so the cleanup is one call to ctx.revert() when the component unmounts. No three.js scene is created anywhere; the "cosmic" in the name is the photograph and the black sections.

The shell

AppShell is the one client component in the signed-in frame, and it is a client component for three reasons only: it holds the open state of the mobile drawer, it reads the ?c= search parameter to highlight the active conversation, and it reads the pathname to highlight Profile. It receives the conversation list as a prop and fetches nothing.

The navigation column is built once as nav and placed twice. On a medium screen and up it is a 256 pixel aside with a hairline right border. Below that it is hidden, a header with a hamburger button (labelled "Open menu" for assistive technology) appears, and tapping it renders a fixed overlay: a 60% black backdrop that closes on click, and a drawer 288 pixels wide, capped at 82% of the screen. Every link in the column closes the drawer on click, so navigating on a phone never leaves it open over the new page. The column holds the brand link to /app, a "New reading" link to /app/chat, a Profile link, the "Past readings" list, and a sign-out form whose action is the signOut server action imported from app/(auth)/actions.ts. A conversation is highlighted when its id matches ?c= and the pathname is exactly /app/chat; a conversation with no title is shown as "Reading". The outer frame is h-dvh with overflow-hidden, and <main> is min-h-0 flex-1, which is what lets the chat transcript scroll inside the frame instead of the page scrolling under the sidebar.

The auth form

// components/AuthForm.tsx (props)
{
  title: string;
  subtitle?: string;
  primaryLabel: string;
  primaryAction: (fd: FormData) => void;
  magicAction?: (fd: FormData) => void;
  footer: React.ReactNode;
  error?: string;
  notice?: string;
}

AuthForm has no "use client" directive and no state. It is a server component rendering one form with an email field, a password field, a primary button with formAction={primaryAction} and, when magicAction is given, a second button "Email me a magic link instead" with its own formAction. Two buttons in one form, each bound to a different server action, is how a single email field serves both a password sign-in and a magic link without any client JavaScript. /login and /signup both render it with the same authenticate action; only the title and subtitle differ, because the action itself decides whether to sign in or sign up (chapter 4). Errors arrive as ?error= in the URL and are printed in the accent colour above the form; ?sent=1 prints the magic-link notice in muted text. The inputs use the same recipe as every field on the site: bg-white/[0.04], border-white/10, rounded-lg, and the border turns accent on focus.

The iOS design system

The Swift files under mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Design/ are the same recipe written for SwiftUI. SancharaField is the input: 16 point text, 12 by 14 points of padding, Theme.fieldFill behind it, a Theme.hairline stroke, and an 8 point radius. Its doc comment notes that the web forces 16 pixels on phones so Safari does not zoom, that native has no such bug, and that "the size is the look". SancharaPrimaryButton is bg-fg text-bg rounded-lg: foreground fill, background-coloured text, full width, a spinner in place of the label while loading and disabled while it shows. SancharaSecondaryButton is the outlined button with white at 0.15 for its stroke and muted 14 point text. SancharaSecureField adds an eye toggle the web form does not have, with the comment that choosing a password you cannot see "is the most common reason a sign-up attempt fails twice".

ReadingCard is the one component shared by the Siri snippet and the Home Screen widget (chapter 10): an eyebrow at 10 points with 2 points of tracking, a serif headline in Theme.fg, and body text at 13 points in Theme.muted with a line limit that defaults to 6 because "widgets get less room than a Siri snippet does". It sets its own colours rather than reading the environment, and its header comment gives the reason this chapter opened with: the app is dark-only by design, UIUserInterfaceStyle: Dark in Info.plist and in mobile/project.yml, and a card that went light inside Siri would read as a different product. Its accessibility label concatenates the three strings so VoiceOver reads the card as one element.

12.6How it works

An anonymous visit to /

  1. The middleware runs. There is no session cookie, the path is not under /app or /admin, so the request passes through.
  2. app/page.tsx renders on the server, calls getUser(), finds nobody, and returns <CosmicLanding />.
  3. The HTML that leaves the server is the root layout around the loading fallback: a full-viewport div showing planet-sun.jpg. No headline, no links.
  4. The browser hydrates, requests the CosmicHero chunk, and mounts it. The title's spans exist but are hidden.
  5. The effect runs. Without a reduced-motion preference GSAP makes the title visible and the letters rise; three ScrollTriggers wait for the dark sections. With the preference set, the title is simply shown.
  6. "Get your reading" and "Log in" are ordinary next/link anchors to /signup and /login. Both routes render AuthForm inside the (auth) layout's centred grid with the glow behind it.

A signed-in visit to /

  1. The middleware refreshes the session cookie by calling getUser().
  2. app/page.tsx calls getUser() again, finds a user, and redirect("/app") throws before any component renders. The landing chunk is never requested.
  3. app/(app)/layout.tsx renders: listConversations() runs under RLS for that user and the result is handed to AppShell.
