  4. app/(app)/app/page.tsx renders inside it: getBirthProfile() runs, and if the stored Vedic chart predates the whole-sign change, ensureCurrentChart recomputes both traditions and rewrites the row before the page is returned (chapter 2).
  5. The home shows either the intake prompt or the greeting with "Start a reading".

Signing out from the shell

The "Sign out" button is inside a <form action={signOut}>. Submitting it calls the server action, which creates the cookie client, calls supabase.auth.signOut(), and redirect("/login"). The next request to anything under /app has no session and the middleware sends it to /login as well. No client code is involved beyond the form submission.

12.7Performance and cost

  • The landing ships one animation library. The client chunk for the hero carries GSAP 3.15 and ScrollTrigger and nothing else of note. three.js 0.169 is installed and unused, and Next bundles only what is imported, so it costs install time, not bytes on the wire. Removing it from package.json would be a one-line cleanup with no behaviour change.
  • The first paint is a 141,888 byte JPEG. CosmicHero and its fallback both load planet-sun.jpg as a CSS background, so next/image does no resizing or format conversion for it. The unused Hero.tsx did use next/image with priority; that optimisation went away with the component. starfield.jpg is referenced only by the unused components and is never requested.
  • Nothing above the fold is server-rendered on /. With ssr: false the headline and the two calls to action exist only after hydration. A crawler that does not run JavaScript sees a photograph and the metadata title. That is a trade the code makes for a paint that is never blank; it is worth knowing when reading a search report.
  • Two session checks per request. The middleware calls getUser() on every matched request and app/page.tsx calls it again. With a session cookie present, each is a verification round trip to Supabase Auth.
  • Every /app page is dynamic. force-dynamic on the layout means each render runs listConversations(); the home, chat and profile pages each add getBirthProfile(). Two queries per navigation, both indexed (conversations (user_id, created_at desc), birth_profiles unique on user_id), and the shell itself makes no client fetch.
  • The chart upgrade is on the read path. A stale stored chart costs two Swiss Ephemeris computations and one update the first time its owner loads any page that reads the profile. It happens once per profile.
  • Reduced motion is free. The preference short-circuits the effect before GSAP registers its plugin, so nothing is created that then has to be reverted.
  • The theme costs nothing to keep in sync. Four hex values copied by hand and pinned by four unit tests. There is no build step between the CSS and the Swift.

12.8Edge cases and tests

ConditionBehaviour
Signed-in person opens /Server redirect to /app; the landing chunk is never sent
Signed-out person opens /app/anythingMiddleware redirects to /login before the page renders
Signed-out person opens /admin/loginAllowed; every other /admin path redirects there
Signed in, no birth profileHome shows the intake prompt; /app/chat redirects to /app/intake when profile.chart is missing
Signed in, profile without an avatarThe <img> is not rendered at all, so there is no broken image
Conversation with a null titleListed as "Reading" in the sidebar and in the link's title attribute
prefers-reduced-motionThe hero shows the title without GSAP; CSS disables fade-up, fade-in and glow. heartbeat, shimmer and the caret blink are not disabled
JavaScript disabled, or before hydration/ shows the planet photograph only; no headline or links are in the HTML
Phone narrower than 768pxSidebar hidden; hamburger opens a drawer capped at 82% width; any link closes it
iOS date or time inputmax-width: 100% and appearance: none stop the intrinsic width overflowing; 16px text stops Safari zooming on focus
Headline wider than the viewportbreak-words on the title plus overflow-x: hidden on body; no sideways scroll
Request for /.well-known/apple-app-site-associationServed as application/json; the middleware does not run for it
The Next data cacheNever used under /app: force-dynamic on the layout

Tests

  • app/smoke.test.ts: one test, "layout module loads", which imports the default export of app/layout.tsx and asserts it is a function. The import is named metadata in the test, but it is the default export, which is RootLayout; the metadata object itself is not asserted on. This is the only web test that touches anything in this chapter.
  • mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ThemeTests.swift: testBackgroundMatchesCSSToken, testForegroundMatchesCSSToken, testMutedMatchesCSSToken and testAccentMatchesCSSToken. Each converts the SwiftUI Color to a UIColor, reads its components, rounds them to 0 to 255, and compares with the hex bytes from globals.css. The class comment states the premise: a careless edit to Theme.swift "fails the build rather than quietly drifting". The tests pin the Swift side only; an edit to the CSS would not fail them.
  • No test renders CosmicHero, AppShell or AuthForm. The behaviour in the table above is read from the code, not proven by a suite.

12.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. app/globals.css, for the four tokens and every rule that exists because of a phone.
  2. app/layout.tsx and app/page.tsx, the whole server side of the public site in thirty lines.
  3. components/marketing/CosmicLanding.tsx, then CosmicHero.tsx, for the dynamic import and the one effect.
  4. middleware.ts and lib/supabase/middleware.ts, the guard that lets the pages skip their own session checks.
  5. app/(app)/layout.tsx and components/AppShell.tsx, the frame and why it is the only client component in it.
  6. app/(app)/app/page.tsx, the two states of the home.
  7. components/AuthForm.tsx with app/(auth)/login/page.tsx, two buttons and two server actions in one form.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift and ThemeTests.swift, the copy and the test that pins it.
  9. next.config.ts, last, because both of its settings belong to other chapters.

Check yourself

Why is the hero loaded with ssr: false, and what does the visitor lose?

GSAP needs the DOM and the code was written for a browser-only scene, so the component is rendered only on the client. The visitor loses server-rendered headline text: before hydration the page is the photograph alone.

Where does the sidebar's list of readings come from, and why does the query have no user_id filter?

From listConversations() in the (app) layout, run on the server with the cookie client. Row Level Security restricts the rows to the signed-in user, so the query does not need to.

Why do the (app) pages not check for a session?

The middleware redirects any request under /app without a user to /login before a page renders. The pages check for a birth profile instead, which is the question the middleware cannot answer.

What would happen if someone changed --accent in globals.css?

The site would change and the iOS app would not. ThemeTests compare Swift against constants copied from the CSS, not against the CSS itself, so the test would still pass until Theme.swift was edited to match.

Why are there two getUser() calls on a signed-in visit to /?

The middleware calls one to refresh the cookie and decide whether to redirect protected paths; app/page.tsx calls another to decide whether to redirect the landing itself. The landing is not a protected path, so the middleware cannot make that second decision.

Why is the reading card in iOS given explicit colours instead of the environment's?

It renders inside Siri and inside a widget, where the environment's colour scheme may be light. The app is dark-only, and a card that changed with its host would look like another product.

Part three

Under the hood

Every table and policy, the iOS project and how it is built, how the system is set up, deployed and released, and a catalogue of what breaks and what catches it.

  • 13. The data model
  • 14. The iOS app
  • 15. Setup, deployment, and release
  • 16. What breaks, and how it is handled

Chapter thirteen

13The data model

Twelve tables in seven numbered SQL files, one storage bucket, and two keys. Every table hangs off auth.users and cascades when the user goes. Every table has Row Level Security on, and the shape of its policies says who may write it: the person, or only the server.

This chapter reads supabase/migrations/ in order, as it stands on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. Migrations are the schema's history, and reading them in sequence is reading the product's: a chat with a chart, then a photo, then numerology, then push alerts, then twice-daily readings, then the life timeline and its explanations. After the seven files there is a summary of every table with its RLS shape and every piece of code that reads or writes it, a section on the avatars bucket that no migration creates, and an explanation of the two Supabase keys in terms of the policies. Where a policy and a route handler disagree about who should write a table, the route handler's comment usually says why, and the comment is quoted.

13.1Where the schema lives and how it is applied

supabase/migrations/
├── 0001_init.sql               admins, profiles, birth_profiles, conversations, messages
├── 0002_avatar.sql             birth_profiles.avatar_url
├── 0003_numerology_mode.sql    conversations.tradition may be 'numerology'
├── 0004_alerts_push.sql        device_tokens, transit_conditions, alerts
├── 0005_daily_readings.sql     daily_readings
├── 0006_life_events.sql        life_events, life_event_scans
└── 0007_period_readings.sql    period_readings

lib/supabase/
├── client.ts                   browser client, anon key, "use client"
├── server.ts                   cookie client for server components and actions, anon key
├── middleware.ts               cookie refresh and the /app and /admin guard
├── route.ts                    bearer-or-cookie client for route handlers, anon key
└── admin.ts                    service-role client, "server-only"

docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md       the production facts this chapter quotes
docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md          project creation and the first admin row

The files are plain SQL with a four-digit prefix. There is no migration runner in the codebase and no CI step that applies them. The README gives two ways to apply them: supabase link followed by supabase db push, or pasting each file into the dashboard's SQL editor, "lowest number first". docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md still describes running only 0001_init.sql; the README's instruction is the current one and it means all seven.

Migrations are applied by hand, before the code that needs them The README states the rule: "After deploying a version that adds a migration, apply it to the production Supabase project before traffic reaches the new code." docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md records what happens when that is missed. As of 16 September 2026 the production database did not have the tables from 0006_life_events.sql and 0007_period_readings.sql, although the timeline code that reads them had shipped. The checklist's fix is to run the two files in the SQL editor, in order, exactly as they are in the repo, and confirm with a query against information_schema.tables for life_events, life_event_scans and period_readings. Since main deploys to production on every push (chapter 15), a commit that adds 0008 is live before anyone has had a chance to apply it, and every route that touches the new table returns an error until someone does.

13.2The migrations, in order

0001 · init

The first file enables pgcrypto (for gen_random_uuid()) and creates the five tables the original product needed: who is an admin, a profile row per auth user, the birth details with the computed chart beside them, and conversations with their messages.

TableColumnsKeys and indexes
adminsuser_id uuid, created_at timestamptzPK user_id, references auth.users(id) on delete cascade
profilesid uuid, display_name text, created_atPK id, references auth.users(id) on delete cascade
birth_profilesid uuid, user_id uuid not null, first_name, last_name, birth_date date, birth_time time, place_name, lat, lng double precision, timezone text, chart jsonb, updated_atPK id default gen_random_uuid(); unique (user_id); FK to auth.users cascade
conversationsid uuid, user_id uuid not null, tradition text check in ('vedic','western'), title text, created_atPK id; index (user_id, created_at desc); FK cascade
messagesid uuid, conversation_id uuid not null, role text check in ('user','assistant'), content text not null, created_atPK id; index (conversation_id, created_at); FK to conversations(id) on delete cascade

Two functions and one trigger. is_admin() is language sql stable security definer with set search_path = public, and returns whether a row in admins matches auth.uid(). handle_new_user() is a plpgsql security definer trigger function that inserts a profiles row for a new auth user, taking display_name from raw_user_meta_data->>'display_name' or, failing that, the part of the email before the @. The trigger on_auth_user_created fires after insert on auth.users for each row. This is the only code anywhere that writes profiles, and, as the summary in 13.4 shows, nothing reads it either. 

-- supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql
-- RLS: owner-only. Admin reads happen via the service-role client, which bypasses RLS.
create policy "profiles self" on public.profiles
  for all using (id = auth.uid()) with check (id = auth.uid());

create policy "birth self" on public.birth_profiles
  for all using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

create policy "conv self" on public.conversations
  for all using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

create policy "msg self" on public.messages
  for all using (
    exists (select 1 from public.conversations c
            where c.id = messages.conversation_id and c.user_id = auth.uid())
  ) with check (
    exists (select 1 from public.conversations c
            where c.id = messages.conversation_id and c.user_id = auth.uid())
  );

-- admins table: readable only by admins; never writable via API (no insert/update/delete policy).
create policy "admins read self-or-admin" on public.admins
  for select using (user_id = auth.uid() or public.is_admin());

Four tables get one for all policy each, which covers select, insert, update and delete with the same predicate. messages has no user_id of its own; its policy reaches through conversations, so a message is yours if its conversation is. admins gets a select policy only. There is no insert policy and none is meant to exist: the file's first comment says "presence in this table = admin. No self-service path", and docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md has the first admin row inserted by hand in the SQL editor. The is_admin() function is used nowhere in TypeScript; it exists for that one policy, and the application checks adminship with the service-role client instead (13.6).

0002 · avatar

One statement: alter table public.birth_profiles add column if not exists avatar_url text. The column holds a public URL into the avatars bucket (13.5), not the object path, and it is nullable because a photo is optional. The if not exists makes the file safe to run twice.

0003 · numerology_mode

-- supabase/migrations/0003_numerology_mode.sql
alter table public.conversations drop constraint if exists conversations_tradition_check;
alter table public.conversations
  add constraint conversations_tradition_check check (tradition in ('vedic','western','numerology'));

The check constraint from 0001 is dropped by its generated name and recreated with a third value. This is the whole database side of the numerology mode (chapter 6): a conversation records which of the three lenses it was read through, and the constraint is what stops a typo in a client from creating a fourth.

0004 · alerts_push

The first migration written for the iOS app and the scheduled job. Its header comment sets the pattern every later table follows: "The daily job (/api/cron/alerts) writes both tables with the service-role client, which bypasses RLS. The policies below exist so the app can read its own alerts and manage its own device tokens directly."

TableColumnsKeys and indexes
device_tokenstoken text, user_id uuid not null, platform text default 'ios' check in ('ios'), environment text default 'production' check in ('sandbox','production'), updated_atPK token; index (user_id); FK cascade
transit_conditionsid uuid, user_id, kind text, signature text, label text, severity check in ('info','caution','warning'), scope check in ('natal','transit'), detail text, started_on date default today in UTC, ended_on datePK id; unique partial index transit_conditions_open_signature (user_id, signature) where ended_on is null; index (user_id, ended_on); FK cascade
alertsid uuid, user_id, severity check as above, kinds text[] default '{}', title, body, detail, created_at, read_at timestamptzPK id; index (user_id, created_at desc); FK cascade

The token is the primary key of device_tokens, not the user, because one person can have several phones and one phone can change hands. environment exists because a token minted by a development build is only valid against the APNs sandbox host and vice versa; the comment on the column says so, and the go-live checklist notes that rows registered before the app reported its environment correctly may carry the wrong value.

transit_conditions is the state table behind "this changed today". A row is open while ended_on is null, and the partial unique index guarantees at most one open row per (user_id, signature). The daily diff in lib/alerts/run.ts compares the engine's current conditions against the open rows, inserts the new ones, and sets ended_on on the ones that stopped (chapter 9). The default for started_on is (now() at time zone 'utc')::date, so the date is the server's, not the user's; daily_readings makes the opposite choice on purpose. 

-- supabase/migrations/0004_alerts_push.sql
create policy "device tokens self" on public.device_tokens
  for all using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

create policy "conditions self read" on public.transit_conditions
  for select using (user_id = auth.uid());

create policy "alerts self read" on public.alerts
  for select using (user_id = auth.uid());

-- Marking an alert read is the only write the app makes.
create policy "alerts self update" on public.alerts
  for update using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());

Three shapes in one file. Device tokens are fully owner-writable. Conditions are owner-readable and nothing else. Alerts are owner-readable and owner-updatable, so the app can set read_at, but no client can insert one; the cron job inserts with the service role. Note that the update policy is on the whole row, not on the column: a client could rewrite an alert's title with its own session. The policy states an intent ("the only write the app makes") that the database does not enforce column by column.

0005 · daily_readings

TableColumnsKeys and indexes
daily_readingsid uuid, user_id, for_date date, slot text check in ('morning','night'), tradition text default 'vedic', title, body, detail, created_at, read_atPK id; unique (user_id, for_date, slot); index (user_id, for_date desc); FK cascade

The two comments in this file are the design. On for_date: "The user's own local date, not UTC: 'my reading for the 26th' has to mean their 26th." On the unique constraint: "One reading per slot per day. This is also what makes the job safe to run more often than expected, or twice by accident." The job in lib/alerts/predictions.ts reads which slots already exist for the user's local date and inserts the missing one; a second run the same morning finds the row and does nothing. Policies are select and update for the owner, the same shape as alerts, and for the same reason: the app marks a reading read, the server writes it.

0006 · life_events

TableColumnsKeys and indexes
life_eventsid uuid, user_id, occurred_on date, precision text default 'day' check in ('day','month','year'), title text check length 1 to 120, note text check length at most 2000, source text default 'manual' check in ('manual','extracted'), created_atPK id; index (user_id, occurred_on); FK cascade
life_event_scansuser_id uuid, scanned_at timestamptz, found_count integer default 0PK user_id; FK cascade

The header comment marks this as "the one table in the schema holding what the user tells us about their own life rather than what the ephemeris computes, so it is owner-only in both directions: no admin read policy, no service-role writes outside the extraction job acting for that same user." Both tables get a single for all owner policy, and in code they are written only through the bearer-or-cookie client, never the admin one. precision exists because people remember "March 2021", not an hour; source distinguishes a typed event from one proposed by mining the person's own chat history, "so a bad extraction run can be undone without touching hand-entered events". life_event_scans is one row per user remembering that the mining ran and how many candidates it found, so the offer to scan appears once (chapter 8).

0007 · period_readings

TableColumnsKeys and indexes
period_readingsuser_id, lord text, period_start date, theme text check length 1 to 80, meaning text check length 1 to 800, events_hash text, generated_atPK (user_id, lord, period_start); FK cascade

One row per mahadasha, plus one row with lord = 'now' for the current mahadasha and antardasha pair, keyed on the antardasha's start "so it goes stale by itself when the sub-period changes". events_hash records which pinned moments the text was written against; when the events inside a period change, the hash no longer matches and the explain route rewrites that period on the next open. The composite primary key is what the explain route's upsert(rows, { onConflict: "user_id,lord,period_start" }) relies on. The policy is one for all owner rule, with the comment "Written by the explain route acting as the user, never by admin tooling." No table in the schema has a touch_updated_at style trigger; every timestamp is a column default or a value the application writes.

13.3The entity relationships

The shape matters for two operations. Account deletion (app/api/account/route.ts) is one call, admin.auth.admin.deleteUser(user.id), and the cascades do the rest; the route's comment lists profiles, birth profiles, conversations and messages, and the same on delete cascade is on every other table too. And any query the application makes needs at most one join, messages to conversations, which is the join the msg self policy performs on every access to a message.

13.4Every table, its RLS shape, and who touches it

The table below was built by reading every .from("...") call under app/ and lib/, excluding tests, and noting which client made it. "User client" means the cookie client (lib/supabase/server.ts) or the bearer client (lib/supabase/route.ts), both of which evaluate RLS as the signed-in user. "Admin client" means createAdminSupabase(), which bypasses RLS. The last column lists the iOS stores that query the table directly over PostgREST with the user's own session, which is the same trust level as the user client and the reason the policies exist at all.

TableRLS shapeRead byWritten byiOS direct
adminsSelect for self or any admin; no write policylib/admin/guard.ts isAdminUser (admin client)Nothing in code; a hand-written insertNo
profilesOwner for allNothingThe on_auth_user_created trigger onlyNo
birth_profilesOwner for alllib/data/birthProfile.ts (user); app/api/chat, app/api/kundli, lib/timeline/load.ts (user); lib/alerts/run.ts, lib/alerts/predictions.ts, lib/admin/data.ts (admin)saveBirthProfile upsert on user_id and ensureCurrentChart update, both user client, from the intake action and app/api/profileProfileStore reads
conversationsOwner for alllib/data/chat.ts, app/api/timeline/scan, lib/timeline/load.ts (user); lib/admin/data.ts (admin)lib/data/chat.ts getOrCreateConversation insert (user)ChatStore reads
messagesOwner via conversations, for alllib/data/chat.ts, app/api/timeline/scan, lib/timeline/load.ts count (user); lib/admin/data.ts (admin)lib/data/chat.ts appendMessage insert (user)ChatStore reads
device_tokensOwner for alllib/alerts/run.ts, lib/alerts/predictions.ts (admin)app/api/devices upsert on token and delete (admin); the alert and prediction senders delete dead tokens (admin)No; the app calls the route
transit_conditionsOwner select onlylib/alerts/run.ts (admin)lib/alerts/run.ts insert and update ended_on (admin)No
alertsOwner select and updateNothing under app/ or lib/lib/alerts/run.ts insert (admin)AlertsStore reads and sets read_at
daily_readingsOwner select and updatelib/alerts/predictions.ts reads existing slots (admin)lib/alerts/predictions.ts insert (admin)DailyStore and IntentData read; DailyStore sets read_at
life_eventsOwner for alllib/timeline/load.ts, app/api/timeline/scan (user)app/api/life-events insert and delete (user)No; the app calls the routes
life_event_scansOwner for alllib/timeline/load.ts (user)app/api/timeline/scan upsert (user)No
period_readingsOwner for alllib/timeline/load.ts (user)app/api/timeline/explain upsert (user)No

Four things in that table are worth a second look.

  • profiles is write-only. The trigger fills it and nothing reads it. The name the app shows comes from birth_profiles.first_name; the admin page shows the email from auth.users. The table is a leftover from the first design and costs one insert per sign-up.
  • device_tokens has an owner policy that no code uses. The devices route writes with the admin client, and its comment explains: "a device handed from one account to another has to change owner, and the owner-only RLS policy would (correctly) refuse that update. The user id still comes from the verified session, never from the body." The policy remains so a client could read its own registrations; today none does.
  • alerts is never read on the web. Only the iOS app lists alerts and marks them read, directly against PostgREST. The web has no inbox (chapter 9).
  • The timeline tables are the only ones written as the user from a route handler. life_events, life_event_scans and period_readings are inserted, upserted and deleted through the bearer-or-cookie client, so RLS is the authorisation. Every other server-side write goes through the admin client, and every one of those routes takes the user id from the verified session first.
A rejected write does not throw supabase-js resolves with an error field when RLS or a constraint rejects a write; it does not throw. The comment in lib/data/birthProfile.ts spells this out and is why every write in lib/data/ checks error and turns it into a throw. A read blocked by RLS is worse: it returns an empty result that looks exactly like "no rows yet". That is what a missing policy, or a missing session on a bearer client, looks like from a route handler, and it is the first thing to suspect when a signed-in user sees an empty home.

13.5The avatars bucket

The one piece of storage is not in any migration. The README's architecture sketch lists "Storage (avatars)" beside Postgres and Auth, and points at docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md for "the storage bucket", but that document has no bucket step, so the bucket is created in the dashboard by hand. The code tells you what it must look like. 

// app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts (the same function is in app/api/profile/route.ts)
async function uploadAvatar(userId: string, file: File): Promise<string | null> {
  const ext = file.type === "image/png" ? "png" : file.type === "image/webp" ? "webp" : "jpg";
  const path = `${userId}/avatar.${ext}`;
  const bytes = Buffer.from(await file.arrayBuffer());
  const admin = createAdminSupabase();
  const { error } = await admin.storage
    .from("avatars")
    .upload(path, bytes, { contentType: file.type, upsert: true });
  if (error) return null;
  const { data } = admin.storage.from("avatars").getPublicUrl(path);
  // cache-bust so a replaced photo shows immediately
  return `${data.publicUrl}?v=${Date.now()}`;
}

Four facts follow. The upload is made with the service-role client, so the bucket needs no storage policies for writes and none exist in the repo. The object name is {user id}/avatar.{ext} with upsert: true, so each person has at most one photo per extension, and a person who uploads a PNG after a JPEG has two objects with only the newer one referenced. The URL stored in birth_profiles.avatar_url comes from getPublicUrl, which only works if the bucket is public; the home page, the profile page and the admin list all render it in a plain <img> with no token. And a failed upload returns null, which saveBirthProfile reads as "keep the old photo": the comment there says "Only overwrite the avatar when a new one was uploaded". The same function is duplicated in app/api/profile/route.ts for native clients, byte for byte. Nothing in the code deletes an object, on account deletion or otherwise.

13.6The anon key and the service-role key

Supabase issues two keys for a project and the code names them by their environment variables. NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY is public by design; the README says it is "safe to ship in the iOS binary", and the NEXT_PUBLIC_ prefix means Next inlines it into browser bundles. SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY is read only through lib/env.ts, which imports server-only so any client component that reaches for it fails the build. The policies in this chapter are what make the first key safe and the second one necessary.

ClientFileKeyRuns asUsed from
Browserlib/supabase/client.tsanonThe signed-in user, via the session in cookiesNothing today; no client component imports it
Cookielib/supabase/server.tsanonThe signed-in user, via the session in cookiesServer components and server actions; lib/supabase/middleware.ts builds the same kind of client over the request's cookies
Bearerlib/supabase/route.tsanon, plus Authorization: Bearer forwardedThe user whose JWT the iOS app sent; falls back to the cookie clientEvery app/api/* route that acts for a user; cron has no session and geocode needs none
Adminlib/supabase/admin.tsservice roleNobody: RLS is bypassedCron jobs, admin pages, device registration, avatar upload, account deletion

With the anon key, every query runs as the authenticated role if a valid JWT is attached, or as anon if none is. Every policy in the schema compares a column to auth.uid(). For the anon role that function returns null, no row matches, and every table reads as empty and refuses every write. So the anon key on its own can do nothing, which is what makes it publishable. With a JWT attached the same key sees exactly the rows whose user_id (or id, or conversation) is the token's subject, which is why listConversations and getBirthProfile need no user filter and why the iOS stores can query PostgREST directly.

The service role bypasses RLS entirely. Reading the policies tells you where it is unavoidable:

  • transit_conditions, alerts and daily_readings have no insert policy. Their writer is the cron job in lib/alerts/, which has no user session because no user is present at 02:30 UTC. It runs as the service role and iterates every birth_profiles row, which an owner policy would never let one session do.
  • admins has no write policy, and its read policy calls is_admin(). requireAdmin() could read it as the signed-in user; instead isAdminUser uses the admin client, and lib/admin/data.ts then reads other people's birth_profiles, conversations and messages, which no policy allows and which is the point of an admin page. Every admin function calls requireAdmin() first; the service role is behind a check, never in front of one.
  • device_tokens needs to change owner, and the owner policy forbids that, so the devices route uses the admin client after verifying the session.
  • Account deletion and the avatar upload are Auth and Storage operations, not table writes; auth.admin.deleteUser exists only on the service role, and the bucket has no policies because only the service role writes it.

Everything else runs as the user. The rule in the code is simple to state and easy to check with a grep for createAdminSupabase: outside tests it appears in ten files, every one of them either imports server-only itself or is a route handler or server action, and the comment in lib/supabase/admin.ts is the rule in one line: "Bypasses RLS. Only ever import from server code behind an admin auth check."

What the policies do not check An owner policy proves the row belongs to the caller. It does not check columns: the update policies on alerts and daily_readings permit rewriting the title as well as read_at, and the for all policy on birth_profiles lets a client write any chart JSON it likes. The application writes chart only from the Swiss Ephemeris on the server, but the database would accept a hand-made one from a valid session. The schema trusts its owners with their own rows completely; it trusts them with nobody else's at all.

13.7Study notes

Read in this order

  1. supabase/migrations/0001_init.sql, for the trigger, the five policies and the comment about admin reads.
  2. supabase/migrations/0004_alerts_push.sql, for the three policy shapes in one file and the partial unique index.
  3. supabase/migrations/0005_daily_readings.sql, two comments that are the design.
  4. supabase/migrations/0006_life_events.sql and 0007_period_readings.sql, the owner-only-in-both-directions tables.
  5. lib/supabase/route.ts, how one function serves cookies and bearer tokens.
  6. lib/supabase/admin.ts and lib/env.ts, the service role and server-only.
  7. app/api/devices/route.ts, the one route whose comment argues against its own table's policy.
  8. lib/data/birthProfile.ts, for the note on rejected writes resolving rather than throwing.
  9. docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md, section 3, for what an unapplied migration looks like in production.

Check yourself

Why can the cron job not use the cookie or bearer client?

It has no session: no user is signed in when Vercel calls the route, and it must read every user's birth_profiles row and insert readings for each. Only the service role sees across users, so the job is written for it and the tables it writes have no insert policies at all.

A user's listConversations() returns an empty list although they have chats. What are the first two suspects?

A missing or expired session on the client that made the call (RLS returns nothing, not an error), or a policy that does not match the row. Neither throws; both look like "no data".

Why is the token, not the user, the primary key of device_tokens?

One person can have several phones and one phone can be signed into a different account later. A token must belong to exactly one user at a time, and moving it is an upsert on the token, which is why the route writes it as the service role.

What does unique (user_id, for_date, slot) on daily_readings buy?

Idempotence for the twice-daily job. A second run for the same local date and slot finds the row and writes nothing, so running the job more often, or twice by accident, is safe.

Which tables does the service role never touch, and why does that matter?

life_events, life_event_scans and period_readings. They hold what the person said about their own life, so the migration deliberately gives them no admin path; their routes act as the user and RLS is the whole authorisation.

Why does deploying a commit that adds a migration need a manual step, and in what order?

Nothing applies migrations automatically and main deploys on push. The SQL must be run against production, lowest number first, before the code that reads the new table takes traffic; otherwise every route that touches it errors until it is.

Chapter fourteen

14The iOS app

Astrya for iOS is a SwiftUI app that talks to the same route handlers and the same Supabase project as the web app, holds no secret the web does not also publish, and is built from a 127-line YAML spec rather than a checked-in Xcode project. Everything about how it is assembled, configured, signed and shipped follows from that.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It covers the plumbing under the features: the generated project and its three targets, the generated configuration file, the entry point and the routing state machine, the design tokens, the API client and its streaming reader, the plist and entitlement choices, how the XCTest suite is organised, and the two GitHub Actions workflows that build it on every push and ship it to TestFlight on every tag. The features themselves (chat, kundli, timeline, alerts, on-device answers) live in their own chapters; chapter 10 in particular covers the widget extension and the App Group cache.

14.1One spec, three targets

The Xcode project is generated. mobile/project.yml is the whole description of the app for XcodeGen, and .gitignore excludes mobile/Sanchara.xcodeproj/, mobile/build/, mobile/SourcePackages/ and the generated sources folder. The comment in the ignore file is the rule: "Run cd mobile && xcodegen generate after changing the spec." The practical consequence is that adding a Swift file means regenerating the project, and that two engineers can never diverge on a project.pbxproj merge, because there is no such file to merge. 

# mobile/project.yml
name: Astrya
options:
  bundleIdPrefix: com.shivvyas
  deploymentTarget:
    iOS: "17.0"
  createIntermediateGroups: true
  groupSortPosition: top

packages:
  Supabase:
    url: https://github.com/supabase/supabase-swift
    from: "2.0.0"

settings:
  base:
    SWIFT_VERSION: "5.0"
    ENABLE_USER_SCRIPT_SANDBOXING: YES
    CODE_SIGN_STYLE: Automatic

The base settings also name the development team, which this handbook does not reproduce. Signing is automatic everywhere: locally through Xcode, on CI by turning signing off, and in the TestFlight workflow through an App Store Connect API key. One package is declared, supabase-swift at 2.0.0 or later, and only the app target links it.

TargetTypeBundle idSourcesDepends on
Astryaapplicationcom.shivvyas.astraSanchara/Sources, Sanchara/Resources as a resources phasethe Supabase package product; the SancharaWidgets target
SancharaWidgetsapp-extensioncom.shivvyas.astra.widgetsSancharaWidgets plus six app files by pathnothing
SancharaTestsbundle.unit-testcom.shivvyas.astra.testsSanchara/Teststhe Astrya target

Three per-target settings are worth knowing. GENERATE_INFOPLIST_FILE is NO for the app and the extension, because each keeps a real Info.plist that the TestFlight workflow edits with PlistBuddy; the test bundle lets Xcode generate its own. TARGETED_DEVICE_FAMILY is "1", iPhone only. And the extension sets SKIP_INSTALL: YES, the standard way to keep an embedded extension out of the archive's products list. The extension carries its own deploymentTarget of iOS 17.0 with a comment noting that widgets and controls arrived on that schedule while the app itself still runs on iOS 17; the newer surfaces (controls on iOS 18, snippets and the on-device model on iOS 26) are gated in code with #available, not by raising the floor.

14.2Configuration and the icon

make-config.sh and the generated AppConfig

# mobile/scripts/make-config.sh
read_var() { grep -E "^$1=" "$ENV_FILE" | head -1 | cut -d= -f2- | tr -d '"' | tr -d "'" | tr -d '\r'; }

SUPABASE_URL="$(read_var NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL)"
SUPABASE_ANON_KEY="$(read_var NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY)"
API_BASE_URL="${SANCHARA_API_BASE_URL:-https://astra.shivvyas.com}"

cat > "$OUT" <<SWIFT
// Generated by mobile/scripts/make-config.sh, do not edit, do not commit.
import Foundation

enum AppConfig {
    static let supabaseURL = URL(string: "$SUPABASE_URL")!
    static let supabaseAnonKey = "$SUPABASE_ANON_KEY"
    static let apiBaseURL = URL(string: "$API_BASE_URL")!
}
SWIFT

The app has no secrets file and no build-time environment; it has one generated Swift file, mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Generated/AppConfig.swift, written by mobile/scripts/make-config.sh from the repo's .env.local. The script reads exactly two variables, the same two the web app ships to browsers: NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL and NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY. Its header comment states the boundary: those values "are designed to ship in clients (RLS is the security boundary, not secrecy)", while SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY "are never read here and must never reach the app binary." The script fails loudly if either value is missing. A third value, SANCHARA_API_BASE_URL, is read from the shell environment rather than the file and defaults to the production host, which is how a developer points a build at a local or preview API without touching the checked-in code. The output folder is gitignored, so the generated file exists on every machine that built the app and in no commit.

Three places consume the three constants. Supa.client is constructed from supabaseURL and supabaseAnonKey. SancharaAPI.request builds every URL by appending a path to apiBaseURL. And AuthStore uses apiBaseURL to form the auth/callback redirect for magic-link email.

make-icon.py

The app icon is not a design file; it is mobile/scripts/make-icon.py, a Pillow script that writes Sanchara/Resources/Assets.xcassets/AppIcon.appiconset/AppIcon.png. The docstring describes the mark: "a warm disc with a soft corona on the near-black ground, using the same tokens as app/globals.css." The three colours are literally the site's tokens (BG 0x0A0A0B, ACCENT 0xE8663D, FG 0xF4F1EA). The script renders on a 4096-pixel canvas and downsamples to 1024 with Lanczos so the edges stay clean at small sizes. It builds the image in four passes: sixty concentric translucent rings blurred into a glow for the corona, the disc itself with a warm vertical falloff so it reads as lit, a thin bright limb along the upper edge masked by its own falloff so it fades toward the equator, and the final resize. The disc sits at 46 percent of the height rather than centred, with a radius of a fifth of the canvas. Changing the icon means changing numbers in a script and re-running it, which is the same discipline as the generated project and the generated config: nothing binary is hand-made.

14.3Entry point and routing

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/SancharaApp.swift
@main
struct SancharaApp: App {
    // APNs callbacks have no SwiftUI equivalent, so the app keeps a delegate.
    @UIApplicationDelegateAdaptor(SancharaAppDelegate.self) private var appDelegate
    @State private var auth = AuthStore()

    var body: some Scene {
        WindowGroup {
            RootView()
                .environment(auth)
                .preferredColorScheme(.dark)
        }
    }
}

Sixteen lines. One AuthStore is created here and passed down through the environment; nothing else in the app creates one. The app delegate exists for one reason the comment gives, APNs token and notification callbacks, and it lives in Services/Push.swift. The dark colour scheme is forced at the root and again in Info.plist, which section 14.6 returns to.

RootView is a switch over AuthStore.State, and SignedInView is a switch over ProfileStore.State. Between them they are the whole routing policy of the app: there is no tab bar and nothing at the root remembers a screen. ChatView owns the one NavigationStack that matters and presents history, profile, kundli, timeline and alerts as sheets. 

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/RootView.swift
var body: some View {
    Group {
        switch auth.state {
        case .loading:
            LoadingScreen()
        case .signedOut:
            AuthView()
        case .awaitingCode(let email, let purpose):
            VerifyCodeView(email: email, purpose: purpose)
        case .signedIn:
            SignedInView()
        }
    }
    .task { await auth.start() }
    .onOpenURL { url in
        // Two kinds of URL arrive here: Supabase's auth callback, and our
        // own scheme from a widget tap. Only the first is for the auth
        // store; handing it a sanchara:// URL would show "link expired".
        if DeepLink.isDeepLink(url) {
            DeepLink.shared.handle(url: url)
        } else {
            Task { await auth.handle(url: url) }
        }
    }
}

AuthStore.start() sets the first state from whether supabase-swift has a session in the Keychain, then loops over authStateChanges for the life of the app: .signedIn and .tokenRefreshed with a session move to .signedIn, .signedOut moves to .signedOut. The awaitingCode state carries the email and a CodePurpose, either .signIn (a passwordless code) or .confirmSignUp (confirming the address on a password account), which decides which Supabase OTP type verifies it. A password sign-in that fails with "not confirmed" lands in awaitingCode rather than an error, which is how an unconfirmed account recovers.

SignedInView owns a ProfileStore and calls load() in a .task. The store seeds the chart and details from the App Group cache, fetches the birth_profiles row that has a non-null chart, and lands on .needsIntake when there is none or .ready when there is one. IntakeView is handed a closure that calls load() again once the profile is saved, so the same switch carries the person from intake to chat without any navigation code. .failed is reached only when the fetch throws and the cache had nothing; it shows the message, a "Try again" button that reloads, and a "Sign out" button. The web app makes the same decision in a redirect: /api/chat refuses a user with no chart and the chat page sends them to intake.

14.4Design: tokens, components, the card

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift
enum Theme {
    /// `--bg: #0a0a0b`
    static let bg = Color(hex: 0x0A0A0B)
    /// `--fg: #f4f1ea`
    static let fg = Color(hex: 0xF4F1EA)
    /// `--muted: #9a978f`
    static let muted = Color(hex: 0x9A978F)
    /// `--accent: #e8663d`
    static let accent = Color(hex: 0xE8663D)

    /// Tailwind `border-white/10`, used for every hairline on the site.
    static let hairline = Color.white.opacity(0.10)
    /// Tailwind `bg-white/[0.04]`, the fill behind form controls.
    static let fieldFill = Color.white.opacity(0.04)
    /// Tailwind `rounded-lg` is 0.5rem.
    static let cornerRadius: CGFloat = 8
    static let chartLine = Color.white.opacity(0.22)
    /// The site's easing: `cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1)`.
    static let ease = Animation.timingCurve(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1, duration: 0.6)
}

The web app defines exactly four colours on :root in app/globals.css, and Theme carries the same four hex values with the CSS variable quoted above each. The file's comment gives the reason: matching them exactly "is what makes the native app read as the same product as the site." ThemeTests asserts each of the four against its CSS token, so a careless edit fails the build rather than drifting. The Color(hex:) initialiser exists so tokens can be written the way they appear in CSS. Two derived values are documented as departures: chartLine is heavier than hairline because the kundli's rule lines are the content, not a divider, and at 0.10 white "the square all but vanished on an OLED screen in daylight". A Text.eyebrow() extension reproduces the site's eyebrow style at 12pt with 3.6 points of tracking, which is 0.3em.

Components.swift holds four controls, each a transcription of a Tailwind class list: SancharaField (the site's input, 16pt text because that is the size the web forces on phones so Safari does not zoom, and "the size is the look"), SancharaPrimaryButton (foreground on background, with a spinner that replaces the title while loading and disables the button), SancharaSecondaryButton (a white-at-15-percent border and muted text), and SancharaSecureField, a password field with an eye toggle, because "choosing a password you cannot see is the most common reason a sign-up attempt fails twice."

ReadingCard.swift is the third file and the only one that leaves the app: it is compiled into the widget extension and rendered by the Siri snippet, so an eyebrow, a serif headline and a muted body look the same in three places. Its body parameter is named text rather than body, because body is the View requirement. It sets its own colours rather than reading the environment, for the reason given in its comment: the app is dark-only, "and a card that went light inside Siri while the app stayed dark would read as a different product."

14.5Services: the client and the session

One Supabase client

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Supabase.swift
enum Supa {
    static let client = SupabaseClient(
        supabaseURL: AppConfig.supabaseURL,
        supabaseKey: AppConfig.supabaseAnonKey
    )

    /// The current access token, for `Authorization: Bearer` against the
    /// Astrya API on Vercel. Returns nil when signed out.
    static func accessToken() async -> String? {
        try? await client.auth.session.accessToken
    }
}

supabase-swift persists the session in the Keychain by default on Apple platforms, which the comment notes is what the design requires: never UserDefaults. The stores read tables directly through this client (birth_profiles, daily_readings, conversations, messages) under the owner-only RLS policies, and everything that needs a secret or the ephemeris goes through SancharaAPI with the session's access token as a bearer.

SancharaAPI

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift
enum APIError: LocalizedError {
    case unauthorized
    case server(String)

    var errorDescription: String? {
        switch self {
        case .unauthorized: "Your session expired. Please sign in again."
        case .server(let message): message
        }
    }
}

private static func request(_ path: String, method: String) async throws -> URLRequest {
    var req = URLRequest(url: AppConfig.apiBaseURL.appendingPathComponent(path))
    req.httpMethod = method
    guard let token = await Supa.accessToken() else { throw APIError.unauthorized }
    req.setValue("Bearer \(token)", forHTTPHeaderField: "Authorization")
    return req
}

private static func check(_ response: URLResponse, _ data: Data) throws {
    guard let http = response as? HTTPURLResponse else { return }
    switch http.statusCode {
    case 200..<300: return
    case 401: throw APIError.unauthorized
    default:
        let message = String(data: data, encoding: .utf8) ?? "Something went wrong."
        throw APIError.server(message)
    }
}

The client is an enum of static functions, one per route handler, each a few lines: request builds the URL and attaches the bearer, URLSession.shared performs it, check maps the status. There are two error cases and no more. A 401 is unauthorized, which every screen renders as a sign-in prompt; anything else outside 2xx is server(message) carrying the response body verbatim, because the route handlers already write their errors as sentences. A missing token throws before any network call. The route handlers themselves are the same ones the web app calls; the /api/geocode call is the one exception to the bearer rule because that route is public, and the comment on it says so.

FunctionRouteNotes
saveProfilePOST /api/profileMultipart body built by BirthProfileInput.multipartBody; the optional JPEG travels as a photo part
kundliPDFGET /api/kundliReturns the PDF bytes
deleteAccountDELETE /api/accountRequired by App Store guideline 5.1.1(v)
geocodeGET /api/geocode?q=Public, no token; returns coordinates with the timezone the ephemeris needs
timeline, explainTimeline, scanLifeEventsGET /api/timeline, POST /api/timeline/explain, POST /api/timeline/scanThe two POSTs set a 120-second timeout because one model call covers all twelve periods, or a whole transcript
addLifeEvents, deleteLifeEventPOST and DELETE /api/life-eventsBatch add; single delete
registerDevice, unregisterDevicePOST and DELETE /api/devicesThe APNs token and its environment
chatStreamPOST /api/chatAn AsyncThrowingStream<ChatEvent, Error>, below

Streaming a reading

// mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift
if let id = http.value(forHTTPHeaderField: "x-conversation-id") {
    continuation.yield(.conversationId(id))
}

var undecoded = Data()
var batch = ""
for try await byte in bytes {
    undecoded.append(byte)
    guard let piece = String(data: undecoded, encoding: .utf8) else { continue }
    undecoded.removeAll(keepingCapacity: true)
    batch += piece
    if batch.count >= 24 {
        continuation.yield(.text(batch))
        batch = ""
    }
}
if !batch.isEmpty { continuation.yield(.text(batch)) }
continuation.finish()

/api/chat answers with plain UTF-8 text chunks rather than server-sent events, so the client reads raw bytes with URLSession.shared.bytes(for:). Two things follow. A chunk boundary can split a multi-byte character, so bytes are held in undecoded until they decode as a whole string. And byte-at-a-time updates would redraw the transcript thousands of times for one reading, so text is coalesced into batches of at least 24 characters before being yielded. The conversation id arrives first, from the x-conversation-id response header, before any text; ChatStore keeps it and sends it back on the next turn so the server appends to the same conversation, exactly as components/Chat.tsx does on the web. The request timeout is 120 seconds because readings can think for a while before the first byte. A non-2xx status is read to the end and thrown as server(body); a 401 is unauthorized. The stream's onTermination cancels the underlying task, which is what lets a new reading supersede one still streaming without leaking the connection.

14.6Info.plist and the entitlements

mobile/Sanchara/Info.plist is short and every key in it is a decision. It is also declared a second time as info.properties in project.yml, which is the copy with the comments.

KeyValueWhy
UIUserInterfaceStyleDark"The app is dark by design, the cosmic theme has no light variant." SancharaApp repeats it with preferredColorScheme(.dark).
UISupportedInterfaceOrientationsportrait onlyOne orientation, one device family (iPhone).
CFBundleURLTypesscheme sanchara, name com.shivvyas.astra"sanchara://timeline, how a tap on the period widget lands in the app. Universal Links cover the web; this covers the Home Screen."
UILaunchScreencolour LaunchBackgroundA colour set in the asset catalog, so the launch frame is the same near-black as the first screen.
ITSAppUsesNonExemptEncryptionfalseSkips the export-compliance question on every TestFlight upload.
CFBundleShortVersionString, CFBundleVersion1.0, 1Placeholders. The TestFlight workflow overwrites both from the tag and the run number.

mobile/Sanchara/Sanchara.entitlements holds four capabilities, each with a comment in project.yml pointing at the portal step it needs.

  • aps-environment: development. The comment is a warning: change it to production for TestFlight and App Store builds, because "a token minted under one environment is invalid in the other." The TestFlight workflow makes that edit itself, and ApsEnvironment.current in Services/Push.swift reads the answer back from the embedded provisioning profile at runtime rather than trusting the build configuration, because a Release build run from Xcode still carries the development entitlement. The server is told sandbox unless the profile literally says production.
  • com.apple.developer.applesignin: Default. Sign in with Apple; the App ID and Supabase's Apple provider both need enabling, per docs/APPLE_SIGN_IN.md.
  • com.apple.developer.associated-domains: applinks: and webcredentials: for the production host. The first makes the magic-link and confirmation emails open the app; the second lets the system password manager offer saved credentials.
  • com.apple.security.application-groups: group.com.shivvyas.astra. The cache chapter 10 is built on. The extension's own entitlements file contains this key and nothing else.

14.7The test suite

One test target, SancharaTests, depends on the app and uses @testable import Astrya. There is no UI test target and no test that needs a network, a session or Apple Intelligence: everything that decides something is a pure function or a value type, and the tests read as lists of the rules in plain English. Fourteen files, 95 test methods.

FileTestsWhat it pins
ApsEnvironmentTests6The provisioning-profile parser: development reads as sandbox, production as production, and no plist, no entitlement, an unknown value or a simulator build all fall back to sandbox.
AuthValidationTests4Email validation accepts ordinary addresses and rejects malformed ones; code entry keeps only digits; the Apple sign-in nonce is random and hashed.
CandidateResolverTests9How a proposed timeline moment becomes a pinned one: dated candidates pass through, undated ones need a year and land on 1 January at year precision, and the payloads from older servers still decode.
ChartFactsTests6The on-device table's derived rows, the clean fallback without them, and a sweep for forbidden vocabulary.
DailyReadingTests4The readings inbox groups newest day first with morning above night, and labels today and yesterday as words.
DerivedFactsTests4NatalChart decodes with or without a derived block, ignores undeclared keys, and tolerates an absent placement.
KundliSnapshotTests1Renders the kundli to a PNG for inspection and asserts only that something was drawn; a pixel assertion "that fails whenever anyone touches a colour is a test that gets deleted."
KundliTests12Which rashi lands in which house, that the twelve regions tile the square exactly, that planets file under their own house, degrees print as degrees and minutes, every graha has a glyph, and every label anchor sits inside its own house.
OnDeviceRoutingTests15The cheap filter, the house-number parser, the rows the table returns, and that nothing handed to the model names astrology.
PeriodProgressTests8The period widget's fraction and years-left maths, agreement with the timeline's own progress, and the period cache's round trip and clear.
ProfilePhotoTests4Large photos are downscaled and small ones left alone; the multipart body carries a photo part only when one was picked.
SuggestionTests6Three starters per mode, fallback when the on-device model is unavailable, and a settled result when it is.
ThemeTests4The four colour tokens match app/globals.css exactly.
TimelineTests12Band lengths and year labels, progress clamping, which dasha pair a moment fell in, date rendering to the precision given, and that dates parse in UTC rather than the device zone.

Two conventions hold across the suite. Test classes that drive a @MainActor type (SuggestionTests, DailyReadingTests) are themselves @MainActor, and the rest are plain XCTestCases over value types. And tests that depend on the environment skip rather than fail: SuggestionTests uses XCTSkipIf and XCTSkipUnless on SuggestionEngine.isOnDeviceAvailable, so the same file passes on a Mac, on the simulator and on a device with Apple Intelligence, asserting a different thing in each.

14.8CI and TestFlight

The CI job

.github/workflows/ci.yml runs on every push to main and every pull request, with a concurrency group per ref so a newer push cancels an older run. It has two jobs; the ios job runs on macos-26 with mobile as its working directory and does, in order:

  1. Selects the newest Xcode 26 on the runner with xcode-select and prints its version.
  2. Installs XcodeGen with Homebrew.
  3. Writes a placeholder .env.local with a fake Supabase URL and anon key, then runs ./scripts/make-config.sh. The comment explains: the generated file "is never committed. The simulator build only needs the values to be present, not real."
  4. Runs xcodegen generate.
  5. Restores mobile/SourcePackages from the Actions cache, keyed on the hash of project.yml, so the Supabase package is not re-cloned on every run.
  6. Picks the first available iPhone simulator by name and runs xcodebuild test against it with CODE_SIGNING_ALLOWED=NO and -clonedSourcePackagesDirPath SourcePackages. Output is teed to xcodebuild.log and filtered to errors, warnings from the app's own sources, and the test suite and test case result lines, with set -o pipefail so the filter cannot hide a failure.
  7. Uploads the full log as an artifact, but only on failure.

No signing, no secrets, no device. The whole XCTest suite runs unsigned on a simulator, which is possible because nothing in it needs an entitlement to be present: ChartCache falls back to the caches directory when the App Group container is missing, and the tests that need Apple Intelligence skip.

The TestFlight workflow

.github/workflows/ios-testflight.yml runs on every tag matching v* and by hand from the Actions tab, in a single concurrency group that does not cancel in progress. It needs five repository secrets, checked up front so a missing one fails in the first step with its name: SUPABASE_URL and SUPABASE_ANON_KEY, which are baked into AppConfig.swift through the same script the CI job uses; and ASC_KEY_ID, ASC_ISSUER_ID and ASC_KEY_P8, an App Store Connect API key with the App Manager role and its .p8 contents. The header comment states the consequence: "Signing is automatic: with the API key, Xcode creates or refreshes the distribution certificate and profile itself. No certificates are stored here." 

# .github/workflows/ios-testflight.yml
- name: Set version and build number
  run: |
    BUILD="${GITHUB_RUN_NUMBER}"
    for plist in Sanchara/Info.plist SancharaWidgets/Info.plist; do
      if [[ "${GITHUB_REF_TYPE}" == "tag" ]]; then
        /usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c "Set :CFBundleShortVersionString ${GITHUB_REF_NAME#v}" "$plist"
      fi
      /usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c "Set :CFBundleVersion ${BUILD}" "$plist"
    done

- name: Use production push environment
  run: /usr/libexec/PlistBuddy -c "Set :aps-environment production" Sanchara/Sanchara.entitlements

After the same Xcode, XcodeGen, config and generate steps as CI, the workflow edits three files in place. The marketing version comes from the tag with its v stripped (v0.3.0 becomes 0.3.0); on a manual run the value in the plist is kept. The build number is the workflow run number, which App Store Connect requires to be unique per version, and it is written to both the app's and the extension's plist because the two must agree. Then aps-environment is switched to production in the entitlements file, because "push tokens minted under the development environment are rejected by production APNs, so store builds must carry the production entitlement." The API key is written to the runner's private_keys folder with mode 600.

Two xcodebuild invocations follow. archive builds the Astrya scheme in Release for generic/platform=iOS into build/Sanchara.xcarchive, with -allowProvisioningUpdates and the three authentication-key flags so Xcode can mint the distribution profile. -exportArchive then reads mobile/ExportOptions.plist and, because that file sets method to app-store-connect and destination to upload, sends the build straight to App Store Connect instead of writing an .ipa. The plist also sets automatic signing, uploadSymbols true so crash logs symbolicate, and manageAppVersionAndBuildNumber false so the numbers the workflow just wrote are the ones that ship; it names the team, which this handbook does not print. Both logs are uploaded as an artifact whether or not the run succeeded.

Why the version lives in the tag The web app deploys from main on every push, and the changelog and package.json carry the version. The iOS build number cannot come from those, because two uploads of the same version need different build numbers, and the marketing version should not be edited by hand in two plists. Deriving one from the tag and the other from the run number means a release is git tag v0.3.0 && git push --tags and nothing else on the iOS side.

14.9Study notes

Read in this order

  1. mobile/project.yml, for the three targets, the entitlements with their comments, and the six files the extension borrows.
  2. mobile/scripts/make-config.sh, for the three constants and the two variables it refuses to read.
  3. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/SancharaApp.swift and RootView.swift, for the whole navigation policy in under a hundred lines.
  4. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Auth/AuthStore.swift, State and start(), for how the left half of the state machine moves.
  5. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/ProfileStore.swift, for the right half and the offline rule.
  6. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Design/Theme.swift, then app/globals.css beside it.
  7. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/SancharaAPI.swift, request, check and chatStream.
  8. mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Push.swift, ApsEnvironment, for why the push environment is read from the profile.
  9. .github/workflows/ci.yml and ios-testflight.yml, for the two build paths, then mobile/ExportOptions.plist.

Check yourself

Why is there no Sanchara.xcodeproj in the repository?

XcodeGen generates it from mobile/project.yml, and both CI and the TestFlight workflow run xcodegen generate themselves. A generated project cannot suffer a pbxproj merge conflict.

Which two environment variables reach the app binary, and which two never do?

NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL and NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY, which the web already ships to browsers and RLS protects. SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY and ANTHROPIC_API_KEY are never read by make-config.sh.

What decides whether a signed-in user sees intake or chat?

ProfileStore.load(): a birth_profiles row with a non-null chart means .ready and ChatView; none means .needsIntake and IntakeView, whose completion closure calls load() again.

Why does chatStream hold bytes back before yielding text?

The route streams raw UTF-8 and a chunk boundary can split a multi-byte character; bytes are kept until they decode, then coalesced into batches of at least 24 characters so the transcript is not redrawn per byte.

The entitlements file says aps-environment: development. How does a TestFlight build end up with production?

The TestFlight workflow edits the file with PlistBuddy before archiving. At runtime ApsEnvironment.current reads the embedded provisioning profile rather than the build configuration, so the server is told the environment the token was actually minted under.

Where does the iOS build number come from?

The GitHub Actions run number, written into both the app's and the extension's Info.plist; the marketing version comes from the tag with the v stripped. ExportOptions.plist sets manageAppVersionAndBuildNumber to false so App Store Connect keeps them.

Why can the whole XCTest suite run unsigned on a simulator?

Nothing in it needs an entitlement: ChartCache falls back to the caches directory without the App Group, and the tests that need Apple Intelligence skip instead of failing.

Chapter fifteen

15Setup, deployment, and release

One repository produces two artifacts: a Next.js app that Vercel builds from every push to main, and an iOS archive that GitHub Actions uploads to TestFlight on every version tag. Nothing secret is in git, the production build needs no secrets to compile, and every one-time step that lives outside the repo is written down so it can be done again.

This chapter describes the repository on main on 30 September 2026, at version 0.3.0. It is the chapter to read before the first npm run dev, before the first deploy to a fresh Vercel project, and before cutting a release. It covers the local setup for the web app and the iOS app, the one-time Supabase and Apple configuration, the Vercel deployment and its two cron schedules, the production checklist that exists because a cron ran for weeks refusing every request, the three GitHub workflows, the release procedure, and the rules about where secrets may live. Where a document under docs/ names an account, a project reference or a machine, this chapter does not; the procedure is the same for whoever holds the keys.

15.1The shape of a release

A commit takes two roads out of the repository. Every push to main is built by Vercel's Git integration and becomes the production web app; the same push runs .github/workflows/ci.yml, which typechecks, tests and builds the web app and builds and tests the iOS app on a simulator. A tag of the form v* takes the second road: release.yml publishes a GitHub release from the matching section of CHANGELOG.md, and ios-testflight.yml archives the iOS app with the version from the tag and uploads it to App Store Connect. There is no deploy step in Actions for the web app, and nothing in the repository makes Vercel wait for CI.

15.2Local setup

The web app

The README lists the prerequisites: Node.js 24, a Supabase project, an Anthropic API key, and for iOS, Xcode 26, XcodeGen and an Apple Developer account. The web app starts in three commands: npm install, cp .env.example .env.local with the values filled in, and npm run dev, which serves on port 3000. The migrations under supabase/migrations/ are applied to the project either with the Supabase CLI (supabase link then supabase db push) or by pasting each file into the SQL editor, lowest number first. There are seven, and the order matters because later files reference tables the earlier ones create: 

supabase/migrations/
0001_init.sql                admins, profiles, birth_profiles, conversations, messages
0002_avatar.sql              avatar_url and the avatars bucket
0003_numerology_mode.sql     the numerology chat mode
0004_alerts_push.sql         device_tokens, transit_conditions, alerts
0005_daily_readings.sql      daily_readings, one row per user, date and slot
0006_life_events.sql         life_events, life_event_scans
0007_period_readings.sql     period_readings

.env.example is the complete list of environment variables, and the comments in it are the documentation. The split between required and optional is enforced in lib/env.ts, which is the only place process.env is read: 

// lib/env.ts
import "server-only";

function required(name: string): string {
  const v = process.env[name];
  if (!v) throw new Error(`Missing required env var: ${name}`);
  return v;
}

function optional(name: string): string | undefined {
  const v = process.env[name];
  return v && v.length > 0 ? v : undefined;
}

export const env = {
  supabaseUrl: () => required("NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL"),
  supabaseAnonKey: () => required("NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY"),
  supabaseServiceRoleKey: () => required("SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY"),
  anthropicApiKey: () => required("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY"),
  cronSecret: () => optional("CRON_SECRET"),
  apnsKeyId: () => optional("APNS_KEY_ID"),
  apnsTeamId: () => optional("APNS_TEAM_ID"),
  apnsBundleId: () => optional("APNS_BUNDLE_ID"),
  apnsPrivateKey: () => optional("APNS_PRIVATE_KEY")?.replace(/\\n/g, "\n"),
};

Two details of this file shape everything downstream. Every entry is a function, not a value, so a variable is read when a request needs it and never at module load or at build time. And the file imports server-only, so the bundler refuses to include it in anything that ships to a browser. Chapter 16 follows both consequences through the failure modes.

VariableRequiredWhere it comes fromWhat stops without it
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URLyesSupabase project settings, APIEvery database and auth call
NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEYyesSame page, the anon key. Designed to ship in clients; Row Level Security is the boundaryEvery database and auth call
SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEYyesSame page, the service_role key. Bypasses RLSAdmin reads, the cron jobs, device registration, avatar upload, account deletion
ANTHROPIC_API_KEYyesThe Anthropic consoleReadings, alerts, extraction, period meanings
CRON_SECRETnoAny long random string; Vercel sends it as a bearer token to scheduled routesThe cron route answers 401 to everyone, including Vercel
APNS_KEY_IDnoThe ten-character id of the APNs keyPushes are skipped; alerts and readings are still written
APNS_TEAM_IDnoThe Apple Developer teamSame
APNS_BUNDLE_IDnoThe app's bundle identifier; .env.example carries it as a defaultSame
APNS_PRIVATE_KEYnoThe full contents of the .p8 file, BEGIN and END lines included; escaped newlines are unescaped by env.tsSame

The two NEXT_PUBLIC_ variables are the only ones a browser may see, and they are the only two the iOS app is ever handed. SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY is used by lib/supabase/admin.ts, whose comment reads "Bypasses RLS. Only ever import from server code behind an admin auth check."

The iOS app

The Xcode project is not committed. mobile/project.yml is the XcodeGen spec, and xcodegen generate must be run after every change to it and after every new source file. Before that, mobile/scripts/make-config.sh writes mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Generated/AppConfig.swift from the repo's .env.local. The script reads exactly two variables, the Supabase URL and the anon key, and its header says why: those are publishable, and the service-role key and the Anthropic key "are never read here and must never reach the app binary." A third value, apiBaseURL, comes from the SANCHARA_API_BASE_URL shell variable if it is set and otherwise defaults to the production API, which is how a debug build is pointed at a local or preview server. 

cd mobile
./scripts/make-config.sh       # reads the Supabase URL and anon key from ../.env.local
xcodegen generate
open Sanchara.xcodeproj

project.yml declares three targets: the app, the SancharaWidgets extension and a SancharaTests unit-test bundle. It also declares every entitlement: associated domains for Universal Links, aps-environment for push, Sign in with Apple, and the App Group the widget and Siri read the chart from. Each of those needs a matching capability on the App ID in the Apple Developer portal, which is one-time work covered in the next section. The deployment target is iOS 17; the on-device model features are weak-linked behind #if canImport(FoundationModels) and gated at runtime on iOS 26, so the same build runs on both.

15.3Supabase and Apple, once

Four documents under docs/ describe configuration that lives in dashboards rather than in git. Each names the project it was written for; the steps do not depend on that. In summary:

  • Project and keys (docs/SUPABASE_SETUP.md). Create a project, copy the URL, the anon key and the service-role key into .env.local, enable the Email provider under Authentication, run the migrations, sign up in the app, then make that user an admin with one insert into public.admins using the id from the Users page. The admin area at /admin has its own login and checks this table.
  • Email codes (docs/EMAIL_OTP.md). Sign-in by email sends a six-digit code, not a link. Supabase decides which to send from the template: a template with only {{ .ConfirmationURL }} sends a link and no code, and the app's code screen then has nothing to accept. Two templates, Magic Link and Confirm signup, must include {{ .Token }}. Both keep the link as well, so the web app and the Universal Link path keep working. On the app side the flow is signInWithOTP then verifyOTP(email:token:type:), with .email for passwordless and .signup after a password sign-up.
  • Sign in with Apple (docs/APPLE_SIGN_IN.md). The app signs in natively with signInWithIdToken, so no web redirect is involved and no environment variable is needed. Two switches must be on: the Apple provider in the Supabase project, with the app's bundle identifier in its Client IDs list, and the Sign In with Apple capability on the App ID, which real devices and TestFlight need for signing. The error Issuer https://appleid.apple.com is not enabled means the first switch is off. The document also records a known gap: account deletion does not revoke the Apple token, which Apple's guideline 5.1.1(v) asks for, and lists the three steps closing it would take.
  • Push (docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md). Apply migrations 0004 and 0005, create an APNs key in the Developer portal (it downloads once), and set CRON_SECRET and the four APNS_* variables in Vercel. The document then explains the twice-daily reading and the dosha sweep, which the alerts chapter covers in full.
  • App Groups (docs/SIRI_AND_WIDGETS.md). Register the group, enable it on both the app's and the widget's App IDs, and let Xcode regenerate the profiles. Without it "the widget shows its empty state forever and Siri says there is no chart", because the two processes are reading different containers.

Finally, the auth redirect URLs. docs/DEPLOY.md has Supabase's Site URL set to the production domain and /auth/callback on that domain added to the Redirect URLs, with a wildcard pattern for preview deployments if they should authenticate too. The web app's callback route is app/auth/callback/.

Order of operations Keys first, migrations second, templates and providers third, admin last. The admin insert needs a user id, which needs a sign-up, which needs the Email provider and the templates. Everything else can be done in any order, and the push and App Group steps can be left until the iOS app needs them; the web app and the API run without them.

15.4Vercel and the cron schedules

The web app deploys from main through Vercel's Git integration. The project's environment variables are set for both Production and Preview; docs/DEPLOY.md walks through vercel link and vercel env add for the four required variables, and docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md for the five optional ones. Development is left out of the Vercel store on purpose, because local development reads .env.local. After a variable is added, the running functions do not see it until the next deployment; vercel redeploy --prod is the step people forget.

Two settings in next.config.ts matter for the deployment. serverExternalPackages: ["swisseph-wasm"] keeps the ephemeris out of the bundler so its .wasm binary is shipped intact for the server runtime. And a headers() rule serves /.well-known/apple-app-site-association as application/json, because the file has no extension and Next would otherwise serve it as an octet stream, which Apple rejects; without it Universal Links and the webcredentials association silently fail.

The schedule is the whole of vercel.json:

{
  "crons": [
    { "path": "/api/cron/daily", "schedule": "30 2 * * *" },
    { "path": "/api/cron/daily", "schedule": "30 15 * * *" }
  ]
}

02:30 and 15:30 UTC are 08:00 and 21:00 in India, which is where the first users are. The route handler at app/api/cron/daily/route.ts declares runtime = "nodejs" because the ephemeris is WebAssembly and maxDuration = 300 because it runs the model once per user per half-day. It checks Authorization: Bearer against CRON_SECRET and then runs two halves in order: runDuePredictions, the readings, then runDailyAlerts, the dosha sweep. Each user is handled on their own clock: anything before 14:00 local is the morning slot, anything after is the night slot, and the row is stored against the user's local date under a unique index. That is what makes extra runs harmless, and it is why docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md says a mixed-timezone user base can move to an hourly schedule at no extra model cost: each hourly run writes only for users whose current slot is still missing. /api/cron/alerts still exists for running the dosha sweep on its own by hand.

15.5The production checklist

docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md exists because of a specific failure. In mid-September 2026 production had written no daily readings for three weeks and had never sent a dosha alert.
