The code was deployed and the crons were firing on schedule, but CRON_SECRET was not set, so the route refused every request with a 401, and the four APNS_* variables were not set either. Two migrations the timeline feature depended on had also never been applied to the production database. None of that is visible from a green build, which is the point of the checklist. Its steps, generalised:

  1. Log the Vercel CLI into the right account and link the project. The CLI remembers one login, and vercel env against the wrong scope fails in confusing ways. vercel env ls production shows what is already set.
  2. Set the secrets. CRON_SECRET from openssl rand -hex 32, then APNS_KEY_ID, APNS_TEAM_ID, APNS_BUNDLE_ID and APNS_PRIVATE_KEY (the whole .p8, BEGIN and END lines included). Then vercel redeploy --prod.
  3. Apply any migration the deployed code expects, by hand, in order. Nothing in the pipeline applies migrations. The README says it plainly: after deploying a version that adds a migration, apply it before traffic reaches the new code. The checklist confirms with a query against information_schema.tables for the three timeline tables.
  4. Run the cron by hand. A curl with the bearer secret against /api/cron/daily returns JSON with two objects, predictions and alerts. predictions.written should be at least 1; predictions.skipped counts users whose current slot was already written, which is the normal result of a second run in the same half-day; alerts.pushConfigured says whether the APNs variables were seen. A 401 means the secret is missing or does not match. A query on daily_readings grouped by date and slot confirms today's rows.
  5. Check the push environment on each phone. The app reports sandbox or production from its signing entitlement when it registers, and the server sends to the matching APNs host. Rows in device_tokens written by builds from before that fix may carry the wrong environment; the checklist deletes them by updated_at and has each device reopen the app once.
  6. Decide the schedule. Twice a day at fixed UTC times, or hourly.
Two facts a green deployment cannot tell you Whether the environment variables the cron and the pushes need are present, and whether the database has every table the code will query. The first fails closed and silently (a 401 to a scheduled request that nobody is watching). The second fails loudly but only on the code path that reaches the missing table. Check both after every deploy that touches either.

15.6Continuous integration

.github/workflows/ci.yml runs on every push to main and every pull request, under a concurrency group keyed on the ref with cancel-in-progress, so a second push to the same branch cancels the run for the first. It has two independent jobs.

The web job

On ubuntu-latest with Node 24 and the npm cache: npm ci, then npx tsc --noEmit, then npm test (which is vitest run), then npm run build. The build step carries the comment that explains why the job needs no secrets: 

# The build needs no secrets: env vars are read at request time, not at
# build time, so a missing key fails the route rather than the build.
- name: Build
  run: npm run build

This is the consequence of env.ts exposing functions. A next build evaluates every module once to discover routes and pre-render what it can; if env.ts read process.env at the top level, the build would throw on a runner with no variables, and CI would need the production keys just to prove the code compiles. As written, the build succeeds on a bare runner and the first request to a route that needs a missing variable is the thing that fails. The tests, likewise, never need a key: every module that calls the model or the database is stubbed at the boundary, and a single alias in vitest.config.ts points server-only at the package's empty entry so route handlers and lib/env.ts can be imported in a plain Node process.

The iOS job

On macos-26, working in mobile/: select the newest Xcode 26 on the runner, brew install xcodegen, then write a placeholder .env.local and run make-config.sh. The placeholder is the trick worth knowing. The generated AppConfig.swift is never committed, and a build without it does not compile, so the job writes a .env.local containing a stand-in URL and a stand-in key, both obviously fake. The comment in the workflow says exactly what is being proved: "The simulator build only needs the values to be present, not real." No test in the iOS suite reaches the network, so nothing ever tries to use them.

Then xcodegen generate, a cache of mobile/SourcePackages keyed on the hash of project.yml (so the Supabase Swift package is fetched once per dependency change, not per run), and one xcodebuild test against the first available iPhone simulator with CODE_SIGNING_ALLOWED=NO. The output is filtered to errors, warnings in the app's own sources, and test suite lines; the full log is uploaded as an artifact only on failure. The job proves that the spec generates a project, that the app and the widget extension compile, and that the XCTest suite in mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ passes. It cannot prove signing, push, or anything that needs an entitlement, which is what the TestFlight workflow is for.

15.7The release and TestFlight workflows

release.yml

Runs on a pushed tag matching v* with contents: write. An awk program extracts the section of CHANGELOG.md whose heading is ## [X.Y.Z], stopping at the next version heading or at the link references at the foot of the file. If no section exists the workflow warns and falls back to the commit subjects since the previous tag. Then gh release create publishes the release with the tag as its title and the extracted notes as its body. If the release already exists the job exits cleanly, so re-pushing a tag is safe.

ios-testflight.yml

Runs on the same tags, or by hand from the Actions tab, under a concurrency group named testflight that does not cancel in-progress runs, because a half-finished upload is worse than a queued one. The job:

  1. Checks that all five repository secrets are set and fails with a named error for each one missing, before any Xcode work starts.
  2. Selects Xcode 26, installs XcodeGen, writes .env.local from the SUPABASE_URL and SUPABASE_ANON_KEY secrets, runs make-config.sh, and generates the project. These are the same two publishable values a developer's .env.local holds; the API base URL stays at the production default.
  3. Sets the version. On a tag, CFBundleShortVersionString in both the app's and the widget's Info.plist becomes the tag without its v; on a manual run the value in project.yml is kept. CFBundleVersion becomes the workflow run number in both, because App Store Connect requires a unique build number per version.
  4. Sets aps-environment to production in Sanchara/Sanchara.entitlements with PlistBuddy. The committed spec says development, which is right for a debug build on a phone, and wrong for TestFlight: "Push tokens minted under the development environment are rejected by production APNs, so store builds must carry the production entitlement."
  5. Writes the App Store Connect API key from ASC_KEY_P8 to ~/private_keys/AuthKey_<id>.p8 with mode 600.
  6. Archives with -allowProvisioningUpdates and the three authentication key flags. With an API key, Xcode creates or refreshes the distribution certificate and profile itself, which is why no certificate is stored in GitHub.
  7. Exports with mobile/ExportOptions.plist, whose method is app-store-connect and whose destination is upload, so the build goes straight to App Store Connect instead of producing an .ipa. Symbols are uploaded; version management is left to the workflow, not to Apple.
  8. Uploads the archive and export logs as an artifact whether or not the job succeeded.
Repository secretValueUsed by
SUPABASE_URLSame as NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URLBaked into AppConfig.swift
SUPABASE_ANON_KEYSame as NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEYBaked into AppConfig.swift
ASC_KEY_IDApp Store Connect API key idArchive and export authentication
ASC_ISSUER_IDApp Store Connect issuer idArchive and export authentication
ASC_KEY_P8Full contents of the downloaded .p8Written to the runner's private_keys for the archive

The README adds that the API key needs the App Manager role and that the app record must already exist in App Store Connect. The team id the export uses is in ExportOptions.plist and project.yml; it is an identifier, not a secret, and it is the same value APNS_TEAM_ID carries on the server.

15.8The release procedure

Releases are tagged from main. The README's "Releasing" section is four steps:

  1. Make sure CI is green.
  2. Add a section to CHANGELOG.md and bump version in package.json.
  3. Commit, then git tag -a vX.Y.Z -m "vX.Y.Z".
  4. git push origin main --tags.

The push of main deploys the web app through Vercel. The tag triggers both release workflows. Nothing else is needed, and nothing in the workflows reads package.json: the web app's version is informational, the iOS version comes from the tag, and the release notes come from the changelog. Keeping the three in step is a discipline, not an enforcement.

CHANGELOG.md follows Keep a Changelog 1.1.0 and the project uses Semantic Versioning; the file says so in its first paragraph. Each version has a heading of the form ## [0.3.0] - 2026-09-18, subsections Added, Changed and Fixed as needed, and a link reference at the foot of the file pointing at the GitHub release for that tag. The awk in release.yml depends on exactly this shape: the heading must start ## [ followed by the bare version, and the link references must be the last thing in the file. Three versions exist: 0.1.0 and 0.2.0 on 16 September 2026, and 0.3.0 on 18 September. The entries are written for the person using the app, in bold lead-ins followed by a sentence or two of plain language; the commit history is where the engineering detail lives.

Versions on the iOS side project.yml pins CFBundleShortVersionString at 1.0 and CFBundleVersion at 1 for local builds. Those are placeholders. A tagged workflow run overwrites both, and the run number guarantees each upload is distinct. A build archived by hand in Xcode would carry 1.0 (1) and would collide with any earlier hand-archived build; upload through the workflow instead.

15.9Secrets discipline

The rule in docs/DEPLOY.md is short: locally, secrets live only in .env.local; in the cloud, only in Vercel's encrypted environment store; and no real key or service-role secret is ever committed. The repository enforces the first two with .gitignore and with where values are read. 

.gitignore, the entries that matter here
.env*                                  every env file ...
!.env.example                          ... except the documented template
.vercel                                the CLI's link to a project
mobile/Sanchara.xcodeproj/             generated by XcodeGen
mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Generated/     AppConfig.swift, written from .env.local
mobile/SourcePackages/                 resolved Swift packages
mobile/build/                          archives and exports
supabase/.temp/                        the CLI's project reference and versions
xcuserdata/, *.xcuserstate             per-user Xcode state
Secret or credentialLocallyIn productionReaches a client?
Supabase URL and anon key.env.local, and AppConfig.swift generated from itVercel env store; GitHub secrets for the TestFlight buildYes, by design. The browser bundle and the iOS binary both carry them; RLS is the boundary
Supabase service-role key.env.localVercel env storeNever. Read only in lib/supabase/admin.ts, which imports server-only; make-config.sh does not read it
Anthropic API key.env.localVercel env storeNever. Read only in lib/anthropic.ts, server-only
CRON_SECRETOptional in .env.localVercel env store; Vercel sends it back on scheduled callsNever
APNs key id, team id, bundle id, .p8Optional in .env.localVercel env storeNever. Read only in lib/push/apns.ts, server-only. The .p8 is also kept offline because Apple lets it download once
App Store Connect API keyNot neededGitHub secrets; written to the runner for one archiveNever
Signing certificatesXcode automatic signingNone stored; Xcode mints them from the API keyNever
A user's sessionCookies (web), Keychain via supabase-swift (iOS)SameIts own device only; every /api/* call carries it as a cookie or a bearer token

Three code-level habits back the table. First, server-only at the top of lib/env.ts, lib/supabase/admin.ts, lib/supabase/route.ts, lib/anthropic.ts, lib/push/apns.ts and every alerts module turns an accidental client import into a build error. Second, the user id in every mutating route comes from the verified session, never from the request body, so a leaked anon key still cannot act as someone else. Third, make-config.sh only knows the names of the two publishable variables; there is no code path by which a server key could be generated into the app.

What a leaked repository exposes The bundle identifier, the team id, the production hostname, the App Group name and the URL scheme. All are already visible in the shipped app. No key, no token, no project reference.

15.10Study notes

Read in this order

  1. .env.example, for every variable and the comment that explains it.
  2. lib/env.ts, for required versus optional and why each is a function.
  3. vercel.json and app/api/cron/daily/route.ts, for the schedule and the guard.
  4. .github/workflows/ci.yml, both jobs; note the two comments about secrets.
  5. .github/workflows/ios-testflight.yml, top to bottom; it is the whole iOS release.
  6. .github/workflows/release.yml, for the changelog contract.
  7. mobile/scripts/make-config.sh and mobile/project.yml, for what the app is handed and which entitlements it claims.
  8. docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md, for the failure that motivated it.

Check yourself

Why does npm run build succeed on a CI runner with no environment variables?

Because lib/env.ts exposes functions that read process.env when called, inside a request. Nothing reads a variable at module load, so the build evaluates every module without touching one.

What does the iOS CI job write to .env.local, and why is that safe?

A placeholder URL and a placeholder key. make-config.sh refuses to run without values, the compiler needs the generated file, and no test reaches the network, so the values only need to exist.

Which two things does the TestFlight workflow change in the checked-out tree before archiving?

The version and build number in both Info.plist files, and aps-environment in the app's entitlements, from development to production.

A cron fires on schedule and nothing is written. What are the first two things to check?

That CRON_SECRET is set in the Production environment and a redeploy happened after it was added, and that every migration the code expects has been applied to the production database.

Why can the anon key ship in the iOS binary when the service-role key cannot?

Every table has Row Level Security, so the anon key can only read and write rows the signed-in user owns. The service-role key bypasses RLS entirely; it is read only in server-only modules.

What in CHANGELOG.md does release.yml depend on?

A heading of exactly ## [X.Y.Z] for the tagged version, and the link references being the last block in the file; the awk stops at either the next version heading or the first reference line.

Chapter sixteen

16What breaks, and how it is handled

A catalogue of the ways the system fails, and for each one the code that meets it. The organising rule is that a failure should cost the user the least thing it can: a push rather than a reading, a reading rather than a chart, a chart upgrade rather than a request, and never a wrong number presented as a right one.

This chapter describes the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. Every item below was read from the file it names, and every test named exists in the repository under that name. Where a failure mode has no test, the item says "untested" rather than implying one. Items are grouped by the layer they belong to: configuration, authentication, the model, place and time, stored data, push, the device, untrusted text, and deletion. A table at the end puts the whole catalogue on one page.

16.1Where the guards sit

Three kinds of request enter the system and each passes a fixed sequence of guards. A user request, from the web app or the iOS app, reaches a route handler, which builds a Supabase client for the caller (reading two environment variables as it does so), asks that client who the user is, validates the body, and only then touches the database and the external services. A scheduled request from Vercel Cron passes one guard, the cron secret, and then runs under the service-role client with its own guards against repetition. A request on the phone from a widget, a Siri intent or the chat's local router never leaves the device unless the on-device path cannot answer, in which case it becomes the first kind.

16.2Configuration

A required environment variable is missing

Symptom. The build succeeds and the deployment is green; the first request to a route that needs the variable returns a 500, and the function log carries Missing required env var: NAME.

Handling. lib/env.ts exposes one function per variable. required() reads process.env when called and throws with the name; optional() returns undefined for an absent or empty value. Because the read happens inside a request, a missing key fails that request rather than the build, which is the property .github/workflows/ci.yml relies on when it runs next build with no secrets at all (section 15.6). The variable is first touched where a client is built: createRouteSupabase reads the URL and anon key, createAdminSupabase the service-role key, anthropic() the model key. In a route handler that throw happens before the handler's own try, so Next.js turns it into a 500. The file also imports server-only, which makes any import from client code a build error rather than a leak; the same alias in vitest.config.ts is what lets tests import it.

Test. Untested. Nothing exercises required() against an empty environment.

The cron route is called without CRON_SECRET

Symptom. The schedule fires, nothing is written, and the invocation log shows a 401. docs/GO_LIVE_CHECKLIST.md records production in exactly this state for three weeks.

Handling. Both app/api/cron/daily/route.ts and app/api/cron/alerts/route.ts begin the same way: 

const secret = env.cronSecret();
if (!secret || request.headers.get("authorization") !== `Bearer ${secret}`) {
  return new Response("Unauthorized", { status: 401 });
}

An unset secret and a wrong secret are the same case. The alerts route's comment gives the reason: with no secret the route "refuses everything rather than exposing a job that spends model tokens to anyone who finds the path." Failing closed is deliberate; the cost is that a forgotten variable is silent until someone looks at the logs or the tables, which is what the checklist's curl step exists to do.

Test. Untested. There is no route.test.ts under either cron directory.

APNs credentials are absent

Symptom. Locally, or on a deployment before the four APNS_* variables were set: readings and alerts appear in the app's inbox but no notification arrives. The cron's JSON reports alerts.pushConfigured: false and pushesSent: 0.

Handling. isPushConfigured() in lib/push/apns.ts requires all four values. Both jobs write their rows first and call pushToDevices last, and that function returns 0 before it reads any device row when push is not configured. Should ApnsClient.send be reached anyway, it returns { ok: false, status: 0, reason: "APNsNotConfigured" } without opening a connection. .env.example says the same in one line: "Leave blank locally: alerts are still written, pushes are skipped." The client also survives Apple being unreachable: the HTTP/2 session installs an error handler so a socket failure cannot become an unhandled event, each request has a ten-second timeout, and the provider token is minted once and reused for 45 minutes because Apple rate-limits regeneration.

Test. Untested. lib/push/apns.ts has no test file, and the pushConfigured flag in the summaries is not asserted anywhere.

16.3Authentication

A bad, expired or missing bearer token

Symptom. The iOS app gets a 401 with the body Unauthorized from any authenticated route. The web app, which sends cookies, is unaffected.

Handling. Two functions in lib/supabase/route.ts. bearerTokenFrom returns null when the header is absent, when the scheme is not bearer (case-insensitively), or when the scheme is present but the token is empty. createRouteSupabase then chooses: no token means the cookie-backed client from lib/supabase/server.ts, so a web caller never notices this code exists; a token means a fresh supabase-js client that forwards it as an Authorization header with persistSession and autoRefreshToken off. The route does not inspect the token itself. It calls supabase.auth.getUser(), and a forged, expired or revoked token yields no user, at which point every mutating route returns 401 before reading the body. For a valid token, Row Level Security evaluates as that user for the rest of the request, which is the reason the anon key can ship in the binary. On the web, pages under /app and /admin (except /admin/login) are redirected to the login page by updateSession in lib/supabase/middleware.ts when there is no user; the API routes still check for themselves.

Tests. lib/supabase/route.test.ts: "extracts the token from an Authorization header", "accepts a lowercase scheme", "returns null when the header is absent", "returns null for a non-bearer scheme", "returns null when the scheme is present but the token is empty". The 401 on a missing user is pinned per route: app/api/devices/route.test.ts "rejects an unauthenticated caller", app/api/account/route.test.ts "returns 401 and deletes nothing when unauthenticated", app/api/profile/route.test.ts "returns 401 when there is no user". An expired token reaching getUser is not itself tested; the tests stub the user as absent.

16.4The model

The model API fails during a chat reading

Symptom. One of two things, depending on when. Before the stream starts: a 500 whose body reads "Something went wrong preparing your reading. Please try again." After it starts: the reply stops and a final line appears in the transcript, [The stars are momentarily clouded. Please try again.].

Handling. app/api/chat/route.ts does all its preparation, the profile read, the chart upgrade, the transit chart, the timeline context, the conversation and the history, inside one try that ends in the 500 above. Two of those steps have their own softer handling first: a failed transitChart sets transits to undefined and the reading proceeds without today's sky, and a failed loadTimeline is logged and skipped. lib/astrology/transits.ts also deletes a rejected promise from its per-day cache, with the comment "never cache a failure", so one bad ephemeris call does not poison the day for every user in that degree cell.

Once the ReadableStream has been returned the status is already 200 and the x-conversation-id header has been sent, so a mid-stream error cannot change the response code. The stream's start wraps the model call in a try/catch/finally: the catch enqueues the apology line, and the finally saves whatever assistant text had arrived, if any, and closes the stream. A partial reading is therefore kept in the conversation rather than lost, and the user's own message was already saved before the stream began. On iOS, ChatStore.send in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/ChatStore.swift catches a thrown stream error and puts its description into the still-empty assistant bubble, or into errorMessage if text had already arrived; a CancellationError from a superseding message leaves the transcript as it was.

Tests. Untested on the chat route; there is no test file under app/api/chat/. The bounded-history rule the route depends on is tested in lib/data/history.test.ts: "keeps everything when it fits the budget", "drops the oldest turns first", "never starts on an assistant turn", "keeps the newest turn even when it alone exceeds the budget", "returns nothing for an empty conversation".

The model fails while composing a reading or an alert

Symptom. A push arrives, or a row appears in the inbox, with plain wording: "Your reading for today" and a sentence inviting the user to open the app, or for a dosha change, "Sade Sati is active" with the condition's own detail. Nothing is skipped.

Handling. Both composers treat the model as optional. In lib/alerts/compose.ts, composeAlert catches any error, and also treats output that parseAlertCopy cannot read as a failure, then returns fallbackCopy(started, ended), which names the conditions from the facts the engine already decided. In lib/alerts/predictions.ts, composePrediction does the same and returns fallbackPrediction(slot, firstName). The parser accepts a delimited TITLE/BODY/DETAIL shape and, failing that, the older JSON shape, because markdown inside a JSON string was found to break two replies in seven. The comment above fallbackPrediction states the rule: "Never leave the slot empty: a plain prompt to open the app still beats silence."

Tests. lib/alerts/compose.test.ts: "still names the condition when the model call fails", "reads as relief when a condition has ended", "returns null when neither shape is present", "rejects a delimited reply missing its detail", "still accepts a JSON reply", "rejects output missing a field". lib/alerts/predictions.test.ts: "still says something useful when the model call fails".

16.5Place and time

A birthplace that cannot be geocoded

Symptom. The user types a town and the suggestion list stays empty, or the geocoder is down.

Handling. geocodePlace in lib/geo.ts returns an empty array for a query under two characters, for a non-OK response from the geocoder, and for a response with no results; it never throws for those. GET /api/geocode is public and returns that array. The web form in components/IntakeForm.tsx offers a second path: "Can't find your birthplace? Enter coordinates manually", which takes a latitude and longitude and derives the timezone in the browser with resolveTimezone, the same tz-lookup call the server uses, so a chart can still be computed for a village no geocoder knows. The iOS form in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Intake/IntakeView.swift debounces the search by 300 milliseconds, turns a thrown request into an empty list with try?, and keeps Save disabled until a place has been picked; it has no manual coordinate entry. On the server, POST /api/profile rejects a missing field or a non-finite coordinate with a 400 before any chart is computed, and a chart computation that throws returns a 500 reading "Could not compute your chart. Please try again." The web app's saveIntake server action in app/(app)/app/intake/actions.ts has no equivalent catch: a compute failure there surfaces as a Next.js error page.

Tests. app/api/profile/route.test.ts: "returns 400 when a required field is missing", "returns 400 when lat is not a number", "saves the profile and returns 200 for a valid payload". The empty-result paths of geocodePlace are untested; lib/geo.test.ts covers only resolveTimezone.

Timezone edge cases

Symptom. A reading lands at the wrong hour, or a chart is computed for the wrong instant.

Handling, and where it stops. The timezone is never typed by anyone. It is derived from coordinates by tz-lookup, stored with the profile, and reused: the iOS form's prefill keeps the stored coordinates and zone on edit because "the birthplace has not moved." computeChart in lib/astrology/chart.ts parses birthDate and birthTime with Luxon in that zone and converts to UT before the ephemeris sees them. The scheduled jobs read profile.timezone || "UTC", decide the slot from the user's local hour with slotForHour (before 14:00 is morning), and store for_date as the user's local date, so "my reading for the 26th" means their 26th. The transit chart is sampled at midday UTC once per day per whole-degree cell of latitude and longitude, which keeps the prompt text stable for the cache; the comment notes only the Moon moves enough within a day to matter. Two edges are not guarded. tzlookup throws RangeError("invalid coordinates") outside the valid range, and the web form's manual mode calls it with any finite numbers, so a latitude of 95 throws in the browser. And Luxon given an unknown zone name returns an invalid DateTime, whose fields are NaN, and nothing checks validity before julday; in practice the zone always comes from tz-lookup, so the path is unreachable through the forms. Users far from the fixed UTC schedule can have both cron runs land in the same half of their day and get one reading instead of two, which docs/PUSH_ALERTS.md documents together with the hourly schedule that would fix it.

Tests. lib/geo.test.ts: "maps New Delhi coordinates to Asia/Kolkata", "maps New York coordinates to America/New_York". lib/alerts/slots.test.ts: "splits the user's local day in two", "returns the current slot when it has not been written", "returns nothing when the current slot already exists", "does not backfill a slot the user has moved past". The invalid-coordinate and invalid-zone paths are untested.

16.6Stored data

A chart stored before whole-sign houses

Symptom. None visible, which is the design. Before 0.3.0 the stored Vedic chart carried Placidus house numbers under whole-sign labels, so the kundli and the PDF could draw a planet in a house whose sign it was not in.

Handling. Every chart now carries schemaVersion, and CHART_SCHEMA_VERSION in lib/astrology/constants.ts is 2. isChartStale in lib/astrology/derived.ts is true when the stored version (1 if absent) is lower, or when the chart has no derived facts. ensureCurrentChart in lib/data/birthProfile.ts recomputes both traditions together with Promise.all, writes them back in one update, and returns the upgraded row; the comment explains that both are recomputed "so a profile is never half upgraded", and that doing it on read "means no migration and no backfill job." It runs from getBirthProfile, which the three server-component pages use, and from the chat route. Any failure, whether the compute throws or the write comes back with an error, is logged with its stage and the original profile is returned, because an upgrade that cannot be written is not a reason to fail the request that triggered it. Readers that only need facts do not wait for the upgrade: factsFor derives them in process from the stored positions, and deriveFacts recomputes houses whole-sign regardless of the stored numbers. The alerts job has a companion guard: partitionForAlerts in lib/alerts/run.ts never notifies a natal condition once the user has natal rows on record, because a natal condition "cannot start or end, so a change in one is always an artifact of the computation changing."

Tests. lib/data/birthProfile.test.ts: "returns the profile unchanged when there is no stored vedic chart", "returns the profile unchanged when the stored chart is already current", "recomputes and writes both traditions together for a stale chart", "swallows a thrown compute/write exception and returns the original profile", "swallows a resolved write error (RLS/constraint) and returns the original profile unchanged", "returns an upgraded profile when the stored chart is stale". lib/astrology/derived.test.ts: "recomputes houses whole-sign, ignoring the stored numbers". lib/alerts/run.test.ts: "alerts natal conditions on a first read, when no natal rows exist", "never alerts a natal condition once the user has natal rows", "keeps transit conditions alertable in both cases".

A failed profile read that looked like a missing profile

Symptom. Fixed in 0.3.0. A user with a chart opened the app during a database hiccup and was sent to the intake form as though they had never filled it in.

Handling. supabase-js resolves { data: null, error } on a failed read rather than throwing, and the old getBirthProfile ignored error and returned data ?? null. It now throws with the error's message, matching saveBirthProfile in the same file, while a genuine no-row result, which maybeSingle() reports as { data: null, error: null }, still returns null. The three pages that read through it (app/(app)/app/page.tsx, chat/page.tsx and profile/page.tsx) now surface an error instead of the intake form. The chat route does its own read and checks profileError explicitly, returning a 500 that says "Something went wrong loading your profile."

Tests. lib/data/birthProfile.test.ts: "throws on a genuine read failure instead of reporting it as no profile", "returns null when there is no stored profile".

Duplicate cron runs

Symptom. None. The job can be fired twice in a minute, or moved to an hourly schedule, and a user still gets one morning reading, one night reading and one alert per change.

Handling. Three mechanisms. First, daily_readings has unique (user_id, for_date, slot) from supabase/migrations/0005_daily_readings.sql, and writeDueReading reads the slots already written for today before deciding anything through isSlotDue. If two runs race past that read, the insert fails with Postgres code 23505, which the code treats as "already done" and counts as skipped. Second, transit_conditions has a partial unique index, (user_id, signature) where ended_on is null, from 0004_alerts_push.sql, and runAlertsForUser diffs today's condition signatures against the open rows, so an unchanged sky inserts nothing, closes nothing and sends nothing. Third, deriveHadNatalRows fails closed: if the count of natal rows errors, the user is treated as already having them, so a transient database error cannot turn a standing natal dosha into a "has begun" push. Both jobs also bound their work to 500 profiles per run and count a failing user in failures rather than aborting the run.

Tests. lib/alerts/slots.test.ts, the four names above. lib/alerts/run.test.ts: "is false when the count query succeeds with zero natal rows", "is true when the count query succeeds with at least one natal row", and the test whose name begins "fails CLOSED", which passes an errored count and expects true. The 23505 branch is untested.

Account deletion ordering

Symptom. The user taps Delete on the profile screen and confirms.

Handling. DELETE /api/account takes the user id from the verified session, never from the body, so a caller can only delete themselves, then calls admin.auth.admin.deleteUser(user.id) with the service-role client. Every table references auth.users(id) on delete cascade: admins, profiles, birth_profiles, conversations (and through it messages), device_tokens, transit_conditions, alerts, daily_readings, life_events, life_event_scans and period_readings. One call removes everything the database holds, and because device_tokens goes with it the next cron cannot push to the phone. If the call fails the route returns a 500 with a message and the account is untouched. On the phone, ProfileView.deleteAccount calls the API first and only on success runs AuthStore.signOut, which unregisters the device (a no-op now, but the same path sign-out always takes), signs out of Supabase, and clears the App Group cache so the widget stops showing the deleted chart. Two things are not removed: the avatar object in the avatars storage bucket, which the route never touches, and the Sign in with Apple grant, which docs/APPLE_SIGN_IN.md lists as a known gap against guideline 5.1.1(v).

Tests. app/api/account/route.test.ts: "returns 401 and deletes nothing when unauthenticated", "deletes only the calling user", "returns 500 when deletion fails". The cascade itself is a database constraint and is not tested from code.

16.7Push

A token minted under the wrong APNs environment

Symptom. The cron reports pushesSent: 0 for a device that is registered, and the function log shows apns send failed 400 BadDeviceToken. Before 0.2.0 a Release build run from Xcode produced exactly this: it reported production from its build configuration while carrying a development entitlement.

Handling. The environment is read from the truth APNs itself uses. ApsEnvironment.current in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Push.swift opens embedded.mobileprovision, finds the plist inside the signed envelope by searching for <plist and </plist>, and returns production only when aps-environment is exactly that string; anything else, including no profile at all (the simulator), is sandbox. The app sends that word with every registration. POST /api/devices checks the token is 64 to 200 hex characters, maps anything other than sandbox to production, and upserts on the token with the admin client so a phone handed to a new account changes owner. ApnsClient.send picks the host from the row's environment, and when Apple answers 410, Unregistered or BadDeviceToken, the row is deleted: "Apple only tells you a token is dead when you use it." A token registered under the wrong host is therefore dropped on first use and written back correctly at the next launch, since PushStore.register runs whenever iOS hands the app a token. The TestFlight workflow removes the remaining way to get this wrong by setting the entitlement to production before archiving.

Tests. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/ApsEnvironmentTests.swift: testDevelopmentProfileIsSandbox, testProductionProfileIsProduction, testProfileWithoutThePushEntitlementIsSandbox, testNoPlistAtAllIsSandbox, testAnUnknownValueIsNotTrustedAsProduction, testSimulatorBuildReportsSandbox. app/api/devices/route.test.ts: "rejects a malformed token", "stores the token against the session user, not the body", "defaults an unknown environment to production", "deletes only this user's row for that token". The dead-token deletion in the jobs is untested.

16.8The device

A widget with no cached chart

Symptom. A freshly installed app, a signed-out phone, or an App Group that was never enabled. The widget must draw something.

Handling. Neither widget fetches. ReadingProvider in mobile/SancharaWidgets/ReadingWidget.swift reads ChartCache.shared.loadReading(), and when that is nil the entry's reading is nil and the view shows its empty state, "Open Astrya to get your first reading." PeriodProvider in PeriodWidget.swift tries three sources in order: the cached period with its theme, then the cached chart's dasha without a theme ("the dates are true"), then the empty state "Open Astrya to map your periods." Both empty states name the next action rather than apologising. ChartCache itself, in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/ChartCache.swift, falls back to the app's own caches directory when the App Group container is unavailable, so the app keeps working and only the sharing stops; docs/SIRI_AND_WIDGETS.md names that as the symptom of a missing App Group capability. Every read is a try?, so a corrupt file reads as absent. The reading cache carries an isFresh flag so a widget "should not keep insisting on yesterday's morning reading"; today the Siri snippet in IntentData.swift checks it and the widget provider does not. Sign-out calls clear(), which removes all four files, so the widget of a shared phone cannot show the previous account.

Tests. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/PeriodProgressTests.swift: testCachedPeriodSurvivesTheRoundTrip, testClearRemovesTheCachedPeriod, testFractionClampsOutsideThePeriod, testZeroLengthAndMalformedDatesGiveZero. The empty views themselves are untested.

The on-device model is unavailable

Symptom. An iOS 17 phone, an iOS 26 phone without Apple Intelligence, or a model that refuses. The question must still be answered.

Handling. Three layers, each of which falls through to the server. OnDeviceReasoner.route in mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Features/Chat/OnDeviceReasoner.swift is wrapped in #if canImport(FoundationModels) so the app builds for iOS 17, then gated on #available(iOS 26.0, *) and on isAvailable, which is SystemLanguageModel.default.availability == .available; any of those false means .escalate. When the model runs, a refusal or a thrown error in isLookup returns false and in answer returns nil, with the comment that this "is not an error worth surfacing: the question simply goes to Claude, which is where it went before." An answer that never called the lookup tool is discarded as a guess. ChatStore.send only tries the reasoner for a Vedic question that is not the opening reading and not an explicit request for the server; on .escalate it streams from /api/chat exactly as before. Because Apple's model refuses astrology, everything it is shown is framed as a reference table by ChartFacts, and a test guards that framing because a slip would make the feature stop answering silently.

Tests. mobile/Sanchara/Tests/OnDeviceRoutingTests.swift: testQuestionsAboutLifeNeverReachTheModel, testQuestionsNamingPartOfTheChartGetOffered, testInterpretiveQuestionsStillDoNotReachTheTable, testAMissingBodyReturnsNothingRatherThanAGuess, testNothingHandedToTheModelNamesAstrology. The availability gate needs a device and is untested.

16.9Untrusted text

Prompt injection through chat history

Symptom. A user, or text pasted by a user, tells the model to ignore its instructions or to invent a placement.

Handling, honestly stated. There is no explicit prompt-injection defence in the repository, and the file the name suggests, lib/data/injection.test.ts, is about dependency injection: its describe is "data helpers accept an injected client", and its three tests are "saveBirthProfile writes through the injected client", "appendMessage writes through the injected client" and "falls back to the cookie client when none is injected". What does exist limits the blast radius. User messages are sent to the model in the user role and are never spliced into the system prompt; the system prompt in lib/astrology/prompt.ts instructs the model to "Never state a placement, lordship, aspect, dasha, condition or number that is not listed above" and to "Interpret only this data"; and the model cannot act, since it has no tools and its output is text that is stored and shown. History is bounded by selectHistory to 6,000 characters, newest first, never starting on an assistant turn. The one place chat history is mined for facts, the timeline scan in app/api/timeline/scan/route.ts, reads only rows with role = 'user' ("Assistant turns are full of astrological narrative that reads like biography and would extract as fiction"), tells the model to "Never infer one from a question they asked, from a hypothetical, or from something you read in an astrological reading", quotes the user's own words as evidence, and writes nothing until the user confirms each candidate; POST /api/life-events then rejects a date before birth or in the future.

Tests. Prompt injection as such: untested. The surrounding limits are: lib/data/history.test.ts, the five names in 16.4; lib/timeline/extract.test.ts: "drops events outside the person's life", "drops impossible dates", "reads a fourth field as evidence, so a pipe in a title becomes the quote", "tells the model how to resolve relative dates and ages, and to keep undated events".

The untested column Eight rows have no test at all. Three are configuration paths that need a missing variable or a live socket to exercise (the env loader, the cron secret, APNs). Three are the chat route's own failure paths, before the stream, during it, and in the transit lookup. The last two are the unguarded coordinate and zone edges, and prompt injection. The chat route in particular is the one file where a regression would be felt by every user and caught by no test.

16.11Study notes

Read in this order

  1. lib/env.ts, for the one decision that makes the build secret-free and the first request the place a missing key fails.
  2. lib/supabase/route.ts and its test, for the bearer and cookie split.
  3. app/api/chat/route.ts, the stream's catch and finally, for what a partial reading becomes.
  4. lib/alerts/compose.ts and predictions.ts, the two fallbacks and the parser that feeds them.
  5. lib/data/birthProfile.ts, for the on-read upgrade and the read that now throws.
  6. lib/alerts/run.ts, for the signature diff, the natal partition and the fail-closed count.
  7. lib/push/apns.ts and mobile/Sanchara/Sources/Services/Push.swift, both ends of the environment question.
  8. mobile/SancharaWidgets/PeriodWidget.swift and OnDeviceReasoner.swift, for the device's two fallbacks.

Check yourself

Why does a missing ANTHROPIC_API_KEY fail a request and not the build?

Because env.anthropicApiKey is a function called inside anthropic() during a request; nothing reads process.env at module load.

What does the chat route do with text the model had already streamed when the call fails?

Saves it as the assistant turn in finally, after enqueuing the apology line, then closes the stream. The status was already 200.

Why does the alerts job treat an errored natal-row count as "has natal rows"?

Because the naive reading would be "first-time reader" and every standing natal dosha would be pushed as news; suppressing natal alerts costs nothing real, since natal conditions cannot change.

A phone registers a sandbox token and the server is configured for production. What happens on the next cron?

The row carries sandbox, so the server sends to the sandbox host; environments are per row, not per server. Only a token registered under the wrong word fails, and Apple's BadDeviceToken gets that row deleted until the next launch re-registers it.

What does the period widget show before the timeline has ever been fetched?

The mahadasha and antardasha from the cached chart, with a progress bar and no theme; only with no chart at all does it show the empty state.

Which test file does not test what its name suggests?

lib/data/injection.test.ts, which tests that the data helpers accept an injected Supabase client. There is no prompt-injection test.

Appendices

Reference

Why the product exists, every decision that shaped it, and a glossary with a reading order for studying the code.

  • A. Why Astrya exists
  • B. Decisions
  • C. Glossary and reading order

Appendix A

AWhy Astrya exists

Most astrology apps either guess the sky or hand a language model a sun sign and let it write. Astrya computes the chart with the Swiss Ephemeris and lets Claude interpret only what was computed. Everything else in this book follows from that one split.

This appendix is the product story behind the code on main on 30 September 2026, version 0.3.0. It is written the way a launch document would be: the problem, the bet, who the app is for, what each release delivered, and a press release with the questions a sceptical reader would ask. Where it makes a claim about behaviour, the claim is grounded in a file you can open. The design notes under docs/design/ record intent; where they and the code disagree, this appendix follows the code and says so.

A.1The problem

An astrology reading has two parts: the chart, and what the chart means. The first part is astronomy. Where the Sun, Moon and planets stood at a particular minute over a particular town is a fact, and it can be computed to a fraction of a degree. The second part is interpretation, which is a craft with a vocabulary of its own: lords, aspects, dignity, periods, transits. A good reading rests the second part on the first.

Consumer apps mostly fail at one end or the other. Some never compute a chart at all: they know a birth date, look up a sun sign, and serve a paragraph that is true of a twelfth of the population. Others compute a chart and then hand a language model a loose description of it and hope. The model, given sign-level facts, writes sign-level prose. The specific-readings design note from 17 September 2026 puts the failure precisely: "A model given sign-level facts writes sign-level prose; the instructions have said 'never a generic horoscope' all along and cannot fix an input problem."

There is a third failure the first design note names directly: a model asked to state a planetary position will state one, whether or not it is true. A language model has no ephemeris. Left to itself it will put Saturn wherever the sentence wants Saturn to be.

A.2The bet

The first design note, dated 23 July 2026, states the bet in one line: "Claude never guesses planetary positions. It interprets computed values." The code keeps that promise with a wall between two kinds of work. On one side, deterministic computation from the birth moment. On the other, a model that is handed the results and told, in the system prompt itself, "These are the only facts you have."

The computed side is computeChart in lib/astrology/chart.ts. It turns a local birth time into universal time with luxon, asks the ephemeris for a Julian day, sets Lahiri as the ayanamsa for Vedic and leaves Western tropical, computes Placidus cusps and the ascendant with houses_ex, then places eight bodies with calc_ut and derives Ketu as Rahu plus 180 degrees. For Vedic it adds nakshatras, the current Vimshottari period, and, since 0.3.0, a derived block: what each planet rules, what it aspects, its dignity, whether it is combust, and where the running dasha lord sits. Both traditions are computed once at intake and stored in birth_profiles.chart.

The interpreted side is buildChartSystem. It renders the chart as text, then sets the rules. The two that carry the product are these, quoted from the prompt: "Never state a placement, lordship, aspect, dasha, condition or number that is not listed above," and "No sign-personality writing. A sentence that would be true of a twelfth of the population is not an answer." The same wall holds outside chat. The daily readings and dosha alerts in lib/alerts/ decide in code whether a dosha exists, and the model's only job, as the comment in compose.ts says, is "to say what the change means and what to do about it." On the phone, the on-device model may only restate rows from a table it looked up.

Who it is for

The app is for someone who wants a reading of their own chart rather than their sign, and who is willing to type a birth time and a birthplace to get it. Two traditions are served, chosen per conversation: Vedic, sidereal with Lahiri, printed as a kundli because, as the PDF renderer's comment puts it, "in most families it is a piece of paper that gets kept"; and Western, tropical. Numerology is a third mode with its own prompt. The choices in the code say who the author had in mind: the cron schedule is annotated in IST, the kundli PDF prints Sanskrit rashi names because "a North Indian chart labelled Scorpio reads as a translation of someone else's diagram," and the alerts vocabulary is Sade Sati, Mangal dosha and Kaal Sarp. The Western mode is real but plainer, and A.5 says why.

A.3What shipped, release by release

The changelog records three tagged versions, all within three days of each other in September 2026. That compresses two months of work, so the table also names the earlier milestones the git history shows.

WhenWhatWhere it lives now
23 to 24 July 2026Design note, chart engine, prompt builder, streaming chat, admin, landing page. Merged as the MVP on 24 July.lib/astrology/, app/api/chat/, app/admin/
25 August 2026iOS design note. Bearer auth on the route handlers, POST /api/profile, DELETE /api/account, the Universal Links file.lib/supabase/route.ts, public/.well-known/
26 August 2026Dosha alerts, twice-daily readings, prompt caching and effort settings. iOS chat, intake, Apple sign-in, email codes, readings inbox.lib/alerts/, mobile/Sanchara/
0.1.0, 16 September 2026First tagged release. The rename to Astrya. Life timeline, kundli on the phone, Siri, widgets, on-device answers.lib/timeline/, mobile/SancharaWidgets/
0.2.0, 16 September 2026A meaning for every period, chat that sees the timeline, better moment extraction, the current-period widget, the push environment fix.lib/timeline/explain.ts, PeriodWidget.swift
0.3.0, 18 September 2026Derived chart facts, whole-sign Vedic houses, gochara, deeper numerology, more answered on the phone, and the fixes those changes forced.lib/astrology/derived.ts, transits.ts, numerology.ts

0.1.0: the whole product, once

The first tag is large because it is the first tag. The changelog's Added list is the feature list of the README: the chart engine for both traditions, streaming readings with an optional deep reading on Opus, the kundli PDF, the life timeline with moments pinned by hand or proposed from chat history "and confirmed before anything is saved," morning and night readings on the user's local clock with a push only when a dosha or hard transit starts or ends, the native app, Siri and widgets, the admin area and the landing page. Two entries sit under Changed. One is the rename, covered below. The other is a correctness fix in the dasha engine: antardashas are laid out from each mahadasha's "virtual start" rather than from the birth moment, which is what the virtualStart field in lib/astrology/dasha.ts records, so that sub-periods line up with the real period.

0.2.0: the timeline learns to speak

Tagged the same day. The timeline had drawn the dasha periods but named them only by planet. This release adds a one-line theme and a short meaning per period, written by Claude from the chart and the moments pinned inside it, stored in period_readings with a hash of those moments so a changed period is rewritten and an unchanged one is not. The chat gains the same timeline as a block in its cached prompt, so "why was 2021 so hard" is answered against the user's 2021. The scan now resolves relative dates and ages from the message date and birth date, quotes the words each moment came from, and keeps undated events for the user to date. A Home Screen and Lock Screen widget shows the current period. The one fix matters operationally: the app had reported its push environment from the build configuration rather than the signing entitlement, so a Release build from Xcode registered a sandbox token as production and never received a push.

0.3.0: readings cite the chart, not the sign

This is the release that closes the gap named in A.1. Every Vedic reading now carries the lord of each house and where that lord sits, which planets aspect what, each planet's dignity, which are combust, and where the running dasha lord is placed. Transits are read as gochara, by the house they occupy from the ascendant and from the natal Moon, with the affliction those positions raise. Numerology sees the personal year and month, the Lo Shu grid, and the relationship between the core numbers. The phone answers questions about rulers, aspects and strength on the device.

The Changed entry is the one users could notice: Vedic houses became whole-sign, because the chart, the PDF and the on-device table had labelled houses whole-sign from the ascendant while filling them with Placidus numbers. "Existing charts upgrade themselves the first time they are read; nothing to do." The Fixed list is the consequence of doing that carefully. Western readings had been handed Vedic technique on a tropical chart and now get "the plain sky again." And a dosha fixed at birth could have announced itself as news when the house system shifted the detectors; "Natal conditions never alert now; they reconcile silently." Both of those are decisions in their own right, and Appendix B records them.

The name

The product was called Astra from the first commit on 23 July 2026 until the commit of 16 September 2026 that renamed the iOS target, the copy and the prompts. The changelog for 0.1.0 states the rule: "The bundle identifier and production hostname are unchanged." So the repository is still astra, the bundle id is com.shivvyas.astra, the App Group is group.com.shivvyas.astra, the production host is astra.shivvyas.com, and the first migration is headed "Astra schema + RLS." The user-facing name is Astrya everywhere: the system prompt opens "You are Astrya," the widget bundle is SancharaWidgets, and the custom URL scheme a widget tap uses is sanchara://. Why the identifiers stayed is a decision in Appendix B; in short, Apple's push, sign-in and associated-domain configuration is wired to them.

A.4PR/FAQ

Press release

Astrya computes your real birth chart and reads it to you. Give it the date, time and place you were born and the Swiss Ephemeris places every planet, house, nakshatra and period exactly. Then ask it anything. The reading names the placement it is reading from, in the Vedic or Western tradition you choose, and it will not tell you something that is true of everyone born under your sign. Twice a day it writes a reading for your morning and your night, and it only sends a notification when something in your sky actually changes. On iPhone the chart lives on the phone, so Siri, the widget and simple questions answer with no network and no charge. Your kundli is a document you can print.

Does the model compute my chart?

No. computeChart in lib/astrology/chart.ts does, using swisseph-wasm inside the route handler, and the result is stored with your profile. The model is handed the result as text and told it is "the only facts you have." If you ask about a placement that is not in the chart, the prompt instructs it to say what the chart does show instead.

Why is my Vedic chart different from my Western one?

Two settings, both in computeChart. Vedic is computed sidereal with the Lahiri ayanamsa, which currently sets positions roughly 24 degrees behind tropical, so a Sun in early Capricorn tropically is in Sagittarius sidereally; the test in chart.test.ts asserts exactly that. Vedic houses are whole-sign from the ascendant; Western houses are Placidus.

Why did my house numbers change in September?

Before 0.3.0 the Vedic chart labelled its houses whole-sign but numbered the planets by Placidus cusps, so a planet could be drawn in a house whose sign it was not in. Since 0.3.0 both agree. A stored chart from before the change carries schemaVersion below 2 and is recomputed the first time it is read, by ensureCurrentChart in lib/data/birthProfile.ts.

Will I get a notification every day?

Two readings are written every day, morning and night on your own clock, and they arrive as pushes when push is configured. A dosha alert is different: it is sent only when a condition starts or ends. The daily job diffs the conditions it computes today against the open rows in transit_conditions, and a condition that merely continues is never re-notified, "which is what keeps a standing Sade Sati from becoming a daily nag." A condition fixed at birth is never announced as news at all.

What does a reading cost to run?

Measured on a real chart and question, and recorded in docs/MODEL_COSTS.md: a mid-conversation turn on Sonnet 5 went from roughly $0.023 to $0.008 after the system prompt was split so its stable half is a cache read, and after routine readings were set to low effort, which cut output from 1,452 to 719 tokens for the same placements cited. A deep reading keeps the default effort on Opus. On an iPhone with Apple Intelligence, a lookup question never reaches the API at all.

Why is a Western reading plainer than a Vedic one?

Because the techniques that make a Vedic reading specific are Vedic. Lordship, graha drishti, moolatrikona, combustion orbs and the natal doshas apply to a sidereal whole-sign chart, and applying them to a tropical Placidus chart would cite Indian technique as though it were Western. So factsFor returns nothing for a Western chart and the prompt renders the plain positions; today's sky is given as where each body is and which natal body it sits on.

Who can read my conversations?

You, through Row Level Security policies that scope every table to auth.uid(), and an admin who signs in at /admin/login and appears in the admins table, which has no insert policy, so signing up can never grant it. The admin area reads through the service-role client on the server and is read-only for transcripts.

A.5What is deliberately not built

The four design notes each carry a non-goals list, and most of those lines are still true of the code. A few items from the iOS note were planned and never landed. Listing both kinds keeps a new contributor from either building something on purpose left out or assuming something exists.
